On Equivalent Characterizations of NP in Abstract Models of Computation
Jeremy C. Kirn, Lucas Meijer, Tillmann Miltzow, Hans L. Bodlaender

Abstract
We investigate machine models similar to Turing machines that are augmented by the operations of a first-order structure ℛ \mathcal{R} , and we show that under weak conditions on ℛ \mathcal{R} , the complexity class NP ​ ( ℛ ) \textup{NP}(\mathcal{R}) may be characterized in three equivalent ways: (1) by polynomial-time verification algorithms implemented on ℛ \mathcal{R} -machines, (2) by the NP ​ ( ℛ ) \textup{NP}(\mathcal{R}) -complete problem SAT ​ ( ℛ ) \textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R}) , and (3) by existential second-order metafinite logic over ℛ \mathcal{R} via descriptive complexity. By characterizing NP ​ ( ℛ ) \textup{NP}(\mathcal{R}) in these three ways, we extend previous work and embed it in one coherent framework. Some conditions on ℛ \mathcal{R} must be assumed in order to achieve the above trinity because there are infinite-vocabulary structures for which NP ​ ( ℛ ) \textup{NP}(\mathcal{R}) does not have a complete problem. Surprisingly, even in these cases, we show that NP ​ ( ℛ ) \textup{NP}(\mathcal{R}) does have a characterization in terms of existential second-order metafinite logic, suggesting that descriptive complexity theory is well suited to working with infinite-vocabulary structures, such as real vector spaces. In addition, we derive similar results for ∃ ℛ \exists\mathcal{R} , the constant-free Boolean part of NP ​ ( ℛ ) \textup{NP}(\mathcal{R}) , by showing that ∃ ℛ \exists\mathcal{R} may be characterized in three analogous ways. We then extend our results to the entire polynomial hierarchy over ℛ \mathcal{R} and to its constant-free Boolean counterpart, the Boolean hierarchy over ℛ \mathcal{R} . Finally, we give a characterization of the polynomial and Boolean hierarchies over ℛ \mathcal{R} in terms of oracle ℛ \mathcal{R} -machines.

1
Introduction

Algorithms are often presented in terms of primitive operations, and the complexity of these algorithms is often measured by counting how many times the primitive operations are applied. Ignoring the implementation details of these primitive operations is an abstraction that simplifies and enhances algorithm analysis. This abstraction is common in scientific computing, wherein algorithms are presented in terms of operations on the real numbers such as arithmetic and trigonometric functions, the exponential function, and others
[
2
]
. It is also common in algebraic dynamic programming, wherein algorithms are presented in terms of operations that are interpreted in different semirings to solve different problems
[
20
]
. Already at this level of abstraction, it is infeasible to solve some algorithmic problems because they are complete for an abstract version of
NP
that depends on these operations. We describe an example of this phenomenon in a way that motivates our work, and we then provide an overview of our contribution.

1.1
Motivation

The complexity class
NP
may be the most important complexity class in theoretical computer science. It can be characterized in at least three different ways, resulting in a trinity:

1.

The class
NP
consists of decision problems
L\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*}
for which there exists a polynomial-time verification algorithm, using witness strings
w\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, implemented by a Turing machine. This is usually taken as the definition of
NP

[
1
]
, although one may define
NP
equivalently in terms of polynomial-time nondeterministic Turing machines. We work with the verification-witness definition of
NP
because it generalizes in a more straightforward manner to our setting of interest.

2.

The class
NP
consists of decision problems
L\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*}
that can be reduced by a polynomial-time Turing machine to
\textup{SAT}\in\textup{NP}
, where
SAT
is the problem of deciding if a Boolean formula is satisfiable.
The Cook-Levin theorem establishes this characterization of
NP
, which is expressed by saying that
SAT
is
NP
-complete
[
9
]
[
34
]
.

3.

The class
NP
consists of decision problems
L\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*}
for which there is a sentence
\Phi
of existential second-order logic
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}
describing
L.
Fagin’s theorem establishes this characterization of
NP
, which is expressed by saying that
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}
captures
NP

[
15
]
.

In part motivated by the success of the theory of
NP
-completeness, Blum, Shub, and Smale initiated the study of computational complexity over the real numbers in
[
5
]
. They did this by investigating machines over the real numbers that have the ability to write any real number into memory, to evaluate infinite-precision subtraction, addition, and multiplication of real numbers, as well as to test the order relation between two real numbers and branch depending on the answer. Performing each of these operations takes one time step. In our terminology, this is a machine over the structure
\overline{\mathbb{R}}=(\mathbb{R},(r:r\in\mathbb{R}),-,+,\cdot,\leq)
, which we obtain from the ordered ring of real numbers
\mathbb{R}=(\mathbb{R},0,1,-,+,\cdot,\leq)
by expanding it with a constant for each
r\in\mathbb{R}
.

Using machines over
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
, one may recreate the above trinity for
\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
. By definition,
\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
consists of
real
decision problems
L\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{*}
for which there exists a polynomial-time verification algorithm, using
real
witness strings
w\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
, implemented by an
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
-machine. One may then show that
\textup{SAT}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
is
\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
-complete under polynomial-time
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
-machine reductions, where
\textup{SAT}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
is the problem of deciding if a quantifier-free formula using the operations of
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
is satisfiable in
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
. This Cook-Levin theorem over
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
is implied by results in
[
3
]
. One may also show that
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
captures
\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
, where
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
is existential second-order metafinite logic over
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
. This Fagin’s theorem over
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
is shown in
[
22
]
.

More recently, Schaefer and Štefankovič
observed in
[
45
]
that many decision problems in computational geometry and beyond may be reduced to the problem
\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathbb{R})=\text{ETR}
of deciding if a quantifier-free formula using the operations of the ordered ring of reals
\mathbb{R}=(\mathbb{R},0,1,-,+,\cdot,\leq)
is satisfiable in
\mathbb{R}
. Notably, such a formula may only contain the constants 0 and 1, and its satisfiability may be decided by a Turing machine
[
55
]
. The above observation prompted the introduction of the complexity class
\exists\mathbb{R}
consisting of those problems reducible to
\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathbb{R})
with a polynomial-time Turing machine. Already more than 200 problems have been identified as
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete, attesting to the importance of
\exists\mathbb{R}

[
43
]
.

Using machines over
\mathbb{R}
, which are just
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
-machines restricted to write only the constants 0 and 1 into memory, we may recreate the above trinity for
\exists\mathbb{R}
. The class
\exists\mathbb{R}
consists of
Boolean
decision problems
L\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*}
for which there exists a polynomial-time verification algorithm, using
real
witness strings
w\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
, implemented by an
\mathbb{R}
-machine. This machine characterization of
\exists\mathbb{R}
is shown in
[
7
,
14
]
, and it is expressed by saying that
\exists\mathbb{R}
is the constant-free Boolean part of
\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
. By definition,
\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathbb{R})
is
\exists\mathbb{R}
-complete under polynomial-time Turing machine reductions. Finally, we will show in this work that
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathbb{R})
captures
\exists\mathbb{R}
, where
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathbb{R})
is the restriction of
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
to use only the constants 0 and 1. A similar result was recently (and independently of our work) established in
[
37
]
by restricting
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
to use only rational constants.

Since it is possible to recreate the above trinity for both
\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
and
\exists\mathbb{R}
, these complexity classes faithfully resemble
NP
. As we noted above, algorithms are often analyzed over different domains according to different sets of primitive operations that depend on the application area. Given the importance of
NP
, a fundamental question in the complexity of algorithms is the following. In which domains and with which sets of primitive operations can we recreate the above trinity?

We formalize this question with first-order structures
\mathcal{R}
,
which consist of a set
R
together with constants
c\in R
, functions
f\colon R^{k}\to R
, and relations
P\subseteq R^{k}
.
Using register machines over
\mathcal{R}
, we refine our fundamental question as follows. Under what conditions on
\mathcal{R}
is it possible to recreate the above trinity for both
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
and
\exists\mathcal{R}
by characterizing them in terms of machine models, complete problems, and descriptive complexity?

1.2
Overview of Our Contribution

In this work, we generalize the above trinity by defining
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
and
\exists\mathcal{R}
in terms of register machines over
\mathcal{R}
and characterizing
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
and
\exists\mathcal{R}
in terms of complete problems and descriptive complexity. Furthermore, we generalize these characterizations to each level of the polynomial hierarchy over
\mathcal{R}
and the Boolean hierarchy over
\mathcal{R}
, as depicted in
Figure

1
, and we give additional characterizations of these hierarchies using oracles.

\Delta_{0}\mathcal{R}=\Sigma_{0}^{\mathcal{R}}=\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})=\Pi_{0}^{\mathcal{R}}=\Delta_{1}\mathcal{R}
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})=\Sigma_{1}\mathcal{R}

\Pi_{1}\mathcal{R}=\textup{coNP}(\mathcal{R})
\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{1}\mathcal{R}}=\Delta_{2}\mathcal{R}
\Sigma_{2}\mathcal{R}
\Pi_{2}\mathcal{R}
\Delta_{3}\mathcal{R}
\Sigma_{3}\mathcal{R}
\Pi_{3}\mathcal{R}
⋮
\textup{PH}(\mathcal{R})=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
\delta_{0}\mathcal{R}=\exists_{0}\mathcal{R}=\textup{BP}^{0}(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R}))=\forall_{0}\mathcal{R}=\delta_{1}\mathcal{R}
\textup{BP}^{0}(\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R}))=\exists_{1}\mathcal{R}

\forall_{1}\mathcal{R}=\textup{BP}^{0}(\textup{coNP}(\mathcal{R}))
\textup{BP}^{0}(\Delta_{2}\mathcal{R})=\delta_{2}\mathcal{R}
\exists_{2}\mathcal{R}
\forall_{2}\mathcal{R}
\delta_{3}\mathcal{R}
\exists_{3}\mathcal{R}
\forall_{3}\mathcal{R}
⋮
\textup{BH}(\mathcal{R})=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}

Figure 1:
The polynomial hierarchy and the Boolean hierarchy over a structure
\mathcal{R}
.

Each of our results requires us to assume that
\mathcal{R}
is a
bipointed
structure, by which we mean that
\mathcal{R}
has at least two distinct constants
0\neq 1
. Our completeness results require us to assume further that
\mathcal{R}
is of
finite type
, by which we mean that
\mathcal{R}
has only finitely many functions and relations, and to assume that
\mathcal{R}

has all constants
, by which we mean that
\mathcal{R}
has a unique constant
r
for each element in its universe
R
.

We begin with complete problems.
It is already known from
[
21
]
that if
\mathcal{R}
is a bipointed structure of finite type with all constants, then
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-complete under polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine reductions, a fact we recall as
Theorem

14
. From this, we derive
Corollary

15
in which we show that if
\mathcal{R}
is a bipointed structure of finite type, then
\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\exists\mathcal{R}
-complete under polynomial-time Turing machine reductions.

We then generalize both of these theorems to each level of the polynomial and Boolean hierarchies over
\mathcal{R}
in the following way.
In
Theorem

16
we show that if
\mathcal{R}
is a bipointed structure of finite type with all constants, then
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
-complete under polynomial time
\mathcal{R}
-machine reductions. From this, we derive
Corollary

17
in which we show that if
\mathcal{R}
is a bipointed structure of finite type, then
\exists_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}
-complete under polynomial-time Turing machine reductions.

We next turn to descriptive complexity theory.
In
Theorem

19
, we show that if
\mathcal{R}
is a bipointed structure, then
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
on metafinite structures over
\mathcal{R}
.
Since there is a structure
\mathcal{Z}
of infinite type for which there is no
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{Z})
-complete problem,
Theorem

19
is strictly more general than any possible generalization of
Theorem

14
. This suggests that descriptive complexity theory is well suited for dealing with structures of infinite type, such as real vector spaces.

Additionally, from
Theorem

19
we derive
Corollary

20
in which we show that
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\exists\mathcal{R}
on Boolean metafinite structures over
\mathcal{R}
.
Lastly, we generalize these results to each level of the polynomial and Boolean hierarchies over
\mathcal{R}
by showing in
Theorem

21
that
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
on metafinite structures over
\mathcal{R}
, and by showing in
Corollary

22
that
\exists_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}
on Boolean metafinite structures over
\mathcal{R}
.

We finally turn to oracle characterizations of these hierarchies. In
Theorem

23
, we show that if
\mathcal{R}
is a bipointed structure, then
\Sigma_{k+1}\mathcal{R}=\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}
.
Additionally, from
Theorem

23
we derive
Corollary

24
in which we show that
\exists_{k+1}\mathcal{R}=\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}
. From this corollary, we can see that it is unlikely that we can replace the non-Boolean oracle class
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
with the Boolean oracle class
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}
. In particular, it is unlikely that
\exists\mathbb{R}^{\exists\mathbb{R}}=\exists_{2}\mathbb{R}
.

We organize our results as follows: In
Section

2
we present preliminary definitions, in
Section

3
we present our results about complete problems, in
Section

4
we present our results about descriptive complexity, and in
Section

5
we present our results about oracles. In each section, we provide proof sketches of our results, as well as a discussion of related work. Full proofs appear in our appendix, as well as an extended preliminaries section to make our results more accessible.

2
Preliminaries

We present a selection of the preliminaries that are most important for understanding our results. The reader who desires more formal detail or more intuition may find both of these in the extended preliminaries of
Appendix

A
.

2.1
Structures

First-order structures are mathematical objects that are designed to interpret the formulas of first-order logic. These formulas are defined relative to a set
\mathsf{Con}
of constant symbols
c
, a set
\mathsf{Fun}
of function symbols
f
with a given arity, and a set
\mathsf{Rel}
of relation symbols
P
with a given arity. This information specifies a vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
from which first-order formulas are constructed in the usual syntax of first-order logic with equality. We note that the presence of equality in formulas is essential to our results. A structure provides an interpretation of each symbol from
\mathsf{Voc}
in some universe.

Definition 1

(Structures)
.

A (first-order) structure
\mathcal{R}
for a vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}=(\mathsf{Con},\mathsf{Fun},\mathsf{Rel})
provides an interpretation of the symbols in
\mathsf{Voc}
by specifying the following:

1.

a nonempty set
R
called the universe, domain, or underlying set of
\mathcal{R}
,

2.

a constant
c^{\mathcal{R}}\in R
for each constant symbol
c\in\mathsf{Con}
,

3.

a function
f^{\mathcal{R}}\colon R^{k}\to R
for each function symbol
f\in\mathsf{Fun}
with arity
k
,

4.

a relation
P^{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq R^{k}
for each relation symbol
P\in\mathsf{Rel}
, with arity
k
.

A structure is a
\mathsf{Voc}
-structure for some vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
. In practice, we will often conflate a symbol of
\mathsf{Voc}
with its interpretation in a
\mathsf{Voc}
-structure
\mathcal{R}
by dropping the superscript
\mathcal{R}
. Furthermore, we will often make the following assumptions about structures:

•

a structure
\mathcal{R}
is bipointed if it has at least two distinct constants
0\neq 1
,

•

a structure
\mathcal{R}
is of finite type if it has finitely many functions and relations,

•

a structure
\mathcal{R}
has all constants if it has a unique constant for each element
r\in R
.

Example 2

(Ordered Rings)
.

We may regard each ordered ring as a structure
\mathcal{R}=(R,0,1,-,+,\cdot,\leq)
. For example, the ordered ring of real numbers
\mathbb{R}=(\mathbb{R},0,1,-,+,\cdot)
is a bipointed structure of finite type. Expanding this structure with a constant for each
r\in\mathbb{R}
, we obtain the structure
\overline{\mathbb{R}}=(\mathbb{R},(r:r\in\mathbb{R}),-,+,\cdot,\leq)
which is a bipointed structure of finite type with all constants.

Example 3

(Real Vector Spaces)
.

By splitting scalar multiplication
\cdot\colon\mathbb{R}\times V\to V
into infinitely many functions
\mu_{r}\colon V\to V

that send
v
to
\mu_{r}(v)=r\cdot v
,
we may regard each real vector space as a structure
\mathcal{V}=(V,0,-,+,(\mu_{r}:r\in\mathbb{R}))
. We expand this structure with an additional constant
1
to make it bipointed. For example,

\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}^{n}=(\mathbb{R}^{n},0,1,-,+,(\mu_{r}:r\in\mathbb{R}))
is a structure where
\mu_{r}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=(r\cdot x_{1},\dots,r\cdot x_{n})
. In particular, the linear reals
\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}=(\mathbb{R},0,1,-,+,(\mu_{r}:r\in\mathbb{R}))
are a structure with
\mu_{r}(x)=r\cdot x
. Note that
\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}
is a bipointed structure that is not of finite type, nor does it have all constants.

2.2
Register Machines Over a Structure

The symbols of a vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
constitute the basic ingredients of a programming language in which we may specify computations over a
\mathsf{Voc}
-structure
\mathcal{R}
. These computations are carried out by a register machine over
\mathcal{R}
, also called an
\mathcal{R}
-machine, which we may depict as in
Figure

2
.

Figure 2:
Visualization of an
\mathcal{R}
-machine adapted from
[
19
]
.

Such machines have an infinite sequence
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}2\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots
of
\mathcal{R}
-registers, each of which may store a single element
r
from the universe
R
of
\mathcal{R}
, and a finite sequence
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}k\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
of index registers, each of which may store a single natural number
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The primary role of index registers
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
is to keep track of indexing and counting during computations, as well as to point to
\mathcal{R}
-registers
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
.

An
\mathcal{R}
-machine manipulates the contents of its registers based on its
\mathcal{R}
-program
\mathcal{P}
, which is a finite enumerated sequence of
\mathcal{R}
-instructions.
Importantly, there are
\mathcal{R}
-instructions directing
\mathcal{R}
-machines to evaluate each operation of
\mathcal{R}
:

1.

the instruction
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=c
directs a machine to store the constant
c
in register
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
,

2.

the instruction
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=f(Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{k}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}})
directs a machine to evaluate the function
f(Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{k}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}})
and store the result in register
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
,

3.

the instruction
\mathsf{if}\ P(Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{k}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}})\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{1}\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{2}
directs a machine to test the relation
P(Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{k}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}})
and go to instruction
\ell_{1}
or
\ell_{2}
depending on the result.

In addition to the above,
\mathcal{R}
-instructions may also direct an
\mathcal{R}
-machine to branch based on a test of equality between
\mathcal{R}
-registers or index registers, to copy the contents of one
\mathcal{R}
-register to another, and to increment or decrement the contents of an index register. Finally, when we would like to equip an
\mathcal{R}
-machine with an oracle
Q\subseteq R^{*}
, we allow
\mathcal{R}
-instructions directing
\mathcal{R}
-machines to query this oracle. A full list of
\mathcal{R}
-instructions is presented in
Table

3
.

In the formal definition of
\mathcal{R}
-machines below, we include the structure
\mathcal{R}
as a component of
\mathcal{R}
-machines. We do this so that the
\mathcal{R}
-machine knows how to interpret the symbols in its program, and in this sense we may view the structure
\mathcal{R}
as the CPU from
Figure

2
, while the other components of
\mathcal{R}
-machines correspond in the expected way to this picture.

Definition 4

(
\mathcal{R}
-Machines)
.

A machine over
\mathcal{R}
, also called an
\mathcal{R}
-machine, is a tuple
M=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{R},\mathbb{N}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}},R^{\mathbb{N}})
specifying the following information:

1.

an
\mathcal{R}
-program
\mathcal{P}
,

2.

the structure
\mathcal{R}
,

3.

a space of index states
\mathbb{N}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}}
,

4.

a space of memory states
R^{\mathbb{N}}
.

We say that the constants of
\mathcal{R}
, other than
0
and
1
, appearing in an instruction of
\mathcal{P}
are the machine constants of
M
, and that
M
is constant free if it has no machine constants. The input and output space of
M
are
R^{*}=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}R^{n}.

Our definition of
\mathcal{R}
-machines is taken with minimal modification from
[
27
,
19
]
, and it is similar to the original definition of BSS machines given in
[
5
]
, which closely resemble register machines, a precursor to random access machines introduced in
[
51
]
. Because our
\mathcal{R}
-instructions allow only sequential access to memory by incrementing or decrementing an index register, we use the name register machine over
\mathcal{R}
.

A significant difference between BSS machines over a ring
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{R}
-machines is that BSS machines over
\mathcal{R}
may write an arbitrary element
r\in R
into memory, while
\mathcal{R}
-machines may write an element
r
into memory only if
r
is an input or a constant of the structure
\mathcal{R}
. We choose to limit
\mathcal{R}
-machines in this way so that the power of
\mathcal{R}
-machines more closely reflects the operations present in the structure
\mathcal{R}
, including the constant operations.

An element
r\in R
usually plays the role of an
input
to an
\mathcal{R}
-machine. When we turn
r
into a
constant
, we assign it a very different role. As a constant,
r
may encode an infinite amount of information into
\mathcal{R}
-programs. For example, consider the ordered ring of reals
\mathbb{R}
and suppose
r_{\textup{halt}}\in\mathbb{R}
encodes the characteristic function of the diagonal halting problem. Upon input
n\in\mathbb{N}
encoded in binary, an
\mathbb{R}
-machine with
r_{\textup{halt}}
as a constant can extract the
n
th bit of
r_{\textup{halt}}
, as described in
[
32
]
, to decide if the
n
th Turing machine halts on input
n
, thereby deciding the diagonal halting problem. This means that
\mathbb{R}
-machines with
r_{\textup{halt}}
as a machine constant are strictly more powerful than Turing machines, even when we restrict these
\mathbb{R}
-machines to Boolean inputs, whereas constant-free Boolean
\mathbb{R}
-machines may be simulated by Turing machines.

Another subtlety concerning constants arises when one regards constants as functions of arity zero. This view would clash with the definition of structures of finite type, which may have infinitely many constants but only finitely many functions. When we assume that
\mathcal{R}
is of finite type, we do so in order to guarantee that
\mathcal{R}
has an
effective encoding
, which is an encoding the symbols in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
as strings in
R^{*}
in such a way that an
\mathcal{R}
-machine can take the code of a symbol and evaluate the corresponding operation. There is an effective encoding for
\mathcal{R}
if and only if there is a universal
\mathcal{R}
-machine
[
27
]
.

Structures with infinitely many constants may still have an effective encoding because a constant may always stand as a code for itself, whereas there are structures with infinitely many unary functions, such as
\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}
, for which there is no effective encoding. To get an intuition for this phenomenon, we consider why there can be no universal
\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}
-machine. Any
\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}
-machine can have only finitely many scalar functions
\mu_{r}
in its program, and it is not possible to express each function in
\{\mu_{r}:r\in\mathbb{R}\}
in terms of a finite subset of these functions.

There is a long history capturing computations over a first-order structure with machines and methods similar to ours
[
16
]
. We refer the reader to the introduction of
[
27
]
for further references into this history. There are multiple incomparable ways to generalize computation to the real numbers
[
41
]
. Machines over
\mathbb{R}
given an algebraic generalization, while type two computability and computable analysis give a topological generalization
[
40
]
. The algebraic approach provides fertile ground for complexity theory. For an introductory survey of early results in the complexity of computation over
\mathbb{R}
and other structures, see
[
36
]
.

2.3
Complexity Classes

We view each subset
L\subseteq R^{*}
as a decision problem over a structure
\mathcal{R}
. Under a suitable encoding, the main decision problems we will work with consist of satisfied sentences of a specified grammatical form. A sentence
\Phi
is in prenex normal form when it is a string of quantifiers followed by a quantifier free formula
\varphi
as in
Q_{1}x_{1}\dots Q_{n}\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}),
where
Q_{i}\in\{\exists,\forall\}
. Supposing
\Phi
has
k-1
quantifier alternations, we say that
\Phi
is a
\Sigma_{k}
sentence when
Q_{1}=\exists
, and we say that
\Phi
is a
\Pi_{k}
sentence when
Q_{1}=\forall
.

Definition 5

(Decision Problems of Satisfied Sentences)
.

The theory of
\mathcal{R}
is the set
\mathsf{Th}(\mathcal{R})
of sentences
\Phi
in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
that are satisfied in
\mathcal{R}
, and the Boolean theory of
\mathcal{R}
is the subset
\mathsf{BTh}(\mathcal{R})\subseteq\mathsf{Th}(\mathcal{R})
of sentences
\Phi
such that all constants in
\Phi
are 0 or 1. We identify the following fragments of these theories that we refer to as the
k
th existential or universal fragment of the theory or Boolean theory of
\mathcal{R}
, respectively:

1.

\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})=\{\Phi:\Phi\text{ is a $\Sigma_{k}$ sentence and }\mathcal{R}\vDash\Phi\}
,

2.

\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})=\{\Phi:\Phi\text{ is a $\Pi_{k}$ sentence and }\mathcal{R}\vDash\Phi\}
,

3.

\exists_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})=\{\Phi\in\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R}):\text{all constants in $\Phi$ are 0 or 1}\}
,

4.

\forall_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})=\{\Phi\in\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R}):\text{all constants in $\Phi$ are 0 or 1}\}
.

A complexity class
\mathcal{C}\subseteq 2^{R^{*}}
is any set of decision problems
L\subseteq R^{*}
. The polynomial hierarchy
\textup{PH}(\mathcal{R})
of complexity classes over a structure
\mathcal{R}
generalize the classical polynomial hierarchy, and these classes are our main focus.

Definition 6

(Polynomial Hierarchy Over
\mathcal{R}
)
.

We define two sequences of complexity classes
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
and
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
as follows. A decision problem
L\subseteq R^{*}
is in
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
if and only if there is a polynomial
q
and a polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
v\in R^{n}

v\in L\iff(Q_{1}w_{1}\in R^{q(n)})\dots(Q_{k}w_{k}\in R^{q(n)})M(v,w_{1},\dots,w_{k})=1,

where the quantifiers
Q_{i}\in\{\exists,\forall\}
alternate, starting with
Q_{1}=\exists
. The class
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
is defined by the same condition except the first quantifier is
Q_{1}=\forall
. Define the polynomial hierarchy over
\mathcal{R}
as the class
\textup{PH}(\mathcal{R})=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}.

We define
\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})=\Sigma_{0}\mathcal{R}
, and we note that this is the class of decision problems that can be decided by a polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine. Furthermore, we define
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})=\Sigma_{1}\mathcal{R}
and
\textup{coNP}(\mathcal{R})=\Pi_{1}\mathcal{R}
, and we note that these classes generalize classical
NP
and
coNP
. We may relate a complexity class
\mathcal{C}
over a structure to classical complexity classes by taking the constant-free Boolean part of
\mathcal{C}.

Definition 7

(Constant-Free Boolean Part)
.

The Boolean part of a decision problem
L
is the set
\textup{BP}(L)=L\cap\{0,1\}^{*}
. The Boolean part of a complexity class
\mathcal{C}
is the set
\textup{BP}(\mathcal{C})=\{\textup{BP}(L):L\in\mathcal{C}\}.
If
\mathcal{C}
is defined in terms of
\mathcal{R}
-machines, then
\mathcal{C}^{0}
is the class obtained by restricting to
\mathcal{R}
-machines with no machine constants. We write
\textup{BP}^{0}(\mathcal{C})
for
\textup{BP}(\mathcal{C}^{0})
, and we say that
\textup{BP}^{0}(\mathcal{C})
is the constant-free Boolean part of
\mathcal{C}
.

By taking the constant-free Boolean part of each level of
\textup{PH}(\mathcal{R})
, we obtain the Boolean hierarchy over a structure
\mathcal{R}
, which is a hierarchy of Boolean complexity classes.

Definition 8

(Boolean Hierarchy Over
\mathcal{R}
)
.

We define the complexity classes
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}
and
\forall_{k}\mathcal{R}
as the constant-free Boolean parts of
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
and
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
, respectively, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
. That is,
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}=\textup{BP}^{0}(\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R})\textup{ and }\forall_{k}\mathcal{R}=\textup{BP}^{0}(\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}).
The Boolean hierarchy over
\mathcal{R}
is the complexity class
\textup{BH}(\mathcal{R})=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}
.

As we will see, it is quite fruitful to analyze the polynomial and Boolean hierarchies over
\mathcal{R}
in terms of
\mathcal{R}
-machines with oracles.

Definition 9

(Oracle Complexity Classes)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a complexity class over
\mathcal{R}
. For each of level of the polynomial and Boolean hierarchies over
\mathcal{R}
, the complexity classes
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{D}}
,
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{D}}
,
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{D}}
, and
\forall_{k}\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{D}}
are obtained by modifying the definition of the corresponding class to allow
\mathcal{R}
-machines with oracles from
\mathcal{D}
.

2.4
Metafinite Structures

Metafinite structures may be thought of as finite structures
\mathcal{A}
that are given finite access to a potentially infinite structure
\mathcal{R}
by a finite set
\mathbf{F}
of weight functions
W\colon A^{k}\to R
.
They were first used to generalize Fagin’s theorem from
NP
to
\textup{NP}(\mathbb{R})
in
[
22
]
, and their general theory was developed in
[
23
]
.
Just as
\mathcal{R}
-machines separate the finite and discrete operations of index registers from the potentially infinite and continuous operations of
\mathcal{R}
-registers, metafinite structures separate the finite and discrete aspect of a structure from the potentially infinite and continuous aspects of a structure.

In order to talk about metafinite structures, we need a notion of metafinite vocabularies, which
will consist of three separate vocabularies
\mathsf{MVoc}=(\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{MVoc}),\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{MVoc}),\mathsf{W}(\mathsf{MVoc}))
. The
primary vocabulary

\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{MVoc})
is a finite vocabulary, the
secondary vocabulary

\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{MVoc})
is a potentially infinite vocabulary, and the
weight vocabulary

\mathsf{W}(\mathsf{MVoc})
is a finite set of function symbols
F
, each with a given arity. We say that
\mathsf{MVoc}
is a
metafinite vocabulary over
\mathcal{R}
whenever the secondary vocabulary of
\mathsf{MVoc}
is the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
.

Definition 10
.

A metafinite structure for a metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
is a triple
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
where

1.

the primary part of
\mathcal{D}
is a finite structure
\mathcal{A}
for vocabulary
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{MVoc})
,

2.

the secondary part of
\mathcal{D}
is a potentially infinite structure
\mathcal{R}
for vocabulary
\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{MVoc})
,

3.

the set of weight functions of
\mathcal{D}
is a finite set
\mathbf{F}
of functions
F\colon A^{k_{F}}\to R
.

We say that
\mathcal{D}
is a Boolean metafinite structure if the image of each weight function
F\in\mathbf{F}
is contained in
\{0,1\}
, so that
F\colon A^{k_{F}}\to\{0,1\}\subseteq R
.

The
size
of a metafinite structure
\mathcal{D}
, denoted by
|\mathcal{D}|
, is the cardinality of the universe
A
of its primary part
\mathcal{A}
. In practice, we will conflate the function
F\colon A^{k_{F}}\to R
with the symbol for
F
. Whenever the secondary part of
\mathcal{D}
is
\mathcal{R}
, then we say that
\mathcal{D}
is an
\mathcal{R}
-structure
. For a given metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
over
\mathcal{R}
, let
\textup{Struct}(\mathsf{MVoc})
be the class of metafinite structures in this vocabulary.

Example 11
.

A simple class of metafinite structures is obtained by letting
\mathcal{A}=(V,E)
be a finite graph, letting
\mathcal{R}=\mathbb{R}
be the ordered ring of real numbers, and letting
\mathbf{F}
contain a single binary function
F\colon V^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
that assigns
0
to all pairs
(v_{1},v_{2})
that are not related by
E
. The resulting
\mathbb{R}
-structure is a finite graph with edges that have real number weights. Conversely, every graph with real weights can be interpreted as an
\mathbb{R}
-structure.

An important restriction of metafinite logic is that first-order variables, those from
\mathsf{Var}=\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\dots\}
, range only over the primary part
\mathcal{A}
of an
\mathsf{MVoc}
-structure
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
. Second-order variables of positive arity
k
, those from
\mathsf{VAR}_{k}=\{X_{1}^{k},X_{2}^{k},X_{3}^{k},\dots\}
, range over all possible functions
W\colon A^{k}\to R
, even those not in
\mathbf{F}
. First-order metafinite logic allows only quantification over first-order variables, while second-order metafinite logic allows quantification over second-order variables as well. Note that even second-order metafinite logic does not allow quantification over the secondary structure
\mathcal{R}
.

A sentence
\Phi
of second-order metafinite logic is in prenex normal form when it is a string of second-order quanifiers followed by a first-order sentence
\varphi
, as in
QX_{1}\dots QX_{N}\varphi(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})
, where
Q_{i}\in\{\exists,\forall\}
. Supposing
\Phi
has
k-1
quantifier alternations, we say that
\Phi
is a
\Sigma_{k}
sentence when
Q_{1}=\exists
, and we say that
\Phi
is a
\Pi_{k}
sentence when
Q_{1}=\forall
.

Definition 12

(Fragments of Second-Order Metafinite Logic)
.

Let
\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
be the set of all second-order sentences in any metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
over
\mathcal{R}
. We identify the following fragments of metafinite second order logic over
\mathcal{R}
that we refer to as the
k
th existential or universal fragment or Boolean fragment of
\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
, respectively:

1.

\Sigma_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})=\{\Phi\in\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R}):\Phi\text{ is a $\Sigma_{k}$ sentence}\}
,

2.

\Pi_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})=\{\Phi\in\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R}):\Phi\text{ is a $\Pi_{k}$ sentence}\}
,

3.

\exists_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})\,=\{\Phi\in\Sigma_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R}):\text{all constants from $\mathcal{R}$ in $\Phi$ are 0 or 1}\}
,

4.

\forall_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})\,=\{\Phi\in\Pi_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R}):\text{all constants from $\mathcal{R}$ in $\Phi$ are 0 or 1}\}
.

3
Cook-Levin Theorem Over a Structure

The idea behind our hardness results is a generalization of the idea behind the classical Cook-Levin theorem: It is possible to encode the computation of a machine in a logical formula. In the classical setting, the machine is a polynomial-time Turing machine and the formula is a Boolean formula. In our setting, the machine is a polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine, and the formula is a first-order formula in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
.

In more detail, let
M
be an
\mathcal{R}
-machine running in time
n^{m}
and let
v\in R^{n}
be some string. Because of the time bound of
M
, we may represent the computation of
M
on input
v
in a finite computation table, as depicted in
Table

1
. Row
t
of this table specifies the contents of the registers of
M
at time
t
. If the last row of this table specifies that
M
returns 1, then we call this table an accepting computation table.

Since the program of
M
is built from the operations of
\mathcal{R}
, it is plausible that we may use a formula in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
to describe this table. Indeed, we do just that by constructing the formula
\varphi_{v}(\overline{y})\equiv(\textup{{start}}_{v}\wedge\textup{{update}}\wedge\textup{{accept}})(\overline{y}).
The subformula
\textup{{start}}_{v}(\overline{y})
uses constants for each
v_{i}
in
v=(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\in R^{n}
to express the zeroth row of the table. To be able to do this for each
v\in R^{*}
, we need to assume that
\mathcal{R}
has all constants.
The subformula
\textup{{update}}(\overline{y})
expresses that row
t+1
of the table results from modifying row
t
of the table according to the program of
M
, which is possible because both the program of
M
and the running time of
M
are finite.
Finally, the subformula
\textup{{accept}}(\overline{y})
expresses that the last row of the table is an accepting configuration in which
M
returns 1.

By encoding the time
t
, the label
\ell_{t}
, and the index
\nu_{i,t}
as binary strings in
\{0,1\}^{*}\subseteq R^{*}
, we capture the computation table of
M
on input
v
with a finite string
\overline{r}
of elements from the universe of
\mathcal{R}
. The formula
\varphi_{v}(\overline{y})
is constructed so that
\overline{r}
correctly represents an accepting computation table of
M
on input
v
if and only if
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi_{r}(\overline{r})
. Since
M(v)=1
if and only if there is such an
\overline{r}
, we see that
M(v)=1\text{ if and only if }\mathcal{R}\vDash\exists\overline{y}\varphi_{v}(\overline{y}).
By allowing some variables in
\textup{{start}}_{v}(\overline{y})
to remain unconstrained, we make space for witness strings
w
in the input to
M
, which yields the following lemma.

Registers

Time

L\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}

I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}

\dots

I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}k_{\mathcal{P}}-1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}

Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}

\dots

Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}n^{m}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}

0

\ell_{0}

\nu_{0,0}

\dots

\nu_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1,0}

\rho_{0,0}

\dots

\rho_{n^{m},0}

⋮

t

\ell_{t}

\nu_{0,t}

\dots

\nu_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1,t}

\rho_{0,t}

\dots

\rho_{n^{m},t}

⋮

n^{m}

\ell_{n^{m}}

\nu_{0,n^{m}}

\dots

\nu_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1,n^{m}}

\rho_{0,n^{m}}

\dots

\rho_{n^{m},n^{m}}

Table 1:
Computation table of
M
running in time
n^{m}
on input
v\in R^{n}
.

Lemma 13

(Encoding Computations in First-Order Formulas)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a bipointed structure with all constants,
M
be a polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine,
q
be a polynomial, and
v\in R^{*}
be a string. Then for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a first-order formula
\exists\overline{y}\varphi_{v,k}(\overline{x}_{1},\dots,\overline{x}_{k},\overline{y})
in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
, with
\varphi_{v,k}
quantifier free, such that for all strings
w_{1},\dots,w_{k}\in R^{q(|v|)}
,

M(v,w_{1},\dots,w_{k})=1\text{ if and only if }\mathcal{R}\vDash\exists\overline{y}\ \varphi_{v,k}(w_{1},\dots,w_{k},\overline{y}).

Theorem 14

(Cook-Levin Analogue
[
21
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a bipointed structure of finite type with all constants.
Then
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-complete under polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine reductions.

Proof Sketch.

To show that
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is in
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
, it suffices to show that a polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine can decide if
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi(\overline{r})
for any quantifier-free
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
-formula
\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
and any
\overline{r}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{k})\in R^{k}
. We show this by arguing that a polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine can recursively decide if
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi(\overline{r})
based on the syntactic structure of
\varphi
. In order to implement the base case of this algorithm, we need
\mathcal{R}
to have an effective encoding so that an
\mathcal{R}
-machine can take the encoding of a symbol in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
and use it to evaluate the corresponding operation. Our assumption that
\mathcal{R}
is of finite type guarantees the existence of an effective encoding that may, moreover, be decoded in constant time. Hence, this recursive algorithm may be implemented on a polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine.

To see that
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-hard, note that each
L\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
is of the form
L=\{v\in R^{*}:(\exists w\in R^{q(|v|)})\ M(v,w)=1\}
for some polynomial
q
and some polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
.
Lemma

13
implies that
v\in L
if and only if
\exists\overline{x}\exists\overline{y}\varphi_{v,1}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\in\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R}).
Furthermore, the number of atomic subformulas in
\varphi_{v,1}
is polynomial in the length of
v
, so
\varphi_{v,1}
may be constructed from
v
in polynomial-time by an
\mathcal{R}
-machine.
∎

We know the assumption that
\mathcal{R}
is of finite type is not strictly necessary because there are structures
\mathcal{R}
of infinite type for which
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
complete, as shown in
[
38
]
.
We have a strong intuition that the existence of an effective encoding for
\mathcal{R}
is a necessary condition for
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
to be in
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
because if there is an operation of
\mathcal{R}
that
\mathcal{R}
-machines cannot decode, then
\mathcal{R}
-machines should not be able to test the truth of formulas with this operation. Given the fact that
\mathcal{R}
has an effective encoding if and only if there is a universal
\mathcal{R}
-machine, we conjecture that
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is in
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
if and only if there is a universal
\mathcal{R}
-machine that runs with polynomial-overhead.

We also have a strong intuition that the presence of all constants in
\mathcal{R}
is a necessary condition for
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
to be
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-hard. Constants for each
v_{i}
in
v=(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\in R^{n}
are used to hard-code
v
into the formula
\varphi_{v}(\overline{x},\overline{y})
. Since this formula expresses the computation table of
M
on inputs of the form
(v,w)
for some
w
, it seems necessary to specify
v
in this formula, and it is not clear how to do this without constants while retaining the membership of
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
in
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
.

The first completeness results of this form appear in
[
6
]
, where the existence of a complete problem for
\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
is established. In
[
4
]
, it is shown that the semialgebraic feasibility problem is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-complete whenever
\mathcal{R}
is an ordered ring, as is Hilbert’s nullstellensatz problem whenever
\mathcal{R}
is a ring with all constants.
Each of these problems may be seen as modifications or restrictions of
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
.

To the best of our knowledge, it was first noted in
[
7
]
that the
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-completeness of the above problems under
\mathcal{R}
-machine reductions yields
\exists\mathcal{R}
-completeness of the Boolean variant of these problems under Turing machine reductions. We generalize this observation from rings to arbitrary bipointed structures of finite type in the following corollary.

Corollary 15

(Boolean Cook-Levin Analogue)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a bipointed structure of finite type. Then
\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\exists\mathcal{R}
-complete under polynomial-time Turing machine reductions.

Proof Sketch.

All constants in a sentence
\exists\overline{x}\varphi(\overline{x})
of
\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
are either 0 or 1, which implies that
\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is in
\exists\mathcal{R}
. Furthermore, the reduction in
Theorem

14
from
L\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
to
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
restricts to a reduction from
L\in\exists\mathcal{R}
to
\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
. Finally, the polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine implementing this reduction takes only Boolean inputs and need not execute operations of
\mathcal{R}
other than writing 0 and 1 and testing for equality, so this reduction may be implemented on a polynomial-time Turing machine.
∎

When comparing
Corollary

15
with
Theorem

14
, we see that the assumption that
\mathcal{R}
has all constants has been dropped. This is possible because, even though
\exists\mathcal{R}
is defined in terms of
\mathcal{R}
-machines, it is a Boolean complexity class, by which we mean that
L\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*}
for every
L\in\exists\mathcal{R}
. This implies that the constants
0
and
1
are sufficient to encode the input
v\in\{0,1\}^{*}
in the formula
\varphi_{v,1}(\overline{x},\overline{y})
when reducing
L
to
\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
. We do, however, need constants again when we extend
Theorem

14
to each level of the polynomial hierarchy over
\mathcal{R}
in the following proposition.

Theorem 16

(Higher Cook-Levin Analogue)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a bipointed structure of finite type with all constants. Then for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq 1
,
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
-complete and
\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
-complete under polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine reductions.

Proof Sketch.

The argument showing that
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
-complete follows from
Lemma

13
in essentially the same way as the argument showing that
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-complete, with small modifications and an additional step to account for the alternating string of quantifiers in the definition of
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
and
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
.
∎

Previously, most research into the polynomial hierarchy over
\mathcal{R}
focused on the cases when
\mathcal{R}
is either
\mathbb{R}_{\textup{add}}=(\mathbb{R},0,1,-,+)
or
\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}
or a number of related structures
[
11
]
. Many of the results for the additive reals remain true when
\mathcal{R}
is a ring, and in this case we already know that
k
-fold quantified version of polynomial feasibility is complete for each level of the polynomial hierarchy over
\mathcal{R}

[
4
]
. Exotic quantifiers are used in
[
8
]
to extend the polynomial hierarchy over
\mathbb{R}
in order to achieve completeness results for problems from semialgebraic geometry within these newly defined classes.

When restricting to the constant-free Boolean part of these newly defined classes, the exotic quantifiers can be eliminated, thereby yielding completeness results in the Boolean hierarchy over
\mathbb{R}
that is treated in our next corollary. In the same way that we modified the proof that
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-complete to show that
\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\exists\mathcal{R}
-complete, we may modify the proof that
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
-complete to show that
\exists_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}
-complete.

Corollary 17

(Higher Boolean Cook-Levin Analogue)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a bipointed structure of finite type. Then for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq 1
,
\exists_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}
-complete and
\forall_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\forall_{k}\mathcal{R}
-complete under polynomial-time Turing machine reductions.

To the best of our knowledge, the first results about higher levels of the Boolean hierarchy over a structure
\mathcal{R}
appear in
[
11
]
, wherein
\mathcal{R}
is taken to be
\mathbb{R}_{\textup{add}}
or
\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}
, among other structures. In these cases, the authors show that
\exists\mathbb{R}_{\textup{add}}=\textup{NP}=\exists\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}
, and furthermore that the Boolean hierarchy over these structures coincides with the classical polynomial hierarchy.

The authors of
[
8
]
introduce the Boolean hierarchy over
\mathbb{R}
in terms of machine models and show completeness for every level of this hierarchy of a problem that is essentially the circuit version of
\exists_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathbb{R})
and
\forall_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathbb{R})
. This hierarchy also is introduced in
[
47
]
as the real hierarchy by
defining

\exists_{k}\mathbb{R}
as the closure of a problem essentially equivalent to
\exists_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathbb{R})
under polynomial-time Turing machine reductions.
Corollary

17
offers another proof that their definition in terms of complete problems coincides the machine-based definition found here and in
[
8
]
.

Finally, this hierarchy is introduced as the real polynomial hierarchy in
[
24
]
, wherein it is defined using real Turing machines. These are easily seen to be equivalent to
\mathbb{R}
-machines, establishing that their hierarchy is indeed to Boolean hierarchy over
\mathbb{R}
. A numer of problems have already been identified as complete for classes in the second level of this hierarchy, including area-universality
[
13
]
, Hausdorff-distance
[
29
]
, escape-games
[
12
]
, surjectivity
[
8
,
49
]
, and image density
[
8
,
30
]
.

4
Fagin’s Theorem Over a Structure

The idea behind our results in this section is a generalization of the idea behind Fagin’s theorem: It is possible to encode the computation of a machine in second-order logical formula. In the setting of Fagin’s theorem, the machine is a polynomial-time Turing machine, and the formula is a second-order formula that is interpreted in finite structures. In our setting, the machine is a polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine, and the formula is a second-order metafinite formula that is interpreted in metafinite structures over
\mathcal{R}
, which we will abbreviate to
\mathcal{R}
-structures.

In more detail, let
M
be an
\mathcal{R}
-machine running in time
n^{m}
, and let
\mathcal{D}
be an
\mathcal{R}
-structure for a metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
. The time bound of
M
allows us to represent the computation of
M
on input
\mathcal{D}
in a finite computation table, as depicted in
Table

1
. We may describe an accepting computation table with the metafinite formula
\varphi_{M}(\overline{Y})\equiv(\textup{{start}}_{M}\wedge\textup{{update}}\wedge\textup{{accept}})(\overline{Y}).
The intended interpretation of the subformulas
\textup{{start}}_{M}(\overline{Y})
,
\textup{{update}}(\overline{Y})
and
\textup{{accept}}(\overline{Y})
is the same as in the construction of
\varphi_{v}(\overline{y})
in
Lemma

13
. However, while
\varphi_{v}(\overline{y})
describes an accepting computation of
M
on a
fixed
input
v
, the formula
\varphi_{M}(\overline{Y})
describes an accepting computation of
M
on input
\mathcal{D}
for
any

\mathsf{MVoc}
-structure
\mathcal{D}
. In order to achieve this, we construct a subformula
\textup{{input}}_{M}(\overline{Y})
of
\textup{{start}}_{M}(\overline{Y})
such that
\textup{{input}}_{M}(\overline{Y})
expresses the encoding of
\mathcal{D}
as a string in
R^{*}
when it is interpreted in
\mathcal{D}
.

By encoding the time
t
, the label
\ell_{t}
, and the index
\nu_{i,t}
as tuples in
A^{m}
, we capture the computation table of
M
on input
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
with a finite sequence
\overline{Z}
of weight functions. The formula
\varphi_{M}(\overline{Y})
is constructed so that
\overline{Z}
correctly represents an accepting computation table of
M
on input
\mathcal{D}
if and only if
\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi_{M}(\overline{Z})
. Since
M(\mathcal{D})=1
if and only if there is such a
\overline{Z}
, we see that
M(\mathcal{D})=1\text{ if and only if }\mathcal{D}\vDash\exists\overline{Y}\varphi_{M}(\overline{Y}).
By allowing some variables in
\textup{{start}}_{M}(\overline{Y})
to remain unconstrained, we make space for witness weight functions
W
in the input to
M
, which yields the following lemma.

Lemma 18

(Encoding Computations in Second-Order Formulas)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a bipointed structure,
M
be a polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine,
q
be a positive integer, and
\mathsf{MVoc}
be a metafinite vocabulary over
\mathcal{R}
. Then for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a second-order formula
\exists\overline{Y}\varphi_{M,k}(X_{1},\dots,X_{k},\overline{Y})
in the vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
, with
\varphi_{M,k}
first-order, such that for all
\mathsf{MVoc}
-structures
\mathcal{D}
and all weight functions
W_{1},\dots,W_{k}\colon A^{q}\to R
,

M(\mathcal{D},W_{1},\dots,W_{k})=1\text{ if and only if }\mathcal{D}\vDash\exists\overline{Y}\varphi_{M,k}(W_{1},\dots,W_{k},\overline{Y}).

Theorem 19

(Fagin’s Analogue)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a bipointed structure. Then
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
on metafinite structures over
\mathcal{R}
.

Proof Sketch.

We first show that the data complexity of
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
. Suppose
\Phi
is a
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
sentence, which means that
\Phi
is of the form
\Phi\equiv\exists X_{1}\dots\exists X_{N}\varphi(X_{1},\dots,X_{N}),
where
X_{i}
is a second-order variable of arity
k_{i}
, and
\varphi(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})
is a first-order formula in some metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
over
\mathcal{R}
. To show that
\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{Struct}(\mathsf{MVoc}):\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi\}\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
, it suffices to show that given an
\mathsf{MVoc}
-structure
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
and weight functions
W_{1},\dots,W_{N}
with
W_{i}\colon A^{k_{i}}\to R
, an
\mathcal{R}
-machine can decide in polynomial time if
\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi(W_{1},\dots,W_{N}).

This is essentially a matter of showing that an
\mathcal{R}
-machine requires only polynomial-time overhead when simulating the operations of the primary part
\mathcal{A}
of an
\mathcal{R}
-structure
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
that it receives as input because then an
\mathcal{R}
-machine can calculate the truth value of
\varphi(W_{1},\dots,W_{N})
in
\mathcal{D}
in polynomial time. Simulating these operations may be done in polynomial time by analyzing the encoding of the structure
\mathcal{D}
as a string in
R^{*}
according to our knowledge of the fixed metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
of
\mathcal{D}
.

We next show, for any metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
over
\mathcal{R}
, that if
L\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
is a decision problem of
\mathsf{MVoc}
-structures, then there is a sentence
\Phi
of
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathsf{MVoc})
describing
L
. Each
L\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
is of the form
L=\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{Struct}(\mathsf{MVoc}):(\exists W:A^{q}\to R)\ M(\mathcal{D},W)=1\}
for some positive integer
q
and some polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
.
Lemma

18
implies that
L=\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{Struct}(\mathsf{MVoc}):\mathcal{D}\vDash\exists X\exists\overline{Y}\varphi_{M,1}(X,\overline{Y})\}.
As
\exists X\exists\overline{Y}\varphi_{M,1}(X,\overline{Y})
is a
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathsf{MVoc})\subseteq\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
sentence, we conclude that
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
on
\mathcal{R}
-structures.
∎

While our Cook-Levin analogue shown in
Theorem

14
assumes that
\mathcal{R}
is of finite type with all constants, our Fagin’s analogue show in
Theorem

19
makes neither of these assumptions. This is due mainly to the way in which descriptive complexity theory approaches complexity classes. We describe in more detail why we can do without each of these assumptions below.

The assumption that
\mathcal{R}
is of finite type is used
Theorem

14
to guarantee that
\mathcal{R}
has an effective encoding, which we require when showing that
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is in
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
. The corresponding part of
Theorem

19
shows that the data complexity of
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
, which we do by constructing a machine that takes input
(\mathcal{D},\overline{W})
and decides, for a fixed formula
\varphi
, if
\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi(\overline{W})
. Since
\varphi
is fixed and not part of the input, we do not need to encode
\varphi
in a way that a machine can understand. Hence, we do not need the assumption that
\mathcal{R}
has an effective encoding, let alone the assumption that
\mathcal{R}
is of finite type. Notably, this means that
Theorem

19
applies to the structure
\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}
, as well as structures
\mathcal{Z}
of infinite type for which we know that
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{Z})
has no complete problems
[
18
]
.

The assumption that
\mathcal{R}
has all constants is used in
Theorem

14
when showing that
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-hard. The corresponding part of
Theorem

19
shows that for each
L\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
there is some
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
sentence
\exists X\exists\overline{Y}\varphi_{M,1}(X,\overline{Y})
that describes the machine
M
that verifies
L
. Since the formula
\varphi_{M,1}(X,\overline{Y})
describes the computation of
M
on inputs of the form
(\mathcal{D},W)
for
any

\mathcal{R}
-structure
\mathcal{D}
when it is interpreted in
\mathcal{D}
, we do not need to use constants to encode a specific
\mathcal{D}
into
\varphi_{M,1}(X,\overline{Y})
. Hence, we do not need the assumption that
\mathcal{R}
has all constants.

Our proof strategy generalizes that found in
[
22
]
, which shows that
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
captures
\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
. A generalization of Fagin’s theorem to the class of arithmetical structures is already found in
[
23
]
. In more detail, arithmetical structures are metafinite structures
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},N,\mathbf{F})
where
N
resembles
N_{0}=(\mathbb{N},0,1,+,\cdot,\max,\sum,\prod)
in a technical sense. In an effort to capture
NP
over arithmetical structures, the authors define the size of such a structure as the length of its binary encoding. In contrast, we define its size as the length of its encoding as a string of natural numbers because our aim is to capture
\textup{NP}(N)
rather than
NP
. Due to differing definitions of the size of an input, our results are not directly comparable.

Independently of our work, the authors of
[
37
]
show that by restricting
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathbb{R})
to those formulas with only rational constants, one can capture
\exists\mathbb{R}
over discrete
\mathbb{R}
-structures whose definition makes use of the fact that
\mathbb{N}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. Since we work with structures
\mathcal{R}
whose universe may contain neither
\mathbb{Q}
nor
\mathbb{N}
, we restrict
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
to those formulas with only
0
and
1
as constants, and we capture
\exists\mathcal{R}
over Boolean
\mathcal{R}
-structures.

Corollary 20

(Boolean Fagin’s Analogue)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a bipointed structure. Then
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\exists\mathcal{R}
on Boolean metafinite structures over
\mathcal{R}
.

Proof Sketch.

Suppose
\Phi\equiv\exists\overline{X}\varphi(\overline{X})
is a sentence of
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathsf{MVoc})
for a metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
over
\mathcal{R}
, and let
L=\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{BStruct}(\mathsf{MVoc}):\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi\}.
Since all constants from
\mathcal{R}
occurring in
\varphi
are either 0 or 1, the
\mathcal{R}
-machine from
Theorem

19
that decides if
\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi(\overline{W})
is constant free. Moreover, since
L
is a decision problem of Boolean
\mathcal{R}
-structures, we see that
L\in\textup{BP}(\textup{NP}^{0}(\mathcal{R}))=\exists\mathcal{R}
. From this we conclude that the data complexity of
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
is
\exists\mathcal{R}
.

Each
L\in\exists\mathcal{R}
is of the form
L=\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{BStruct}(\mathsf{MVoc}):(\exists W\colon A^{q}\to R)\ M(\mathcal{D},W)=1\}
for some positive integer
q
and some constant-free, polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
.
Lemma

18
implies that
L=\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{BStruct}(\mathsf{MVoc}):\mathcal{D}\vDash\exists X\exists\overline{Y}\varphi_{M,1}(X,\overline{Y})\}
. Furthermore, since
M
is constant free, all constants from
\mathcal{R}
occurring in
\varphi_{M,1}
are 0 or 1. Thus,
\exists X\exists\overline{Y}\varphi_{M,1}(X,\overline{Y})
is an
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
sentence, and from this we conclude that
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\exists\mathcal{R}
on Boolean
\mathcal{R}
-structures.
∎

An advantage using the class of Boolean
\mathcal{R}
-structures is that they may be identified with the class of finite structures that is used in classical descriptive complexity theory. Indeed, this is the perspective taken by the authors of
[
25
]
when they show that
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathbb{R}_{\textup{add}})
captures
NP
on finite structures, using the fact that
\exists\mathbb{R}_{\textup{add}}=\textup{NP}
established in
[
11
]
.

Given the similar result that
\exists\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}=\textup{NP}
, one implication of
Corollary

20
is that
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}})
captures
NP
on Boolean
\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}
-structures. In this way, we can see that Boolean
\mathcal{R}
-structures have a natural place in descriptive complexity theory. We do, however, need to use the full class of
\mathcal{R}
-structures when we extend
Theorem

19
to each level of the polynomial hierarchy over
\mathcal{R}
in the following proposition.

Theorem 21

(Higher Fagin’s Analogue)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a bipointed structure. Then for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq 1
,
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
and
\Pi_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
on metafinite structures over
\mathcal{R}
.

Proof Sketch.

The argument showing that
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
on
\mathcal{R}
-structures follows from
Lemma

18
in essentially the same way as the argument showing that
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
on
\mathcal{R}
-structures, with small modifications and an additional step to account for the alternating string of quantifiers in the definition of
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
and
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
.
∎

In the same way that we modified the proof that
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
on
\mathcal{R}
-structures to show that
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\exists\mathcal{R}
on Boolean
\mathcal{R}
-structures, we may modify the proof that
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
on
\mathcal{R}
-structures to show that
\exists_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}
on Boolean
\mathcal{R}
-structures.

Corollary 22

(Higher Boolean Fagin’s Analogue)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a bipointed structure. Then for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq 1
,
\exists_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}
and
\forall_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\forall_{k}\mathcal{R}
on Boolean metafinite structures over
\mathcal{R}
.

5
Oracles and Hierarchies

The classical polynomial hierarchy was originally defined in terms of oracle classes
[
54
]
. Since the polynomial hierarchy is now usually defined in terms of machine models, characterizing each level of this hierarchy in terms of oracles requires proof. The proof strategy we use below is a generalization of that found in
[
1
]
, for the classical polynomial hierarchy, and
[
11
]
, for the polynomial hierarchy over
\mathbb{R}_{\textup{add}}
and a number of related structures. The latter proof was already generalized to rings in
[
4
]
.

Theorem 23

(Oracle Polynomial Hierarchy Characterization)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a bipointed structure. Then for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Sigma_{k+1}\mathcal{R}=\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}
and
\Pi_{k+1}\mathcal{R}=\textup{coNP}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}.

Proof Sketch.

We proceed by induction on
k\in\mathbb{N}
. The base case holds immediately, and in the inductive step establishing that
\Sigma_{k+1}\mathcal{R}=\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}
, the difficult inclusion to show is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}\subseteq\Sigma_{k+1}\mathcal{R}
. Our inductive hypothesis implies that it is sufficient to show that
a polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
with a
Q\in\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
oracle may be simulated by a polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine
N
with an
S\in\Pi_{k-1}\mathcal{R}
oracle, together with the help of witness strings that are provided in addition to the input.
This is possible because for each
Q\in\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
there is some
S\in\Pi_{k-1}\mathcal{R}
such that
u\in Q
if and only if there is some
w
such that
(u,w)\in S
, and
u\not\in Q
if and only if
(u,w^{\prime})\not\in S
for all
w^{\prime}.
Given the answers
b_{1},\dots,b_{p}\in\{0,1\}
to the queries
u_{1},\dots,u_{p}
that
M
makes on input
v
, as well as the witness strings
w_{1},\dots,w_{p}
and
w^{\prime}_{1},\dots,w^{\prime}_{p}
, the machine
N
can simulate
M
by branching according to the answers
b_{1},\dots,b_{p}
. Furthermore,
N
can check that these answers are correct by querying
(u_{i},w_{i})\in S
if
b_{i}=1
and querying
(u_{i},w^{\prime}_{i})\in S
if
b_{i}=0.
Thus,
\Sigma_{k+1}\mathcal{R}=\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}
, and this implies
\Pi_{k+1}\mathcal{R}=\textup{coNP}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}
, thereby completing our induction.
∎

One usually states the corresponding theorem for the classical polynomial hierarchy as
\Sigma_{k+1}=\textup{NP}^{\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}}
, where
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}
is the problem of deciding if a quantified Boolean formula of the appropriate form is true. This is equivalent to saying that
\Sigma_{k+1}=\textup{NP}^{\Sigma_{k}}
because
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}
is
\Sigma_{k}
-complete. We use the oracle class
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
, rather than the single oracle
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
, so that
Theorem

23
applies to structures for which
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is not
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
-complete. Modifying the proof of
Theorem

23
, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 24

(Oracle Boolean Hierarchy Characterization)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a bipointed structure. Then for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\exists_{k+1}\mathcal{R}=\exists\mathcal{R}^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}^{0}}
and
\forall_{k+1}\mathcal{R}=\forall\mathcal{R}^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}^{0}}.

This corollary tells us that it is unlikely that we can replace the non-Boolean oracle class
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}^{0}
with the Boolean oracle class
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}
, as one might expect from a straightforward analogy with the classical polynomial hierarchy. For example, while
\textup{NP}^{\textup{NP}}=\Sigma_{2}
and
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})}=\Sigma_{2}\mathcal{R}
,
Corollary

24
tells us that
\exists\mathcal{R}^{\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}}=\exists_{2}\mathcal{R}
, so it is unlikely that
\exists\mathcal{R}^{\exists\mathcal{R}}=\exists_{2}\mathcal{R}
. It is instructive to reflect on why our proof requires the constant-free, non-Boolean oracle class
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}
when showing that
\exists\mathcal{R}^{\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}}=\exists_{2}\mathcal{R}
.

In order to show the inclusion
\exists\mathcal{R}^{\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}}\subseteq\exists_{2}\mathcal{R}
, we use a constant-free machine
N
and witness strings to simulate a constant-free machine
M
with an oracle
Q\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}
. If
Q
were not in the constant-free part of
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
, we could hide a constant in the oracle
Q
, making a constant-free simulation impossible. In order to show the inclusion
\exists_{2}\mathcal{R}\subseteq\exists\mathcal{R}^{\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}}
, we replace witness strings
w\in R^{*}
with an oracle
Q\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}
. Since witness strings can be non-Boolean, it should generally be impossible to replace them with an oracle in the Boolean part of
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}
. Thus, we take
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}
as our oracle class because we need to take the constant-free part of
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
, but we cannot take the Boolean part of
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}
.

6
Conclusion

We have characterized the polynomial and Boolean hierarchies over a first-order structure
\mathcal{R}
in terms of machine models, complete problems, descriptive complexity, and oracles. Additionally, we have shown that descriptive complexity is well suited to working with algorithms over infinite vocabulary structures, such as real vector spaces. In this way, we have answered fundamental questions about the complexity of algorithms presented in terms of the primitive operations present in a first-order structure.

Appendix A
Extended Preliminaries

The section is meant as an introductory material for readers who are familiar with theoretical computer science or mathematics, but who may not be familiar with first-order logic, machine models over first-order structures, or the descriptive complexity of metafinite structures. We assume that many readers will use this section only as a form of reference to look up certain details of our definitions when necessary.

A.1
First-Order Structures

In many fields of mathematics and computer science, one studies structures
\mathcal{R}
composed of a set
R
, called the universe or domain of the structure, together with some elements
c\in R
, called constants, some functions
f:R^{k}\to R
, and some relations
P\subseteq R^{k},
where
k
can be any positive integer. These are called first-order structures because the language of first-order logic is designed to speak about them. In this section, we formally introduce first-order structures, leaving the syntax and semantics of first-order logic to
Section

A.2
.

The symbols
c
,
f
, and
P
that we use to refer to the elements, functions, and relations of a structure form a vocabulary with which we can speak about the structure. Formalizing these vocabularies is the first step towards formalizing the relationship between language and structure via first-order logic and first-order structures.

Definition 25

(Vocabularies)
.

A vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
consists of three disjoint, possibly empty sets:

1.

a set
\mathsf{Con}
of constant symbols
c
,

2.

a set
\mathsf{Fun}
of function symbols
f
, each with a positive integer
k
called the arity of
f
.

3.

a set
\mathsf{Rel}
of relation symbols
P
, each with a positive integer
k
called the arity of
P
.

In general, we will write vocabularies as
\mathsf{Voc}=(\mathsf{Con},\mathsf{Fun},\mathsf{Rel})
. If
\mathsf{Voc}
is finite, we may write

\mathsf{Voc}=(c_{1},\dots c_{|\mathsf{Con}|},f_{1},\dots,f_{|\mathsf{Fun}|},P_{1},\dots,P_{|\mathsf{Rel}|}).

Remark 26
.

We make no assumption about the nature of symbols. They can be the elements of any set, including elements such as real numbers that we can never write down on paper in their entirety. Nonetheless, just as we can refer to a real number
r\in\mathbb{R}
with the variable “
r
”, we can refer to these infinite symbols with the variables “
c
”, “
f
”, and “
P
”. Technically, “
c
”, “
f
”, and “
P
” are metavariables of our metalanguage, which is the language that we use to speak about the formal languages of first-order logic built from such symbols. In this case, our metalanguage is the language of mathematics.

Symbols have no inherent meaning. Though we think of a constant symbol
c
as something that can refer to an element of some set, vocabularies do not specify a fixed interpretation of
c
. Similarly, there is no fixed function that a function symbol
f
refers to, nor a fixed relation that a relation symbol
P
refers to. Providing an interpretation of these symbols as constants, functions, and relations in a given set results in a
first-order structure
, which we will often simply call a structure.

Definition 27

(Structures)
.

A (first-order) structure
\mathcal{R}
for a vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}=(\mathsf{Con},\mathsf{Fun},\mathsf{Rel})
, also called a
\mathsf{Voc}
-structure, provides an interpretation of the symbols in
\mathsf{Voc}
by specifying the following:

1.

a nonempty set
R
called the universe, domain, or underlying set of
\mathcal{R}
,

2.

an element
c^{\mathcal{R}}\in R
for each
c\in\mathsf{Con}
,

3.

a function
f^{\mathcal{R}}\colon R^{k}\to R
for each
f\in\mathsf{Fun}
with arity
k
,

4.

a relation
P^{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq R^{k}
for each
P\in\mathsf{Rel}
, with arity
k
.

A structure is a structure for some vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
. This means that each structure is equipped with a particular vocabulary. Given a structure
\mathcal{R}
, the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
is
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
. We often write structures as

\mathcal{R}=(R,(c^{\mathcal{R}}:c\in\mathsf{Con}),(f^{\mathcal{R}}:f\in\mathsf{Fun}),(P^{\mathcal{R}}:P\in\mathsf{Rel})),

where the universe of
\mathcal{R}
is often denoted with the same character, possibly in a different font. We use sequence notation rather than set notation to emphasize that, for example,
(c^{\mathcal{R}}:c\in\mathsf{Con})
is the function that takes
c
to
c^{\mathcal{R}}
, rather than the image of this function. If
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
is finite, we may write

\mathcal{R}=(R,c^{\mathcal{R}}_{1},\dots c^{\mathcal{R}}_{|\mathsf{Con}|},f^{\mathcal{R}}_{1},\dots,f^{\mathcal{R}}_{|\mathsf{Fun}|},P^{\mathcal{R}}_{1},\dots,P^{\mathcal{R}}_{|\mathsf{Rel}|}).

In practice, we will often conflate a symbol of
\mathsf{Voc}
with its interpretation in a
\mathsf{Voc}
-structure
\mathcal{R}
by dropping the superscript
\mathcal{R}
, although it will sometimes be useful to maintain this distinction.

Remark 28
.

We could have defined structures without reference to vocabularies, but as soon as we want to talk about the relationship between a structure
\mathcal{R}
and some language, we need to choose a vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
, and we thereby arrive at
\mathsf{Voc}
-structures anyways. For this reason, structures come equipped with a choice of vocabulary, and different choices result in different structures. This may unsettle the reader who notices that there are many different but essentially equivalent choices of vocabulary for a given mathematical structure. We assure this reader that it is possible to translate between different choices of vocabulary, as explained Section 5.3, especially Remark 1, of
[
28
]
.

Example 29

(Booleans)
.

Boolean values are often represented with the set
\{0,1\}
where
0
represents false and
1
represents true. We may regard Booleans as a structure for the vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{bool}}=(0,1)
containing two constant symbols
0
and
1
by setting
\mathcal{B}=(\{0,1\},0^{\mathcal{B}},1^{\mathcal{B}})
where
0^{\mathcal{B}}=0
and
1^{\mathcal{B}}=1
. Since the interpretation of
0
and
1
is clear in
\mathcal{B}
, we may drop the superscript
\mathcal{B}
and write
\mathcal{B}=(\{0,1\},0,1)
.

Example 30

(Graphs)
.

A directed graph
(V,R)
is a set of vertices
V
together with a binary edge relation
R\subseteq V^{2}
. We may regard a directed graph as a structure for the vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{graph}}=(E)
containing only the binary relation symbol
E
by setting
\mathcal{G}=(V,E^{\mathcal{G}})
where
E^{\mathcal{G}}=R
. If
E^{\mathcal{G}}
is a symmetric relation, then we may view
\mathcal{G}
as an undirected graph. For example,
\mathcal{K}_{2}=(\{0,1\},\{(0,1),(1,0)\})
is a
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{graph}}
-structure representing the complete undirected graph on two vertices.

Example 31

(Partially Ordered Sets)
.

A partially ordered set
(X,R)
is a set
X
together with a binary relation
R\subseteq X^{2}
such that
R
is reflexive, antisymmetric, and transitive. We may regard a partially ordered set as a structure for the vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{pos}}=(\leq)
containing the binary relation symbol
\leq
by setting
\mathcal{X}=(X,\leq^{\mathcal{X}})
where
\leq^{\mathcal{X}}=R
. For example,
\mathcal{N}=(\mathbb{N},\leq)
is a
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{pos}}
-structure where
\leq^{\mathcal{N}}=\leq
is the usual order on the natural numbers. Similarly,
\mathcal{P}_{n}=(2^{\{1,\dots,n\}},\subseteq)
is a
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{pos}}
-structure where
\leq^{\mathcal{P}_{n}}=\subseteq
is the subset inclusion relation.

Example 32

(Monoids)
.

A monoid
(M,0,+)
is a set
M
together with an associative binary operation
+:M^{2}\to M
and a constant
0\in M
that is an identity for the
+
operation. We may regard a monoid as a structure for the vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{mon}}=(0,+)
by setting
M=(M,0^{M},+^{M})
where
0^{M}=0
and
+^{M}=+
. For example,
\mathcal{N}=(\mathbb{N},0,+)
is
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{mon}}
-structure where
0^{\mathcal{N}}=0\in\mathbb{N}
and
+^{\mathcal{N}}=+
is addition. Similarly,
\mathcal{Z}=(\mathbb{Z},1,\cdot)
is a
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{mon}}
