-structure where
0^{\mathcal{Z}}=1\in\mathbb{Z}
and
+^{\mathcal{Z}}=\cdot
is multiplication.

Example 33

(Groups)
.

A group
(G,0,-,+)
is a monoid
(G,0,+)
together with an operation
-\colon G\to G
that maps each element
x
of
G
to its inverse
-x
under the
+
operation. If
x+y=y+x
for all
x
and
y
in
G
, then we say that
\mathcal{G}
is a commutative group. We may regard a group as a structure for the vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{group}}=(0,-,+)
. For example,
\mathcal{Z}=(\mathbb{Z},0,-,+)
is a
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{group}}
-structure where each of the symbols
0
,
-
, and
+
receive their expected interpretation. Similarly,
\mathcal{Q}=(\mathbb{Q}\setminus\{0\},1,^{-1},\cdot)
is a
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{group}}
-structure where
0^{\mathcal{Q}}=1\in\mathbb{Q}
, the function
-^{\mathcal{Q}}=^{-1}
sends
x
to its multiplicative inverse
x^{-1}
, and
+^{\mathcal{Q}}=\cdot
is multiplication.

Example 34

(Rings)
.

A ring
(R,0,1,-,+,\cdot)
is a commutative group
(R,0,-,+)
together with a monoid
(R,1,\cdot)
that is compatible in the sense that the
\cdot
operation distributes over the
+
operation. We may regard each ring as a structure for the vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{ring}}=(0,1,-,+,\cdot)
. For example, the usual definitions of addition and multiplication on
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathbb{C}
give rise to a ring structure on each of these sets where each symbol of
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{ring}}
receives its expected interpretation. Additionally, for any natural number
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{Z}_{m}=(\{0,\dots,m-1\},0,1,-,+,\cdot)
is a
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{ring}}
-structure where the symbols
-
,
+
, and
\cdot
are interpreted as the corresponding operation modulo
m
.

Example 35

(Ordered Rings)
.

An ordered ring
(R,0,1,-,+,\cdot,\leq)
is a ring
(R,0,1,-,+,\cdot)
together with a linear ordering
\leq
that is compatible with the ring structure on
R
in the sense that for all
x,y,z\in R
, if
x\leq y
, then
x+z\leq y+z
, and if
0\leq x
and
0\leq y
, then
0\leq x\cdot y
. We may regard each ordered ring as a structure for the vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{oring}}=(0,1,-,+,\cdot,\leq)
. For example, the ordered ring of real numbers
\mathbb{R}=(\mathbb{R},0,1,-,+,\cdot,\leq)
is a
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{oring}}
-structure.

Example 36

(Real Vector Spaces)
.

A vector space over
\mathbb{R}
is a commutative group
(V,0,-,+)
together with a scalar multiplication operation
\cdot\colon\mathbb{R}\times V\to V
such that for all
r,s\in\mathbb{R}
and all
x,y\in V
,

1.

1\cdot x=x
,

2.

r\cdot(x+y)=r\cdot x+r\cdot y
,

3.

(r+s)\cdot x=r\cdot x+s\cdot x
,

4.

(rs)\cdot x=r\cdot(s\cdot x)
.

We may regard each real vector space as a structure for the vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{lin}}=(0,-,+,(\mu_{r}:r\in\mathbb{R}))
by setting
\mathcal{V}=(V,0^{\mathcal{V}},-^{\mathcal{V}},+^{\mathcal{V}},(\mu_{r}^{\mathcal{V}}:r\in\mathbb{R}))
where each of
0
,
-
, and
+
receives their expected interpretation, and
\mu_{r}^{\mathcal{V}}\colon V\to V
is the function that sends the vector
x
to the vector
r\cdot x
. For example, for any fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{R}^{n}=(\mathbb{R}^{n},0,-,+,(\mu_{r}:r\in\mathbb{R}))
is a
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{lin}}
-structure where
\mu_{r}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=(r\cdot x_{1},\dots,r\cdot x_{n})
. In particular, the linear reals
\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}=(\mathbb{R},0,-,+,(\mu_{r}:r\in\mathbb{R}))
are a
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{lin}}
-structure with
\mu_{r}(x)=r\cdot x
.

Example 37

(Modules over a Ring
\mathcal{R}
)
.

If we replace
\mathbb{R}
in the definition of vector spaces with another ring
\mathcal{R}
, then we obtain the definition of a module over
\mathcal{R}
. We may regard each module over
\mathcal{R}
as a structure for
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{mod}}=(0,-,+,(\mu_{r}:r\in R))
. For example, any commutative group
\mathcal{G}
may be expanded to a module over the ring of integers
\mathcal{Z}
by adding the functions
(\mu_{z}:z\in\mathbb{Z})
to the structure
\mathcal{G}
, where
\mu_{z}\colon G\to G
sends
x
to
\mu_{z}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{z}x
if
z
is nonnegative and to
\mu_{z}(x)=-\left(\sum_{i=1}^{z}x\right)
if
z
is negative.

Remark 38
.

The scalar multiplication operation
\cdot\colon\mathbb{R}\times V\to V
of a vector space
V
cannot in general be viewed as an
m
-ary operation on
V
. To fit the operation
\cdot
into the formalism of single-sorted first-order logic, we split the operation
\cdot
into uncountably many operations
(\mu_{r}:r\in\mathbb{R})
. Another option is to work in the more general system of many-sorted logic, with a sort for scalars from
\mathbb{R}
and a sort for vectors from
V
, in which
\cdot\colon\mathbb{R}\times V\to V
is a valid operation. This example shows that there are mathematical objects that do not fit cleanly into the formalism of single-sorted first-order logic. Topological spaces are another example of this phenomenon. Despite this, we work in single-sorted first-order logic for its simplicity and wide applicability.

Bipointed Structures of Finite Type with All Constants.

In the following sections, we will often encode discrete objects, such as natural numbers, into formulas built from the vocabularies of structures. For this reason, we will often assume that structures have two constants
0
and
1
that we can use to encode discrete objects in binary. We call these structures
bipointed
because they have two distinguished points denoted by
0
and
1
.

Definition 39

(Bipointed Structure)
.

A structure
\mathcal{R}
is bipointed if
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
has two constant symbols
0
and
1
that
\mathcal{R}
interprets as different elements of its universe. That is,
0^{\mathcal{R}}\neq 1^{\mathcal{R}}
.

Many structures are not bipointed. As long as a structure
\mathcal{R}
has at least two distinct elements
a
and
b
in its universe, we may expand the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
to include two constant symbols
0
and
1
, and we may expand the structure
\mathcal{R}
by setting
0^{\mathcal{R}}=a
and
1^{\mathcal{R}}=b
. The expanded structure
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is strictly different from the original structure
\mathcal{R}
, but it is not essentially different.

Example 40

(Bipointed Graphs)
.

The complete undirected graph on 2 vertices
\mathcal{K}_{2}=(\{0,1\},\{(0,1),(1,0)\})
has vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{K}_{2})=(E)
. We may expand this to the vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}^{\prime}=(E,0,1)
and we may expand
\mathcal{K}_{2}
to the structure
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{2}=(\{0,1\},0,1,\{(0,1),(1,0)\})
where
0^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{2}}=0
and
1^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{2}}=1
.

Example 41

(Bipointed Groups)
.

The group
\mathcal{R}=(\mathbb{R},0,+)
has vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})=(0,+)
. We may expand this to the vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}^{\prime}=(0,1,+)
and we may expand
\mathcal{R}
to the structure
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=(\mathbb{R},0,1,+)
by setting
1^{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}=1
.

We will often make two further assumptions about structures that are essential to
Appendix

B
. One of the main contribution of this paper is the observation that these assumptions are not needed for the results in
Appendix

D
and
Appendix

C
. The first assumption is that structures are of finite type. This assumption is essential to the results in
Section

B.1
establishing that
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is in
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
.

Definition 42

(Structures of Finite Type)
.

A structure
\mathcal{R}
with vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})=(\mathsf{Con},\mathsf{Fun},\mathsf{Rel})
is of finite type if both
\mathsf{Fun}
and
\mathsf{Rel}
are finite sets of symbols. Note that
\mathsf{Con}
may be infinite.

The second assumption is that structures have all constants, by which we mean that for all elements of the universe, there is a unique constant naming that element. This assumption is essential to the results in
Section

B.2
establishing that
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-hard.

Definition 43

(Structures with All Constants)
.

A structure
\mathcal{R}
with vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})=(\mathsf{Con},\mathsf{Fun},\mathsf{Rel})
has all constants if there is a bijective correspondence between the universe
R
of
\mathcal{R}
and the set
\{c^{\mathcal{R}}:c\in\mathsf{Con}\}
of constants in
\mathcal{R}
.

If
\mathcal{R}
has all constants, then for every
r
in the universe
R
of
\mathcal{R}
, there is a unique constant symbol
c_{r}
such that
c_{r}^{\mathcal{R}}=r
. Since we often conflate a symbol with its interpretation in a structure, we will often write
r
for both
c_{r}
and
c_{r}^{\mathcal{R}}
. Similarly, we will often write a structure with all constants as

\mathcal{R}=(R,R,(f^{\mathcal{R}}:f\in\mathsf{Fun}),(P^{\mathcal{R}}:P\in\mathsf{Rel})).

If
\mathcal{R}
is a structure without all constants, then
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
denotes the structure in which
(c^{\mathcal{R}}:c\in\mathsf{Con})
has been replaced by
(c_{r}^{\mathcal{R}}:r\in R)
. Note that it is possible for
\mathcal{R}
to have all constants, even uncountably many, but still be of finite type.

Example 44

(Ordered Rings with All Constants)
.

The structure
\mathbb{R}=(\mathbb{R},0,1,-,+,\cdot,\leq)
does not have all constants, but the structure
\overline{\mathbb{R}}=(\mathbb{R},(r:r\in\mathbb{R}),-,+,\cdot,\leq)
does, and
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
remains bipointed because it has both constants
0
and
1
interpreted in their natural way in
\mathbb{R}
. Note that even though
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
has uncountably many constants, it is still of finite type.

Discussion.

In most fields that investigate first-order structures, emphasizing the symbols used to speak about a structure is not common practice, so this emphasis on symbols requires some justification.
To a graph theorist, a directed graph
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
is simply a set
V
together with a binary relation
E\subseteq V^{2}
, and there is no need to mention a symbol “
E
” used to refer to the relation
E
. Similarly, to a group theorist, a group
\mathcal{X}=(X,0,-,+)
is simply a set
X
together with an associative binary operation
+\colon X^{2}\to X
, a unary operation that maps
x
to its inverse
-x
for the
+
operation, as well as an element
0\in X
that is an identity element for the
+
operation. It is just as valid to represent this group in multiplicative notation as
\mathcal{X}=(X,1,^{-1},\cdot)
, so emphasizing particular symbols seems misguided.

Our motivation for specifying the symbols in our language is that this is the first step towards formalizing the relationship between language and structure. For example, let us regard
E
as a symbol, and let
E^{\mathcal{G}}
denote the edge relation of the graph
\mathcal{G}
. That is,
E^{\mathcal{G}}
is the interpretation of the symbol
E
in the graph
\mathcal{G}
. As we will see in the following section, using the symbol
E
, we can write a sentence
\forall x\forall y(E(x,y)\to E(y,x))
specifying the condition that
\mathcal{G}
must satisfy in order for
E^{\mathcal{G}}
to be symmetric. If
E^{\mathcal{G}}
is symmetric, then we say that
\mathcal{G}
satisfies
\forall x\forall y(E(x,y)\to E(y,x))
and we write
\mathcal{G}\vDash\forall x\forall y(E(x,y)\to E(y,x))
.

Formalizing the relationship between language and structure
is surprisingly fruitful. Doing so leads to complete problems for important complexity classes including
NP
and
\exists\mathbb{R}

[
1
,
44
]
, enables machine-independent characterizations of many complexity classes via descriptive complexity theory
[
35
]
, and provides new perspectives on algebraic geometry and analysis, as well as new methods to solve problems therein
[
42
]
.

A.2
First-Order Logic

The relationship between language and structure is the subject of model theory, a branch of mathematical logic. We will focus on first-order logic because it is both widely applicable and well behaved. (Specifically, we will focus on Boolean, single-sorted first-order logic with equality, although we will also treat first and second-order metafinite logic, which is a variant of two-sorted logic.) In the rest of this section, we formally introduce the syntax and semantics of first-order logic.

Symbols of a vocabulary are basic ingredients that first-order sentences are made of. Another kind of basic ingredient is variables.

Definition 45
.

A (first-order) variable is an element of the set
\mathsf{Var}=\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\dots\}
. We sometimes use other characters, such as
x,y,z,\dots
, to denote variables.

Using constant symbols, function symbols, and variables, we may build
terms
that refer to elements of a structure.

Definition 46

(Terms)
.

The set of terms for a vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}=(\mathsf{Con},\mathsf{Fun},\mathsf{Rel})
, also called the set of
\mathsf{Voc}
-terms, is the smallest set of expressions such that:

1.

x
is a term whenever
x\in\mathsf{Var}
,

2.

c
is a term whenever
c\in\mathsf{Con}
,

3.

f(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
is a term whenever
f\in\mathsf{Fun}
has arity
k
and
t_{1},\dots,t_{k}
are terms.

For any term
t
, the set
\mathsf{FV}(t)\subseteq\mathsf{Var}
of free variables of
t
is defined as follows:

1.

\mathsf{FV}(x)=\{x\}
,

2.

\mathsf{FV}(c)=\emptyset
,

3.

\mathsf{FV}(f(t_{1},\dots,t_{k}))=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\mathsf{FV}(t_{i})
.

We may write a term
t
as
t(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
to emphasize that
\mathsf{FV}(t)\subseteq\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}.
For any
\mathsf{Voc}
-structure
\mathcal{R}
and any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, each term
t(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
induces a function
t^{\mathcal{R}}\colon R^{n}\to R
that sends
\overline{r}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})\in R^{n}
to
t^{\mathcal{R}}(\overline{r})
, which is defined as follows:

1.

r_{i}
whenever
t
is the variable
x_{i}
,

2.

c^{\mathcal{R}}
whenever
t
is the constant
c
,

3.

f^{\mathcal{R}}(t_{1}^{\mathcal{R}}(\overline{r}),\dots,t_{n}^{\mathcal{R}}(\overline{r}))
whenever
t
is
f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}).

Definition 47

(Formulas and Sentences)
.

The set of formulas for a vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}=(\mathsf{Con},\mathsf{Fun},\mathsf{Rel})
, also called the set of
\mathsf{Voc}
-formulas, is the smallest set of expressions such that:

1.

t_{1}=t_{2}
is a formula whenever
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are terms,

2.

P(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
is a formula whenever
P\in\mathsf{Rel}
has arity
k
and
t_{1},\dots,t_{k}
are terms,

3.

\neg(\varphi)
is a formula whenever
\varphi
is a formula,

4.

(\varphi\wedge\psi)
is a formula whenever
\varphi
and
\psi
are formulas,

5.

(\varphi\vee\psi)
is a formula whenever
\varphi
and
\psi
are formulas,

6.

\forall x(\varphi)
is a formula whenever
x
is a variable and
\varphi
is a formula

7.

\exists x(\varphi)
is a formula whenever
x
is a variable and
\varphi
is a formula.

If a formula
\varphi
is of the type specified in line 1 or 2, then
\varphi
is an atomic formula. We may drop the parentheses if this enhances clarity, and we may write
\varphi\to\psi
to abbreviate
\neg\varphi\vee\psi
and
\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi
to abbreviate
(\varphi\to\psi)\wedge(\psi\to\varphi)
. We use the symbol
\equiv
to denote equality between formulas so as to avoid confusion with the symbol
=
, which may occur in formulas. For any formula
\varphi
, the set
\mathsf{FV}(\varphi)\subseteq\mathsf{Var}
of free variables of
\varphi
is defined as follows:

1.

\mathsf{FV}(t_{1}=t_{2})=\mathsf{FV}(t_{1})\cup\mathsf{FV}(t_{2}),

2.

\mathsf{FV}(R(t_{1},\dots,t_{m}))=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\mathsf{FV}(t_{i}),

3.

\mathsf{FV}(\neg\varphi)=\mathsf{FV}(\varphi)
,

4.

\mathsf{FV}(\varphi\wedge\psi)=\mathsf{FV}(\varphi)\cup\mathsf{FV}(\psi)
,

5.

\mathsf{FV}(\varphi\vee\psi)=\mathsf{FV}(\varphi)\cup\mathsf{FV}(\psi)
,

6.

\mathsf{FV}(\forall x\varphi)=\mathsf{FV}(\varphi)\setminus\{x\}
,

7.

\mathsf{FV}(\exists x\varphi)=\mathsf{FV}(\varphi)\setminus\{x\}
.

A variable occurring in a formula
\varphi
immediately after a quantifier is bound by that quantifier within its scope, which is the smallest subformula of
\varphi
containing that quantifier.
If a formula
\varphi
has no free variables, then we say that
\varphi
is a sentence. We may write a formula
\varphi
as
\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
to emphasize that
\mathsf{FV}(\varphi)\subseteq\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
, and we sometimes abbreviate this to
\varphi(\overline{x})
. For any
\mathsf{Voc}
-structure
\mathcal{R}
, any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, any formula
\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, and any
\overline{r}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})\in R^{n}
, we define the satisfaction relation
\vDash
as follows:

1.

\mathcal{R}\vDash(t_{1}=t_{2})(\overline{r})
whenever
t_{1}^{\mathcal{R}}(\overline{r})=t_{2}^{\mathcal{R}}(\overline{r}),

2.

\mathcal{R}\vDash P(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})(\overline{r})
whenever
(t_{1}^{\mathcal{R}}(\overline{r}),\dots,t_{m}^{\mathcal{R}}(\overline{r}))\in P^{\mathcal{R}}
,

3.

\mathcal{R}\vDash\neg\varphi(\overline{r})
whenever
\mathcal{R}\not\vDash\varphi(\overline{r})
,

4.

\mathcal{R}\vDash(\varphi\wedge\psi)(\overline{r})
whenever
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi(\overline{r})
and
\mathcal{R}\vDash\psi(\overline{r})
,

5.

\mathcal{R}\vDash(\varphi\vee\psi)(\overline{r})
whenever
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi(\overline{r})
or
\mathcal{R}\vDash\psi(\overline{r})
,

6.

\mathcal{R}\vDash\forall x\varphi(\overline{r},x)
whenever
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi(\overline{r},r)
for all
r\in R
,

7.

\mathcal{R}\vDash\exists x\varphi(\overline{r},x)
whenever
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi(\overline{r},r)
for some
r\in R
.

If
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi(\overline{r})
, then we say that
\mathcal{R}
satisfies
\varphi(\overline{r})
, or that
\mathcal{R}
is a model of
\varphi(\overline{r})
, or that
\varphi(\overline{r})
is true in
\mathcal{R}
.

Example 48

(Booleans)
.

The only terms in the vocabulary of Booleans
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{bool}}=(0,1)
are variables
x
and the constants
0
and
1
. Atomic formulas include
x=y
, which we can make into a sentence
\forall x\exists y(x=y)
by quantifying the variables
x
and
y
. Since
0^{\mathcal{B}}=0\neq 1=1^{\mathcal{B}}
in the Boolean structure
\mathcal{B}=(\{0,1\},0,1)
, we see that
\mathcal{B}
satisfies the sentence expressing that
0
is not equal to
1
. That is,

\mathcal{B}\vDash\neg(0=1).

On the other hand, for the tuple
\overline{r}=(0,0)\in\{0,1\}^{2}
, the terms
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
induce functions
x_{1}^{\mathcal{B}}
and
x_{2}^{\mathcal{B}}
for which
x_{1}^{\mathcal{B}}(\overline{r})=0
and
x_{2}^{\mathcal{B}}(\overline{r})=0
, so

\mathcal{B}\vDash(x_{1}=x_{2})(\overline{r}).

Example 49

(Graphs)
.

The only terms in the vocabulary of graphs
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{graphs}}=(E)
are variables. Atomic formulas include both
x=y
and
E(x,y)
, and sentences include
\forall x\forall yE(x,y)
. Since
(0,1)
is an edge of the graph
\mathcal{K}_{2}=(\{0,1\},\{(0,1),(1,0)\})
, we see that
\mathcal{K}_{2}\vDash E(0,1)
. Since the edge relation of the graph
\mathcal{K}_{2}
is symmetric, we see that

\mathcal{K}_{2}\vDash\forall x\forall y(E(x,y)\to E(y,x)).

However, since
(0,0)
is not in the edge relation
E^{\mathcal{K}_{2}}=\{(0,1),(1,0)\}
, we see that

\mathcal{K}_{2}\vDash\neg E(x_{1},x_{2})(0,0).

Example 50

(Partially Ordered Sets)
.

The only terms in the vocabulary of partially ordered sets
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{pos}}=(\leq)
are variables. Since the ordering relation of any partially ordered set
\mathcal{P}=(P,\leq)
is transitive, we see that

\mathcal{P}\vDash\forall x\forall y\forall z((x\leq y\wedge y\leq z)\to x\leq z).

Since
\mathcal{N}=(\mathbb{N},\leq)
is linearly ordered, we see that

\mathcal{N}\vDash\forall x\forall y(x\leq y\vee y\leq x).

Furthermore,
\mathcal{P}_{2}=(2^{\{1,2\}},\subseteq)
is not linearly ordered because
\{1\}\not\subseteq\{2\}
and
\{2\}\not\subseteq\{1\}
, so we see that

\mathcal{P}_{2}\vDash(\neg(x_{1}\leq x_{2})\wedge\neg(x_{2}\leq x_{1}))(\{1\},\{2\}).

Example 51

(Monoids)
.

Terms in the vocabulary of monoids
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{mon}}=(0,+)
include expressions recursively built from variables
x
and the constant
0
using the
+
symbol. For example, each of
x
,
y
,
0
,
+(x,y)
, and
+(+(x,y),0)
are terms. When it is conventional, we will use infix notation, in which the last two terms become
x+y
and
(x+y)+0
, respectively. Atomic formulas include
x+x=0
and
x+y=y+x
. Since addition in
\mathcal{N}=(\mathbb{N},0,+)
is associative, we see that

\mathcal{N}\vDash\forall x\forall y\forall z((x+y)+z)=(x+(y+z)).

However, there is no additive inverse of
1\in\mathbb{N}
, so we see that

\mathcal{N}\not\vDash\exists x_{2}(x_{1}+x_{2}=0)(1).

Example 52

(Groups)
.

Terms in the vocabulary of groups
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{group}}=(0,-,+)
include all terms of
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{mon}}
, as well as expressions such as
-x
,
-x+y
,
-(x+y)
, and
-x+-y
. Atomic formulas include
-(x+y)=-x+-y
. Since every element of
\mathcal{Z}=(\mathbb{Z},0,-,+)
has an additive inverse, we see that

\mathcal{Z}\vDash\forall x\exists y(x+y=0).

Furthermore, based on our knowledge of addition, we see that

\mathcal{Z}\vDash(x_{1}+-(x_{2}+x_{3})=0)(5,2,3,4).

Example 53

(Ordered Rings)
.

In the vocabulary of ordered rings
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{oring}}=(0,1,+,\cdot,\leq)
, terms include
0\cdot(x+y)
and
1+(0\cdot(x+y))
, as well as any polynomial with coefficients built from the constants
0
and
1
using addition and multiplication: we may see the polynomial
x^{2}+2x+2
as the term

(x\cdot x)+((1+1)\cdot x)+(1+1).

Atomic formulas include
0\cdot x=0
and
0\cdot(x+y)\leq 1+(0\cdot(x+y))
. In the ordered ring of real numbers
\mathbb{R}=(\mathbb{R},0,1,-,+,\cdot,\leq)
, multiplying some real number
r
by
-1
results in
-r
, so we see that

\mathbb{R}\vDash\forall r(-1\cdot r=-r).

Furthermore, since
\sqrt{2}
is a real number, we see that

\mathbb{R}\vDash(x_{1}\cdot x_{1}=x_{2})(\sqrt{2},2).

The ordering of
\mathbb{R}
is compatible with addition and multiplication in
\mathbb{R}
in the sense that addition is a monotone function and multiplication of positives results in a positive, from which we see that

\mathbb{R}\vDash\forall x\forall y\forall z((x\leq y\to x+z\leq y+z)\wedge((0\leq x\wedge 0\leq y)\to 0\leq x\cdot y)).

Example 54

(Real Vector Spaces)
.

In the vocabulary of real vector spaces
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{lin}}=(0,-,+,(\mu_{r}:r\in\mathbb{R}))
terms include
-0+\mu_{\sqrt{2}}(x)+\mu_{1/3}(y)
, as well as any polynomial of degree 1, with coefficients in
\mathbb{R}
whose zero-degree term is zero. We may express the system of linear equations
\pi x+1/2y=0
and
-x+\sqrt{2}x+z=0
with the formula

(\mu_{\pi}(x)+\mu_{1/2}(y)=0)\wedge(-x+\mu_{\sqrt{2}}(x)+z=0).

Because
\mathbb{R}
itself is a real vector space, we see that
\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}=(\mathbb{R},0,-,+,(\mu_{r}:r\in\mathbb{R}))
satisfies the usual axioms of real vector spaces described in
example

36
. In particular, for all
r
and
s
in
\mathbb{R}
, we see that

1.

\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}\vDash\forall x(\mu_{1}(x)=x)
,

2.

\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}\vDash\forall x\forall y(\mu_{r}(x+y)=\mu_{r}(x)+\mu_{r}(y))
,

3.

\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}\vDash\forall x(\mu_{r+s}(x)=\mu_{r}(x)+\mu_{s}(x))
,

4.

\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}\vDash\forall x(\mu_{rs}(x)=\mu_{r}(\mu_{s}(x)))
.

Equality.

In first-order logic, the equality symbol
=
is usually treated as a logical symbol that is interpreted in every structure as the equality relation on the universe of that structure. This uniform interpretation justifies treating the equality symbol as a logical symbol, similar to
\neg
,
\wedge
,
\vee
,
\forall
, and
\exists
, rather than treating it as a relation symbol, whose interpretation may change from structure to structure.

Because the equality symbol is logical rather than relational, equality is not usually included as a relation of structures, even though the formulas of first-order logic may use equality to speak about structures. This is not problematic because for every underlying set of a structure, there is a uniquely determined equality relation on that set that the equality symbol refers to. We highlight the importance of equality here because the main arguments of this paper use equality in an essential way, and it is not clear if our results hold without equality.

To emphasize the convention that equality is considered a logical symbol, one may say first-order logic
with equality
. There are contexts in which the equality-free fragment of first-order logic becomes relevant, such as when comparing the computational complexity of deciding whether or not a structure satisfies a sentence taken from different fragments of first-order logic. This is especially common in research related to the complexity class
\exists\mathbb{R}

[
46
,
48
]
. In this context, one usually includes equality as a relation of structures to emphasize its presence or absence. To translate our results into a form that agrees with this latter convention, one need only add the equality relation to every structure.

A.3
Fragments of Theories:
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
and
\exists_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})

Euclidean geometry, as developed in Euclid’s Elements, is the oldest known mathematical theory formulated in terms of basic assumptions called axioms. Early physical theories were modeled after Euclidean geometry, and their basic assumptions were called laws, such as the laws of motion or the laws of thermodynamics. Explicitly stated basic assumptions remain an important component of modern scientific theories, although philosophers of science would now include other components as well
[
57
]
. Nonetheless, the syntactic view of a theory as a set of basic assumptions has left its mark on the terminology of first-order logic and model theory.

Definition 55

(Theories and Models)
.

Given a vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
, a
\mathsf{Voc}
-theory
T
is simply a set of
\mathsf{Voc}
-sentences.
Whenever a
\mathsf{Voc}
-structure
\mathcal{R}
satisfies each sentence
\varphi
in
T
, we say that
\mathcal{R}
is a model of
T
or that
\mathcal{R}
models
T
, and we write
\mathcal{R}\vDash T
.

Example 56

(Theory of Partially Ordered Sets)
.

In the vocabulary of partially ordered sets
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{pos}}=(\leq)
, the theory of partially ordered sets
T_{\textup{pos}}
is composed of the usual axioms for partially ordered sets stating that
\leq
is reflexive, antisymmetric, and transitive. That is,
T_{\textup{pos}}
is composed of the following sentences:

1.

\forall x(x\leq x)
,

2.

\forall x\forall y((x\leq y\wedge y\leq x)\to x=y)
,

3.

\forall x\forall y\forall z((x\leq y\wedge y\leq z)\to x\leq z).

Since
\mathcal{P}_{n}=(2^{\{1,\dots,n\}},\subseteq)
is a partially ordered set, we see that
\mathcal{P}_{n}\vDash T_{\textup{pos}}
. In general, a
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{pos}}
-structure
\mathcal{P}
is a partially ordered set if and only if
\mathcal{P}\vDash T_{\textup{pos}}
.

Example 57

(Theory of Groups)
.

In the vocabulary of groups
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{group}}=(0,-,+)
, the theory of groups
T_{\textup{group}}
is composed of the usual axioms for groups stating that
0
is an additive identity,
-x
is the additive inverse of
x
, and
+
is associative. That is,
T_{\textup{group}}
contains the following sentences:

1.

\forall x(x+0=x\wedge 0+x=x)
,

2.

\forall x(x+(-x)=0\wedge(-x)+x=0)
,

3.

\forall x\forall y\forall z(x+(y+z)=(x+y)+z)
.

To obtain the theory of commutative groups
T_{\textup{cgroup}}
, we add the following sentence:

[resume]

1.

\forall x\forall y(x+y=y+x)
.

Since
\mathcal{Z}=(\mathbb{Z},0,-,+)
is a commutative group, we see that
\mathcal{Z}\vDash T_{\textup{cgroup}}
. In general, a
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{group}}
structure
\mathcal{G}
is a commutative group if and only if
\mathcal{G}\vDash T_{\textup{cgroup}}.

Example 58

(Theory of Real Vector Spaces)
.

In the vocabulary of real vector spaces
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{lin}}=(0,-,+,(\mu_{r}:r\in\mathbb{R}))
, the theory of real vector spaces
T_{\textup{lin}}
is composed of the usual axioms for real vector spaces stating that the vector space is a commutative group and that scalar multiplication behaves as expected. That is
T_{\textup{lin}}
is the union of the following sets of sentences:

1.

T_{\textup{cgroup}}
,

2.

\{\forall x(\mu_{1}(x)=x)\}
,

3.

\{\forall x\forall y(\mu_{r}(x+y)=\mu_{r}(x)+\mu_{r}(y)):r\in\mathbb{R}\}
,

4.

\{\forall x(\mu_{r+s}(x)=\mu_{r}(x)+\mu_{s}(x)):r,s\in\mathbb{R}\}
,

5.

\{\forall x(\mu_{rs}(x)=\mu_{r}(\mu_{s}(x))):r,s\in\mathbb{R}\}
.

Since
\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}=(\mathbb{R},0,-,+,(\mu_{r}:r\in\mathbb{R}))
is a real vector space,
\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}\vDash T_{\textup{lin}}
. In general, a
\mathsf{Voc}_{\textup{lin}}
-structure
\mathcal{V}
is a real vector space if and only if
\mathcal{V}\vDash T_{\textup{lin}}
.

While many of the usual theories are finite, such as the theory of partially ordered sets and the theory of groups, the example of real vector spaces shows that there are natural examples of infinite theories. Another natural example is the theory
\mathsf{Th}(\mathcal{R})
of a fixed structure
\mathcal{R}
. This theory is composed of all sentences that are true in
\mathcal{R}
. If we restrict the theory of a structure to those sentences in which the only constants that occur are
0
and
1
, then we obtain the Boolean theory of a structure.

Definition 59

(Theory of a Structure)
.

The theory of a structure
\mathcal{R}
is the set
\mathsf{Th}(\mathcal{R})
of sentences in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
that are satisfied in
\mathcal{R}
. That is,

\mathsf{Th}(\mathcal{R})=\{\varphi:\text{$\varphi$ is a $\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})$-sentence such that }\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi\}.

The Boolean theory of a structure
\mathcal{R}
is the set
\mathsf{BTh}(\mathcal{R})
of
\varphi\in\mathsf{Th}(\mathcal{R})
such that every constant occurring in
\varphi
is either
0
or
1
. That is,

\mathsf{BTh}(\mathcal{R})=\{\varphi\in\mathsf{Th}(\mathcal{R}):\text{every constant in $\varphi$ is 0 or 1}\}.

We will call a subset of a theory a
fragment
of that theory. In this way, the Boolean theory of a structure is a fragment of the theory of that same structure. In the following sections, we will show that for many complexity classes
\mathcal{C}
over a structure
\mathcal{R}
, there are fragments of
\mathsf{Th}(\mathcal{R})
that are complete decision problems for
\mathcal{C}
. In order to define these fragments, we first need to define the prenex normal form of formulas, as well as several syntactic gradations within formulas of this form.

Definition 60

(Prenex Normal Form)
.

A formula
\Phi
is in prenex normal form if
\Phi
is a (possibly empty) string of quantifiers followed by a quantifier-free formula
\varphi
. In other words,
\Phi
is of the form

Q_{1}x_{1}\dots Q_{m}x_{m}\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y_{1},\dots y_{n}),

where the variables
x_{i}
are bound by the quantifiers
Q_{i}\in\{\exists,\forall\}
, the variables
y_{j}
are free, and
\varphi
is a quantifier-free formula.

Remark 61
.

We say that a
\mathsf{Voc}
-formula
\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is logically equivalent to a
\mathsf{Voc}
-formula
\varphi^{\prime}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
if, for any
\mathsf{Voc}
-structure
\mathcal{R}
and any tuple
(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})\in R^{n}
,
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
if and only if
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi^{\prime}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
. In every vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
, every
\mathsf{Voc}
-formula
\varphi
is logically equivalent to a
\mathsf{Voc}
-formula
\varphi^{\prime}
in prenex normal form such that
\varphi^{\prime}
has the same free and bound variables as
\varphi

[
56
]
. In particular, every sentence is logically equivalent to a sentence in prenex normal form.

Definition 62

(
\Sigma_{k}
and
\Pi_{k}
Formulas)
.

We say that a formula
\Phi
in prenex normal form is a
\Sigma_{1}
formula if each quantifier
Q_{i}
occurring in
\Phi
is existential, and we say it is a
\Pi_{1}
formula if each quantifier
Q_{i}
occurring in
\Phi
is universal. More generally, we say that
\Phi
is a
\Sigma_{k}
formula if
\Phi
is of the form

Q_{1}\overline{x}_{1}\dots Q_{k}\overline{x}_{k}\varphi(\overline{x}_{1},\dots,\overline{x}_{k},\overline{y}),

where the quantifier blocks
Q_{i}\overline{x}_{i}=Q_{i1}x_{i1}\dots Q_{im_{i}}x_{im_{i}}
are composed of the same quantifier, starting with an existential block
Q_{1}\overline{x}_{1}
and alternating quantifiers
k-1
times between adjacent blocks until reaching the quantifier-free formula
\varphi
. Similarly, we say that
\Phi
is a
\Pi_{k}
formula if the same condition holds, except the first quantifier block
Q_{1}\overline{x}_{1}
is universal.

If
\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is a quantifier-free formula in the vocabulary of a structure
\mathcal{R}
, then we say that
\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is
satisfiable in
\mathcal{R}
if there are elements
r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\in R
such that
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
. We can see from the definition of the satisfaction relation
\vDash
that
\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is satisfiable in
\mathcal{R}
if and only if
\exists x_{1}\dots\exists x_{n}\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathsf{Th}(\mathcal{R}).
In this way, we may identify the set of satisfiable quantifier-free formulas
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
with the first existential fragment
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
of
\mathsf{Th}(\mathcal{R})
that results from existentially quantifying over quantifier-free formulas. More generally, we define the
k
th existential and universal fragments of
\mathsf{Th}(\mathcal{R})
as follows.

Definition 63
.

The
k
th existential fragment of the theory of
\mathcal{R}
is the set
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
of
\Sigma_{k}
sentences
\Phi
in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
that are satisfied in
\mathcal{R}
. That is,

\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})=\{\Phi:\Phi\text{ is a $\Sigma_{k}$ sentence and }\mathcal{R}\vDash\Phi\}.

In particular, we define
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})=\Sigma_{1}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
. Similarly, the
k
th universal fragment of the theory of
\mathcal{R}
is the set
\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
of
\Pi_{k}
sentences
\Phi
in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
that are satisfied in
\mathcal{R}
. That is,

\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})=\{\Phi:\Phi\text{ is a $\Pi_{k}$ sentence and }\mathcal{R}\vDash\Phi\}.

Remark 64
.

The reader versed in computational complexity theory may be troubled by the notation
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
and
\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
when they interpret
SAT
as an abbreviation for “satisfiable”, as then
SAT
would only appropriately describe
\Sigma_{1}
sentences. We hope this cognitive dissonance may be resolved by reinterpreting
SAT
as an abbreviation for “satisfied”
so that one may pronounce
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
as “the
\Sigma_{k}
sentences satisfied in
\mathcal{R}
”. We choose this notation to remain consistent with the standard notation
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}
and
\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}
for the decision problems that generalize
SAT
and provide complete problems for each level of the classical polynomial hierarchy, as in
[
1
]
.

For various structures
\mathcal{R}
, the fragments
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
and
\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
are equivalent to well-known decision problems in the Boolean Turing machine setting and the nonBoolean setting BSS-machine setting. We give a few examples here.

Example 65
.

Over the Boolean structure
\mathcal{B}=(\{0,1\},0,1)
,
\text{SAT}(\mathcal{B})
is equivalent to the Boolean problem
SAT
of deciding the satisfiability of Boolean formulas. This is because each quantifier-free formula
\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{B}
is logically equivalent to a Boolean combination of variable assignments
x_{i}=0
or
x_{i}=1
, which we may think of as setting variable
x_{i}
to false or true, respectively. Similarly,
\Pi_{1}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{B})
is equivalent to the Boolean problem
\Pi_{1}\textup{SAT}
of deciding if a Boolean formula is a tautology. More generally,
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{B})
is equivalent to
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}
, and
\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{B})
is equivalent to
\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}
, as defined in
[
1
]
.

Example 66
.

Over the ordered ring of real numbers with only
0
and
1
as constants
\mathbb{R}=(\mathbb{R},0,1,-,+,\cdot,\leq)
, a quantifier-free formula defines a semialgebraic set presented as a Boolean combination of integer-coefficient polynomial equalities and inequalities. Such a formula is satisfiable in
\mathbb{R}
if and only if this semialgebraic set is nonempty, so
\textup{SAT}(\mathbb{R})=\textup{ETR}
, where
ETR
is the existential theory of the reals decision problem
[
45
,
43
]
.

Example 67
.

Over the ordered ring of real numbers with all constants
\overline{\mathbb{R}}=(\mathbb{R},(r:r\in\mathbb{R}),-,+,\cdot,\leq)
, a quantifier-free formula in the vocabulary of
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
defines a semialgebraic set presented as a Boolean combination of real-coefficient polynomial equalities and inequalities. Such a formula is satisfiable over
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
if and only if this semialgebraic set is nonempty, so
\textup{SAT}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
is the semialgebraic feasibility problem SA-FEAS of
[
3
]
.

As we will see in
Section

A.7
,
\textup{SAT}(\mathbb{R})
is a Boolean decision problem because
\textup{SAT}(\mathbb{R})
may be encoded as a set of binary strings
\textup{SAT}(\mathbb{R})\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*}
. On the other hand, we will see that
\textup{SAT}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
is a nonBoolean decision problem because the presence of real number constants in sentences means there is no binary encoding of
\textup{SAT}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
, which is instead encoded as a set of strings of real numbers
\textup{SAT}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{*}
. In order to derive a Boolean decision problem from a structure including constants other than 0 and 1, we introduce the
k
th existential and universal Boolean fragments of a theory.

Definition 68
.

The
k
th existential Boolean fragment of the theory of
\mathcal{R}
is the set
\exists_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
of sentences
\Phi
in
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
such that every constant occurring in
\Phi
is either
0
or
1
. That is,

\exists_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})=\{\Phi\in\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R}):\text{every constant in $\Phi$ is 0 or 1}\}.

In particular, we define
\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})=\exists_{1}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
. Similarly, the
k
th universal Boolean fragment of
\mathsf{BTh}(\mathcal{R})
is the set
\forall_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
of sentences
\Phi
in
\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
such that every constant occurring in
\Phi
is either
0
or
1
. That is,

\forall_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})=\{\Phi\in\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R}):\text{every constant in $\Phi$ is 0 or 1}\}.

Example 69
.

The only constants in the structure
\mathbb{R}=(\mathbb{R},0,1,-,+,\cdot,\leq)
are 0 and 1, so we have
\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathbb{R})=\textup{SAT}(\mathbb{R})
. Though the structure
\overline{\mathbb{R}}=(\mathbb{R},(r:r\in\mathbb{R}),-,+,\cdot,\leq)
has all constants,
\exists\textup{SAT}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})
restricts to sentences whose only constants are among 0 and 1, so we have
\exists\textup{SAT}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})=\exists_{1}\textup{SAT}(\overline{\mathbb{R}})=\Sigma_{1}\textup{SAT}(\mathbb{R})=\textup{SAT}(\mathbb{R})
.

With this background knowledge in first-order logic, we are now ready to introduce machines over first-order structures. We will first give an extended example of a machine over the structure
\mathbb{R}=(\mathbb{R},0,1,-,+,\cdot,\leq)
to provide some intuition for these abstract devices.

A.4
Extended Example of a Machine over
\mathbb{R}

We provide an extended example of a machine over
\mathbb{R}
that computes the sum of a string of real numbers of variable length. This problem illustrates the role of index registers and
\mathbb{R}
-registers, as well as how
\mathbb{R}
-instructions form the syntax of a basic programming language.

The Algorithm.

Suppose we are working in the structure
\mathbb{R}=(\mathbb{R},0,1,-,+,\cdot,\leq)
. If we would like to compute the sum of a string of real numbers
v=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
, then the following simple algorithm suggests itself: set a variable
i
to have value
1
, and set a variable
x
to have value
r_{i}
before iteratively incrementing
i
and adding
r_{i}
to the value of
x
until and including when
i
reaches
n
.

This algorithm may be expressed in low-level programming language based on the operations of
\mathbb{R}
, as we have done in Table
2
. The result is a finite sequence of instructions, such as
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}+Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
to add two real numbers
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
stored in registers
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
and
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
, as well as indexing instructions, such as
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}2\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}2\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}+1
to increment the natural number
i-1
stored in the register
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}2\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
.

\mathbb{R}
-Registers and Index Registers.

In order to store the real number
r_{i}
in memory with infinite precision, we use an idealized machine with
\mathbb{R}
-registers
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
that are capable of storing a single real number. This machine will have an unbounded sequence of
\mathbb{R}
-registers
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}2\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots
where each
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
can store a single real number
r\in\mathbb{R}
. On input
v=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})\in R^{n}
,
\mathbb{R}
-registers
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i-1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
are initialized to hold the real number
r_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq n
, while all other
\mathbb{R}
-registers are initialized to hold the value
0
. For technical reasons explained in the discussion at the end of
Section

A.5
, we start indexing
\mathbb{R}
-registers with
0
, which causes
r_{i}
to be stored in
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i-1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
.

In order to increment the index
i
in the above algorithm, we also need to store the natural number
i
in memory. Our idealized machine will do this with a bounded sequence of index registers
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}k-1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
where each
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
for
0\leq j\leq k-1
is capable of storing a single natural number. On input
v=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, index register
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
is initialized to hold the value
n
, while all other index registers are initialized to hold the value
0
. The
\mathbb{R}
-registers and the index registers, together with an idealized central processing unit capable of manipulating these registers, form an
\mathbb{R}
-machine as in
Figure

2
.

Since
i
and
n
are natural numbers, they could have been stored in the
\mathbb{R}
-registers. We prefer to use dedicated index registers for two reasons. First, in algorithms based on the structure
\mathbb{R}
, the role of indexing operations is fundamentally different than the role of operations in
\mathbb{R}
. In a sense, indexing operations happen “outside” of the structure
\mathbb{R}
. Second, we would like to generalize this approach to structures
\mathcal{R}
whose universe may not contain the natural numbers.

Instructions, Programs, and Machines.

The instructions in the program of
Table

2
are examples of
\mathbb{R}
-instructions. The full range of possible
\mathbb{R}
-instructions is detailed in
Table

3
. These include instructions to print each constant
0
and
1
of
\mathbb{R}
, to evaluate each function
+
and
\cdot
of
\mathbb{R}
, to branch based on testing the relation
\leq
of
\mathbb{R}
, and to branch on equality
=
, as well as basic indexing instructions. A sequence of
\mathbb{R}
-instructions that ends with the
\mathsf{stop}
instruction forms an
\mathbb{R}
-program.
Table

2
implements the above algorithm for computing the sum of a string of real numbers
v=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
as an
\mathbb{R}
-program
\mathcal{P}
.

The central processing unit of an
\mathbb{R}
-machine interprets and executes the instructions of an
\mathbb{R}
-program until the
\mathsf{stop}
instruction is reached, at which point the machine returns the string
v^{\prime}=(r^{\prime}_{1},\dots,r^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}
that is contained in registers
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}n^{\prime}-1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
, where
n^{\prime}
is contained in
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
. So just as
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
is initialized with the length of the input when the machine starts,
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
determines the length of the output when the machine stops.

This completes the intuitive picture of
\mathbb{R}
-machines presented in
Figure

2
. While it is important to keep this picture in mind, it is also important to note that, formally, an
\mathbb{R}
-machine is just a tuple
M=(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{R},\mathbb{N}^{k},\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
where
\mathcal{P}
is an
\mathbb{R}
-program,
\mathbb{R}
is the structure,
\mathbb{N}^{k}
is a space of index states, and
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
is a space of memory states. For example, if
\mathcal{P}
is the
\mathbb{R}
-program of
Table

2
, then
M=(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{R},\mathbb{N}^{4},\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}})
is an
\mathbb{R}
-machine that computes the sum of a string of real numbers
v=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, meaning that this string can have any length.

Computation.

We now describe how this
\mathbb{R}
-machine works. We use register
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
to store the value
1
and we use register
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}2\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
to store the value
i-1
indexing the register
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i-1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
storing the real number
r_{i}
, for some
1\leq i\leq n
. We also use register
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}3\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
to store the value
0
. Steps 0 through 2 check if the length of the input is 0 or 1, in which case the
\mathbb{R}
-machine stops and returns the value in
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
, which has been initialized to either
0
or
r_{1}
, respectively.

Step 3 sets the value of
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
to
n\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\hss\cr$-$\crcr}}}1
, which is the number of times addition needs to be applied. We use the
\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\hss\cr$-$\crcr}}}
operation, called monus or truncated subtraction, so that indices do not become negative. Starting with the value
i-1=0
, step 4 increments the value
i-1
of
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i-1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
containing
r_{i}
. Step 5 copies
r_{i}
from
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i-1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
into
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
, and step 6 adds the contents of
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
and
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
, storing the result in
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
. This results in
r_{1}+\dots+r_{i}
being stored in
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
after
i-1
executions of steps 4 through 7, which means the program should stop looping when
i-1=n-1
. The value of index register
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
at the end of the execution of a program is the length of the output. Steps 8 through 10 set the value of
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
to 1. Finally, step 12 stops the
\mathbb{R}
-machine and returns the value in register
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
, which is
r_{1}+\dots+r_{n}
.

On input
(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, initialize the registers as follows:

\begin{array}[]{cccccccc}Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=r_{1}&Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=r_{2}&Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}2\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=r_{3}&Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}3\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=r_{4}&\dots&Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}n-1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=r_{n}&Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}n\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=0&\dots\\
&&&&&&&\\
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=n,&I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=0&I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}2\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=0&I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}3\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=0&&&&\end{array}

{\begin{array}[]{ccll}0&:&\mathsf{if}\ I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ 10\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ 1&\textit{\# check if the input has length 0}\\
1&:&I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}+1&\textit{\# increment $I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}$ to 1}\\
2&:&\mathsf{if}\ I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ 11\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ 3&\textit{\# check if the input has length 1}\\
3&:&I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\hss\cr$-$\crcr}}}1&\textit{\# decrement $I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}$ to $n\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\hss\cr$-$\crcr}}}1$}\\
4&:&I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}2\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}2\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}+1&\textit{\# increment the index $i-1$ that points to $r_{i}$}\\
5&:&Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}2\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}&\textit{\# copy $r_{i}$ into $Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}$}\\
6&:&Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}+Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}&\textit{\# add $r_{1}+\dots+r_{i-1}$ in $Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}$ to $r_{i}$ in $Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}$}\\
7&:&\mathsf{if}\ I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}2\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ 8\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ 4&\textit{\# check if $i-1=n-1$}\\
8&:&I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\hss\cr$-$\crcr}}}1&\textit{\# decrement $I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}$}\\
9&:&\mathsf{if}\ I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}3\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ 10\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ 8&\textit{\# check if $I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}$ contains 0}\\
10&:&I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}+1&\textit{\# store $1$ in $I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}$ denoting length of output}\\
11&:&\mathsf{stop}&\textit{\# stop and return $Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}$}\\
\end{array}}

Table 2:
An
\mathbb{R}
-program that computes the sum of a finite sequence of real numbers of variable length.

Structures and Operations.

By allowing each operation of the structure
\mathbb{R}=(\mathbb{R},0,1,-,+,\cdot,\leq)
as a type of
\mathbb{R}
-instruction in
\mathbb{R}
-programs, we capture algorithms and computations over
\mathbb{R}
in the form of
\mathbb{R}
-programs and
\mathbb{R}
-machines. The power of this basic programming language of
\mathbb{R}
-programs is parameterized by the strength of the operations present in the structure
\mathbb{R}
. We can consider additional operations, such as the exponential function
\exp
, as primitive operations by adding them as instructions to our programming language and equipping our machines with the ability to execute these operations. This corresponds to passing to the expanded structure
\mathbb{R}_{\exp}=(\mathbb{R},0,1,+,\cdot,\exp,\leq).

Similarly, we capture algorithms and computations over the complex numbers by modifying our machines in two ways. First, we must change their registers
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}2\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots
from
\mathbb{R}
-registers to
\mathbb{C}
-registers that can hold a single complex number. Second, we must modify the way these machines interpret the constants
0
and
1
to agree with their interpretation in
\mathbb{C}
, and we must change the way these machines execute the operations
+
,
\cdot
, and
\exp
to coincide with their interpretation in
\mathbb{C}
. This corresponds to passing to the structure
\mathbb{C}_{\exp}=(\mathbb{C},0,1,+,\cdot,\exp).

A.5
Register Machines over a Structure
\mathcal{R}

We can see from the discussion in the previous section that any structure
\mathcal{R}
determines a class of
\mathcal{R}
-programs and a class of
\mathcal{R}
-machines. The vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
determines the class of
\mathcal{R}
-instructions, the universe of
\mathcal{R}
determines the type of data
\mathcal{R}
-machines manipulate, and the interpretation of the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
in the universe of
\mathcal{R}
determines the meaning of
\mathcal{R}
-programs when they are executed on
\mathcal{R}
-machines. In this way, we capture algorithms and computations over
\mathcal{R}
in the form of
\mathcal{R}
-programs and
\mathcal{R}
-machines.

Instructions and Programs.

We now formalize this idea for an arbitrary structure
\mathcal{R}
. The definitions of
\mathcal{R}
-program and
\mathcal{R}
-machine that we provide are slight modifications of the definitions found in
[
19
]
and
[
26
]
, which in turn share characteristics with the definitions of machines given in
[
5
]
and
[
22
]
, as well as many others. A discussion comparing our model with other machine models is deferred until the end of this section.

Our formalization begins by defining the basic components of
\mathcal{R}
-programs, namely
\mathcal{R}
-instructions. These instructions are syntactic objects built from the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
, and so we first define
\mathsf{Voc}
-instructions for an arbitrary vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
. Working with arbitrary vocabularies at this stage will allow a
\mathsf{Voc}
-program to be interpreted in any
\mathsf{Voc}
-structure, which can be useful for transferring computational complexity results between structures of the same vocabulary, as is done in
[
39
]
.

We also include a query instruction for an unspecified oracle
Q
that is meant to be interpreted as some
Q\subseteq R^{*}
. Note that query instructions are not allowed in
\mathcal{R}
-programs unless an
\mathcal{R}
-machine has been equipped with an oracle
Q\subseteq R^{*}
.

Definition 70

(
\mathsf{Voc}
-Instructions and
\mathcal{R}
-Instructions)
.

For a vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}=(\mathsf{Con},\mathsf{Fun},\mathsf{Rel})
, a
\mathsf{Voc}
-instruction is an instruction of the form (1)-(9) in Table
3
such that:

1.

j_{0},j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{k},\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
are any nonnegative integers,

2.

c\in\mathsf{Con}
is a constant symbol,

3.

f\in\mathsf{Fun}
is an
k
-ary function symbol, for any positive integer
k
,

4.

P\in\mathsf{Rel}
is an
k
-ary relation symbol, for any positive integer
k
.

For a structure
\mathcal{R}
, an
\mathcal{R}
-instruction is a
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
-instruction.

Computation instructions:

1.

Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=c

2.

Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=f(Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{k}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}})

Branching instructions:

3.

\mathsf{if}\ Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}=Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{2}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{1}\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{2}

4.

\mathsf{if}\ P(Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{k}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}})\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{1}\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{2}

Copy instructions:

5.

Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}

Index instructions:

6.

I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}+1

7.

I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\hss\cr$-$\crcr}}}1

8.

\mathsf{if}\ I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{2}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{1}\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{2}

Stop instruction:

9.

\mathsf{stop}

Query instruction for an oracle
Q

10.

\mathsf{if}\ (Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}})\in Q\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{1}\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{2}

Table 3:
\mathsf{Voc}
-instructions

As we saw in the previous section, we may think of index registers
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
as pointers to
\mathcal{R}
-registers
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
or as a memory location for discrete data. Index instructions of the form
(6)
and
(7)
allow incrementing and decrementing the contents of index registers, which corresponds to moving this pointer left or right along the unbounded sequence
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}2\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots
of
\mathcal{R}
-registers. Since this sequence is one-way infinite, decrement instructions of the form
(7)
use truncated subtraction, or monus, defined as
n\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\hss\cr$-$\crcr}}}m=n-m
if
n\geq m
, and
n\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\hss\cr$-$\crcr}}}m=0
if
n<m
.

Definition 71

(
\mathsf{Voc}
-Programs and
\mathcal{R}
-Programs)
.

For a vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
, a
\mathsf{Voc}
-program
\mathcal{P}
is a finite sequence of labeled
\mathsf{Voc}
-instructions of the form

0:\mathsf{instruction}_{0};\ 1:\mathsf{instruction}_{1};\ \dots;\ \ell_{\mathcal{P}}-1:\mathsf{instruction}_{l-1};\ \ell_{\mathcal{P}}:\mathsf{stop}.

The positive integer
\ell_{\mathcal{P}}+1
is the length of the program
\mathcal{P}
. In each
\mathsf{Voc}
-program
\mathcal{P}
, the
\mathsf{stop}
instruction occurs exactly once, as the last instruction in the sequence. The nonnegative integer
k_{\mathcal{P}}-1
is the largest
j
such that
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
occurs in an instruction of
\mathcal{P}
. For a structure
\mathcal{R}
, an
\mathcal{R}
-program is a
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
-program.

Machines.

Similar to Turing machines, register machines, and random-access machines, each
\mathcal{R}
-machine will contain a fixed
\mathcal{R}
-program. We maintain a distinction between
\mathcal{R}
-programs and
\mathcal{R}
-machines as advocated by Scott in
[
50
]
and endorsed by Gaßner in
[
19
]
. Programs are syntactic objects built from the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
, and machines give meaning to programs by specifying how the instructions are to be interpreted.

Definition 72

(
\mathcal{R}
-Machines)
.

A machine over
\mathcal{R}
, also called an
\mathcal{R}
-machine, is a tuple
M=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{R},\mathbb{N}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}},R^{\mathbb{N}})
specifying the following information:

1.

an
\mathcal{R}
-program
\mathcal{P}
,

2.

the structure
\mathcal{R}
,

3.

a space of index states
\mathbb{N}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}}
,

4.

a space of memory states
R^{\mathbb{N}}
.

We say that the constants of
\mathcal{R}
, other than
0
and
1
, appearing in an instruction of
\mathcal{P}
are the machine constants of
M
. The input and output space of
M
are
R^{*}=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}R^{n}.

In the above definition, we include the structure
\mathcal{R}
as a component of
\mathcal{R}
-machines. We do this so that the
\mathcal{R}
-machine knows how to interpret the symbols in its program, and in this sense we may view the structure
\mathcal{R}
as the CPU from
Figure

2
, while the other components of
\mathcal{R}
-machines correspond in the expected way to this picture.

Note that if
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{Q}
are both
\mathsf{Voc}
-structures, then every
\mathcal{R}
-program
\mathcal{P}
is a
\mathcal{Q}
-program and vice versa. This means that
\mathcal{P}
induces both the
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{R},\mathbb{N}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}},R^{\mathbb{N}})
and the
\mathcal{Q}
-machine
\mathcal{N}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q},\mathbb{N}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}},Q^{\mathbb{N}})
. Despite the fact that these machines have the same program, in general they will interpret this program differently. That is, the machines
M
and
\mathcal{N}
will compute different functions, a notion that we formalize in the following section.

Discussion.

Our definition of
\mathcal{R}
-machines is a slight modification of the definition of BSS RAM’s presented by Gaßner in
[
19
]
. It is a generalization of the original definition of BSS-machines given in
[
5
]
, which resemble register machines because they have high-level instructions from which to build programs. Note that the later definition of BSS-machines given in
[
3
]
was modified to more closely resemble a Turing machine by replacing indirect access instructions with instructions that shift a single head left or right one step at a time. There is a long history of research into computation and complexity over structures. For a brief discussion of this history with extensive references, see the introduction of
[
27
]
.

We have modified the definition of BSS RAM’s found in
[
19
]
in several ways. We allow machines to accept the empty string as an input, and in order to encode the length of this input in an index register—as we do for all inputs—we allow index registers to contain 0, in contrast to the original definition in which only positive integers are allowed. This means we must have an
\mathcal{R}
-register indexed by 0. For uniformity, we also have an index register indexed by
0
.

More significantly, we always allow branching on equality tests, in line with our use of first order logic with equality in which equality is always present by default. (See the note on equality at the end of
Section

A.2
.) Furthermore, we do not include input and output maps since these are fixed in a standard way for each machine, and we do not include the set of labels of
\mathcal{P}
, nor a finite-vocabulary structure
\mathcal{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
that is a reduct of
\mathcal{R}
with sufficient structure to interpret
\mathcal{P}
since both of these may be determined by the program
\mathcal{P}
.

Finally, we avoid calling our machines RAM’s because our programs have no instructions that allow
random
access to memory, even though the copy instruction
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
does allow
indirect
access to memory. When paired with indirect access to memory, an instruction of the form
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=n
, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, would allow random access to memory, as in the paper originally introducing random access machines
[
10
]
. Without such an instruction, our machines are required to sequentially increment
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
until it reaches
n
, so our machines have only sequential access to memory. If one needs to differentiate our machine model from others, we suggest the name
register machines over a structure
.

A.6
Computations Over
\mathcal{R}

Configurations and Transitions.

In order to define precisely how an
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
interprets its program
\mathcal{P}
, we must define the notion of a configuration of
M
. A configuration of
M
is a tuple that encodes a snapshot of
M
at one point in time. This tuple specifies the instruction of
\mathcal{P}
that
M
is about to execute, the current contents of the index registers
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}k_{\mathcal{P}}-1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
and the current contents of the
\mathcal{R}
-registers
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}2\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots
.

Definition 73

(Configurations)
.

A configuration of an
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{R},\mathbb{N}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}},R^{\mathbb{N}})
is a tuple

\mathsf{conf}=(\ell,\nu,\rho)\in\{0,\dots,\ell_{\mathcal{P}}\}\times\mathbb{N}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}}\times R^{\mathbb{N}}.

Here
\ell
is the label of the instruction currently being executed,
\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}}
describes the contents of the index registers, and
\rho\in R^{\mathbb{N}}
describes the contents of the
\mathcal{R}
-registers.
If
\ell=0
, then
\mathsf{conf}
is an initial configuration, and if
\ell=\ell_{\mathcal{P}}
, then
\mathsf{conf}
is a stop configuration. The initial configuration on
v=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})\in R^{n}
is

\mathsf{conf}_{0}(v)=(0,(n,0,\dots,0),(r_{1},\dots,r_{n},0,\dots)).

The state space of
M
is the set
\mathcal{S}_{M}=\{0,\dots,\ell_{\mathcal{P}}\}\times\mathbb{N}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}}\times R^{\mathbb{N}}
of all possible configurations for
M
.
We simply write
\mathcal{S}
, instead of
\mathcal{S}_{M}
, whenever
M
is clear from the context.

We now define the transition relation of
M
, which is a binary relation
\to_{M}
on the state space of
M
.
Again, we write
\to
, instead of
\to_{M}
, whenever
M
is clear from the context.
The relation
\to
describes how one configuration of
M
transitions into another as
M
alters the contents of its registers based on the instructions in its program. We may think of this as the
operational semantics
of
\mathcal{R}
-programs.

Definition 74

(Transition Relation)
.

The transition relation
\to\ \subseteq\mathcal{S}^{2}
of an
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M

is determined as follows.
Suppose
\mathsf{conf}=(\ell,\nu,\rho)\in\{0,\dots,\ell_{\mathcal{P}}\}\times\mathbb{N}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}}\times R^{\mathbb{N}}
is a configuration of
M
.

1.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=c
, then
\mathsf{conf}\to(\ell^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
where

•

\ell^{\prime}=\ell+1
,

•

for all
m\in\{0,\dots,k_{\mathcal{P}}-1\}
,
\nu^{\prime}(m)=\nu(m)
,

•

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho^{\prime}(n)=\begin{cases}c^{\mathcal{R}}&\text{ if }n=j_{0}\\
\rho(n)&\text{ if }n\neq j_{0}.\end{cases}

2.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=f(Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{k}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}})
, then
\mathsf{conf}\to(\ell^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
where

•

\ell^{\prime}=\ell+1
,

•

for all
m\in\{0,\dots,k_{\mathcal{P}}-1\}
,
\nu^{\prime}(m)=\nu(m)
,

•

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho^{\prime}(n)=\begin{cases}f^{\mathcal{R}}(\rho({j_{1}}),\dots,\rho({j_{k}}))&\text{ if }n=j_{0}\\
\rho(n)&\text{ if }n\neq j_{0}.\end{cases}

3.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\mathsf{if}\ Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}=Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{2}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{1}\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{2}
, then
\mathsf{conf}\to(\ell^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
where

•

\ell^{\prime}=\begin{cases}\ell_{1}&\text{ if }\rho({j_{1}})=\rho({j_{2}})\\
\ell_{2}&\text{ if }\rho({j_{1}})\neq\rho({j_{2}})\end{cases}

•

for all
m\in\{0,\dots,k_{\mathcal{P}}-1\}
,
\nu^{\prime}(m)=\nu(m)
,

•

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho^{\prime}(n)=\rho(n)
.

4.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\mathsf{if}\ P(Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{k}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}})\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{1}\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{2}
, then
\mathsf{conf}\to(\ell^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
where

•

\ell^{\prime}=\begin{cases}\ell_{1}&\text{ if }(\rho(j_{1}),\dots,\rho(j_{k}))\in P^{\mathcal{R}}\\
\ell_{2}&\text{ if }(\rho(j_{1}),\dots,\rho(j_{k}))\not\in P^{\mathcal{R}}\end{cases}

•

for all
m\in\{0,\dots,k_{\mathcal{P}}-1\}
,
\nu^{\prime}(m)=\nu(m)
,

•

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho^{\prime}(n)=\rho(n)
.

5.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
, then
\mathsf{conf}\to(\ell^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
where

•

\ell^{\prime}=\ell+1

•

for all
m\in\{0,\dots,k_{\mathcal{P}}-1\}
,
\nu^{\prime}(m)=\nu(m)
,

•

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho^{\prime}(n)=\begin{cases}\rho(\nu(j_{1}))&\text{ if }n=\nu(j_{0})\\
\rho(n)&\text{ if }n\neq\nu(j_{0}).\end{cases}

6.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}+1
, then
\mathsf{conf}\to(\ell^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
where

•

\ell^{\prime}=\ell+1,

•

for all
0\leq j\leq k_{\mathcal{P}}
,
\nu^{\prime}(m)=\begin{cases}\nu(m)+1&\text{ if }m=j_{0}\\
\nu(m)&\text{ if }m\neq j_{0},\end{cases}

•

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho^{\prime}(n)=\rho(n).

7.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\hss\cr$-$\crcr}}}1
, then
\mathsf{conf}\to(\ell^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
where

•

\ell^{\prime}=\ell+1,

•

for all
0\leq j\leq k_{\mathcal{P}}
,
\nu^{\prime}(m)=\begin{cases}\nu(m)\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\hss\cr$-$\crcr}}}1&\text{ if }m=j_{0}\\
\nu(m)&\text{ if }m\neq j_{0},\end{cases}

•

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho^{\prime}(n)=\rho(n).

8.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:\mathsf{if}\ I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j_{2}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{1}\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{2}
, then
\mathsf{conf}\to(\ell^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
where

•

\ell^{\prime}=\begin{cases}\ell_{1}&\text{ if }\nu(j_{1})=\nu(j_{2})\\
\ell_{2}&\text{ if }\nu(j_{1})\neq\nu(j_{2}),\end{cases}

•

for all
m\in\{0,\dots,k_{\mathcal{P}}-1\}
,
\nu^{\prime}(m)=\nu(m)
,

•

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho^{\prime}(n)=\rho(n)
.

9.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell_{\mathcal{P}}:\mathsf{stop}
, then
\mathsf{conf}\to\mathsf{conf}^{\prime}
does not hold for any
\mathsf{conf}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{M}
.

We work only with deterministic machines, which means that the transition relation for any
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
is a partial function
(\to)\colon\mathcal{S}_{M}\rightharpoonup\mathcal{S}_{M}
, analogous to the transition function of Turing machines or the computing endomorphism of BSS-machines. Since
(\to)
is a partial function, may write
(\to)(\mathsf{conf})=\mathsf{conf}^{\prime}
instead of
\mathsf{conf}\to\mathsf{conf}^{\prime}
whenever
(\mathsf{conf},\mathsf{conf}^{\prime})\in\hskip 2.84544pt\to
.

Computations.

The inputs that we can give to an
\mathcal{R}
-machine are finite-length strings
v=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
from the universe of
\mathcal{R}
. As such, the set of strings over the universe of
\mathcal{R}
plays an important role as a domain for computation with
\mathcal{R}
-machines.

Definition 75

(Strings)
.

Given a set
R
, the set of strings over
R
is the set
R^{*}=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}R^{n}
. Elements
v=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
of
R^{*}
are called strings. The size of a string is its length
|v|=n\in\mathbb{N}
. We will typically use
u,v,w,\dots,v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},\dots
to denote strings in
R^{*}
.

Whenever an
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
is given an input
v=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})\in R^{*}
, its registers are initialized so that
v
is stored in its
\mathcal{R}
-registers, and
|v|=n
is stored in its index registers, in the following manner:

\begin{array}[]{cccccccc}Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=r_{1}&Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=r_{2}&Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}2\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=r_{3}&Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}3\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=r_{4}&\dots&Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}n-1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=r_{n}&Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}n\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=0&\dots\\
&&&&&&&\\
