I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=n,&I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=0&I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}2\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=0&I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}3\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=0&\dots&I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}k_{\mathcal{P}}-1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=0.&&\end{array}

The component
r_{i}
of
v
is stored in
\mathcal{R}
-register
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i-1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
because we begin indexing
\mathcal{R}
-registers with 0, for reasons explained in the discussion at the end of
Section

A.5
, but we begin indexing
v
with 1, as is traditional for vectors.

Since we work only with deterministic machines, the input
v\in R^{*}
induces a single, potentially infinite sequence of configurations such that

\mathsf{conf}_{0}=\mathsf{conf}_{0}(v)\text{ and }\mathsf{conf}_{t+1}=(\to)(\mathsf{conf}_{t})

for all
t\in\mathbb{N}
for which
(\to)
is defined on
\mathsf{conf}_{t}
. This sequence is the
computation
of
M
on
v
.

Definition 76

(Computation and Running Time)
.

The computation of
M
on input
v\in R^{*}
is the (potentially infinite) sequence of configurations

\mathsf{conf}_{0}(v)\to\mathsf{conf}_{1}\to\ \dots\to\mathsf{conf}_{n}\to\dots

that cannot be extended. We say that
M
halts on input
v
whenever there is a finite sequence of configurations

\mathsf{conf}_{0}(v)\to\mathsf{conf}_{1}\to\dots\to\mathsf{conf}_{n}

such that
\mathsf{conf}_{n}
is a stopping configuration. In this case, we call the sequence
(\mathsf{conf}_{0}(v),\dots,\mathsf{conf}_{n})
a halting computation, and we say that
M
halts on input
v
in time
T_{M}(v)=n
. If
M
does not halt on input
v
, then we say that
M
diverges on input
v
, and we set
T_{M}(v)=\infty
. The value
T_{M}(v)
is the running time of
M
on input
v
.

If
M
halts on input
v
in configuration
(\ell_{\mathcal{P}},(n_{0},\dots,n_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}),(r^{\prime}_{0},\dots,r^{\prime}_{n_{0}-1},\dots))
, then the
output
of
M
on input
v
is
M(v)=(r^{\prime}_{0},\dots,r^{\prime}_{n_{0}-1})\in R^{n_{0}}
. The value
n_{0}
stored in the zeroth index register
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
when
M
halts determines the length of the output, just
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
is initialized to hold the length of the input when the machine starts. In this way,
M
computes a partial function
M\colon R^{*}\rightharpoonup R^{*}
that sends
v
to
M(v)
whenever
v
is in the domain
\textup{dom}(M)=\{v\in R^{*}:M\text{ halts on input }v\}
.

Definition 77

(Computing a Function with a Time Bound)
.

Given a partial function
F\colon R^{*}\rightharpoonup R^{*}
, we say that
M
computes
F
whenever
M(v)=F(v)
for all
v\in\textup{dom}(M)=\textup{dom}(F)\subseteq R^{*}
. Furthermore, if
F
is a total function, then given a total function
T\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
, we say that
M
computes
F
in time
T
whenever
M
computes
F
and
T_{M}(v)\leq T(|v|)
for all
v\in R^{*}
.

Discussion.

The way we have defined the running time of an
\mathcal{R}
-machine uses
unit cost
for operations, in which each operation in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
can be performed in one time step. Others have considered cost functions in which a basic operation incurs a cost proportional its size, as is done in Chapter 20 of
[
3
]
and
[
33
]
.

We have defined the
size
of an input
v\in R^{*}
to be its length. This means that the size of an element of the universe
r\in R
is
|r|=1
, even if
r
is an infinite object such as a real number. There are other measures of size that one may use to suit one’s purpose. For example, one may use bit size, in which the size of an integer
z\in\mathbb{Z}
is the length of the binary encoding of
z
. This size function is often used when analyzing BSS-machines over the integers, as is done in Chapter 6 of
[
3
]
.

A.7
Complexity Classes Over
\mathcal{R}

Term

Definition

Example

universe

R

\mathbb{R}

element

r\in R

r=\sqrt{2}

strings over
R

R^{*}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}R^{n}

\mathbb{R}^{*}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{R}^{n}

string

v=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})\in R^{*}

v=(0,0,1.2,\sqrt{2},4)\in\mathbb{R}^{*}

size

|v|=n\in\mathbb{N}

|v|=|(0,0,1.2,\sqrt{2},4)|=5\in\mathbb{N}

powerset of strings over
R

2^{R^{*}}=\{S:S\subseteq R^{*}\}

2^{\mathbb{R}^{*}}=\{S:S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{*}\}

decision problem

S\subseteq R^{*}

S_{\ell}=\{v\in R^{*}:\sum_{i=1}^{|v|}r_{i}=\ell\}

complexity class

\mathcal{C}\subseteq 2^{R^{*}}

\mathcal{C}=\{S_{\ell}:\ell\in\mathbb{N}\}

Table 4:
Complexity Theory Terminology

One of the most basic tasks that we may devise an
\mathcal{R}
-machine to carry out is to decide if some string
v
lies in some subset
S\subseteq R^{*}
. An
\mathcal{R}
-machine may decide this for us by computing the characteristic function
\chi_{S}\colon R^{*}\to\{0,1\}
of
S
, for which

\chi_{S}(v)=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }v\in S\\
0&\text{ if }v\not\in S.\end{cases}

In this way, we may view each subset
S\subseteq R^{*}
as a
decision problem
, and if an
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M

decides

S
, we may view
M
as a
decider
.

Definition 78

(Decision Problems and Deciders)
.

A decision problem over
\mathcal{R}
is a set
S\subseteq R^{*}
. We say that an
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
decides
S
when
M
computes the characteristic function
\chi_{S}
of
S
. In this case,
M
is an example of a decider, which is an
\mathcal{R}
-machine that returns
1
or
0
on every input
v\in R^{*}
. We say that
M
accepts
v
if
M(v)=1
, and we say that
M
rejects
v
if
M(v)=0
.

Definition 79
.

The complexity class
\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})
is the set of decision problems that can be decided by a polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine. The complexity class
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
is the set of decision problems
L
such that there is a decision problem
S\in\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})
and some polynomial
q
such that

L=\{v\in R^{*}:(\exists w\in R^{q(|v|)})(v,w)\in S\}.

In case
(v,w)\in S,
we call
w
a witness for
v
. If
M
is a polynomial time
\mathcal{R}
-machine deciding
S
, then we may present
L
equivalently as

L=\{v\in R^{*}:(\exists w\in R^{q(|v|)})M(v,w)=1\}.

In this case, we may view
M
as a verifier, and we say that
M
verifies
L
. Similarly, the complexity class
\textup{coNP}(\mathcal{R})
is the set of decision problems
L
such that there is a polynomial
q
and a polynomial-time machine
M
such that

L=\{v\in R^{*}:(\forall w\in R^{q(|v|)})M(v,w)=1\}.

In addition to analyzing complexity classes
\mathcal{C}
over
\mathcal{R}
, we will relate them to Boolean complexity classes over the domain
\{0,1\}
. Towards this end, we define the Boolean part of decision problems
S
as the set of binary strings in
S
. If
M
is an
\mathcal{R}
-machine that decides
S
, then taking the Boolean part of
S
corresponds to restricting the inputs of
M
to binary strings, or modifying the program of
M
to reject all non-Boolean strings.

Just as we may restrict the inputs to
\mathcal{R}
-machines, may also restrict machines by only allowing the use of the constants
0
and
1
. If
M
uses only these constants, then we say that
M
has no
machine constants.
This becomes relevant if the domain of
\mathcal{R}
is a set like
\mathbb{R}
in which a single element can encode infinite information. Restricting the use of constants brings
\mathcal{R}
-machines closer to Boolean Turing machines. These modifications are summarized in the next definition.

Definition 80
.

The Boolean part of a decision problem
S
is the set
\textup{BP}(S)=S\cap\{0,1\}^{*}
. The Boolean part of a complexity class
\mathcal{C}
is the set
\textup{BP}(\mathcal{C})=\{\textup{BP}(S):S\in\mathcal{C}\}.
If
\mathcal{C}
is defined in terms of
\mathcal{R}
-machines, then
\mathcal{C}^{0}
is the class obtained by restricting to
\mathcal{R}
-machines with no machine constants. We write
\textup{BP}^{0}(\mathcal{C})
for
\textup{BP}(\mathcal{C}^{0})
, and we say that
\textup{BP}^{0}(\mathcal{C})
is the constant-free Boolean part of
\mathcal{C}
.

Warning.

While the Boolean part operator
\textup{BP}(\cdot)
acts directly on complexity classes, the constant-free operator
(\cdot)^{0}
acts on
definitions
of complexity classes by replacing machines in the definition of a complexity class
\mathcal{C}
with constant-free machines to arrive at a well-defined complexity class
\mathcal{C}^{0}
. This means that even if there is an equality of complexity classes
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{D}
, it might be that
\mathcal{C}^{0}\neq\mathcal{D}^{0}
because the definitions of
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
use machines in different ways.

For example, consider the structure
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq}=(\mathbb{R},(r:r\in\mathbb{R}),0,1,-,+,\leq)
. We will show in
Theorem

23
that
\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})^{\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})}=\Sigma_{2}\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq}
. However, we can derive a contradiction from the assumption that
(\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})^{\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})})^{0}=(\Sigma_{2}\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})^{0}
in the following way.

We know from
[
11
]
that
\textup{BP}(\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})=\textup{NP}/\textup{poly}
, the classical nonuniform complexity class defined by machines with polynomial advice, and that
\textup{BP}((\Sigma_{2}\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})^{0})=\Sigma_{2}
, the second level of the classical polynomial hierarchy. Since restricting to constant-free oracle machines does not affect the oracles these machines have access to, we see that
(\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})^{\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})})^{0}=(\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})^{0})^{\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})}
. It is clear that
\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})\subseteq(\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})^{0})^{\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})}
. Thus, if we assume that
(\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})^{\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})})^{0}=(\Sigma_{2}\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})^{0}
, then we have that

\textup{NP}/\textup{poly}=\textup{BP}(\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq}))\subseteq\textup{BP}((\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})^{\textup{NP}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})})^{0})=\textup{BP}((\Sigma_{2}\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{\textup{add}}^{\leq})^{0})=\Sigma_{2},

which is a contradiction because
\textup{NP}/\textup{poly}
contains undecidable problems.

Definition 81
.

For any structure
\mathcal{R}
, we define
\exists\mathcal{R}=\textup{BP}^{0}(\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R}))
as the constant-free Boolean part of
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R}).
Similarly, we define
\forall\mathcal{R}=\textup{BP}^{0}(\textup{coNP}(\mathcal{R}))
, and we define
\delta\mathcal{R}=\textup{BP}^{0}(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})).

Remark 82
.

The notation
\delta\mathcal{R}
is meant to evoke a similarity with the notation
\Delta_{0}=\textup{P}
of the polynomial hierarchy. We will see that
\delta\mathcal{R}\subseteq\exists\mathcal{R}\cap\forall\mathcal{R}
, which resembles
\Delta_{0}\subseteq\Sigma_{1}\cap\Pi_{1}
where
\Sigma_{1}=\textup{NP}
and
\Pi_{1}=\textup{coNP}
.

Definition 83

(Polynomial Hierarchy Over
\mathcal{R}
)
.

We define two sequences of complexity classes
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
and
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
as follows. A decision problem
L\subseteq R^{*}
is in
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
if and only if there is a polynomial
q
and a polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
v\in R^{n}

v\in L\text{ if and only if }(Q_{1}w_{1}\in R^{q(n)})\dots(Q_{k}w_{k}\in R^{q(n)})M(v,w_{1},\dots,w_{k})=1,

where the quantifiers
Q_{i}\in\{\exists,\forall\}
alternate, starting with
Q_{1}=\exists
. The class
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
is defined by the same condition except the first quantifier is
Q_{1}=\forall
. Define the polynomial hierarchy over
\mathcal{R}
as the class
\textup{PH}(\mathcal{R})=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}.

Definition 84

(Boolean Hierarchy Over
\mathcal{R}
)
.

We define the complexity classes
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}
and
\forall_{k}\mathcal{R}
as the constant-free Boolean parts of
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
and
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
, respectively, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
. That is,

\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}=\textup{BP}^{0}(\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R})\textup{ and }\forall_{k}\mathcal{R}=\textup{BP}^{0}(\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}).

The Boolean hierarchy over
\mathcal{R}
is the complexity class
\textup{BH}(\mathcal{R})=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}
.

Example 85
.

If
\mathcal{B}=(\{0,1\},0,1)
is the Boolean structure, then
\Sigma\mathcal{B}=\exists\mathcal{B}=\textup{NP}
and
\Pi\mathcal{B}=\forall\mathcal{B}=\textup{coNP}
.

Example 86
.

If
\mathbb{R}=(\mathbb{R},(r:r\in\mathbb{R}),+,-,\times,/,<)
, then
\Sigma_{1}\mathbb{R}=\textup{NP}(\mathbb{R})
as defined in
[
3
]
, and
\exists_{1}\mathbb{R}=\exists\mathbb{R}
as defined in
[
43
]
.

Example 87
.

If
\mathbb{R}=(\mathbb{R},0,1,-,+,\cdot,\leq)
then
\exists\mathbb{R}=\textup{BP}^{0}(\textup{NP}(\mathbb{R}))
is the complexity class whose defining hard problem is the existential theory of the reals as defined in
[
43
]
.

Definition 88
.

For any complexity class
\mathcal{C}
over
\mathcal{R}
, the complexity class
\Sigma\mathcal{C}
contains a decision problem
L\subseteq R^{*}
if and only if there is a polynomial
q
and a decision problem
S\in\mathcal{C}
such that for all
v\in R^{*}

v\in L\textup{ if and only if }\exists w\in R^{q(|v|)}(v,w)\in S.

Similarly, the complexity class
\Pi\mathcal{C}
contains a decision problem
L\subseteq R^{*}
if and only if there is a polynomial
q
and a decision problem
S
such that for all
v\in R^{*}

v\in L\textup{ if and only if }\forall w\in R^{q(|v|)}(v,w)\in S.

Lemma 89
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N},

\Sigma_{k+1}\mathcal{R}=\Sigma\left(\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}\right)
and
\Pi_{k+1}\mathcal{R}=\Pi\left(\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}\right).

Proof.

Simply pad each witness string so that they are all of the same length.
∎

Oracle Complexity Classes

Oracles are perhaps the central concept in computability theory because they introduce a notion of relative computability
[
53
]
, and they have played a significant role in computational complexity theory.

Definition 90
.

An
\mathcal{R}
-machine with a
Q\subseteq R^{*}
oracle is an
\mathcal{R}
-machine whose program may contain instructions of the form

\mathsf{if}\ (Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}})\in Q\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{1}\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{2}.

Definition 91
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a complexity class, then
\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\mathcal{C}}
is the complexity class of decision problems
L\subseteq R^{*}
for which there is a decision problem
Q\in\mathcal{C}
and some polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
such that
M
with oracle access to
Q
decides
L
.

Example 92
.

The class
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})}
is the class
(\Sigma\textup{P}(\mathcal{R}))^{\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})}=\Sigma\left(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})}\right).

Usually, complexity classes defined in terms of oracle machines are defined using a single fixed oracle. For example, in the classical polynomial hierarchy one may show that
\Sigma_{2}=\textup{NP}^{\textup{SAT}}
. Using the fact that
SAT
is
NP
-complete, one may show that
\textup{NP}^{\textup{SAT}}=\textup{NP}^{\textup{NP}}
. Defining the comlexity class
\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\mathcal{C}}
in terms of the entire class
\mathcal{C}
, rather than a complete problem for
\mathcal{C}
, extends our results to classes
\mathcal{C}
that may not have a complete problem. In particular, we thereby extend our results to structures
\mathcal{R}
of infinite type for which we do not know if
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
has a complete problem.

Within the classical polynomial hierarchy, we define the sequence of complexity classes
\Delta_{k}
as
\Delta_{0}=\textup{P}
, and
\Delta_{k+1}=\textup{P}^{\Sigma_{k}}
. We extend this definition to arbitrary structures
\mathcal{R}
in the next definition.

Definition 93
.

For any structure
\mathcal{R}
, we define a sequence of complexity classes
\Delta_{k}\mathcal{R}
as follows. Define
\Delta_{0}\mathcal{R}=\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})
, and define
\Delta_{k+1}\mathcal{R}=\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}
. Furthermore, define
\delta_{k}\mathcal{R}
as the constant-free Boolean part of
\Delta_{k}\mathcal{R}
. That is,

\delta_{k}\mathcal{R}=\textup{BP}^{0}(\Delta_{k}\mathcal{R}).

As we will see, it is quite fruitful to analyze the polynomial and Boolean hierarchies over
\mathcal{R}
in terms of
\mathcal{R}
-machines with oracles.

Definition 94

(Oracle Complexity Classes)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a complexity class over
\mathcal{R}
. For each of level of the polynomial and Boolean hierarchies over
\mathcal{R}
, the complexity classes
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{D}}
,
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{D}}
,
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{D}}
, and
\forall_{k}\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{D}}
are obtained by modifying the definition of the corresponding class to allow
\mathcal{R}
-machines with oracles from
\mathcal{D}
.

Efficiently Simulating Turing Machines.

We will often want
\mathcal{R}
-machines to implement standard algorithms on binary inputs in a reasonable amount of time.
For example, we claim in Subsection
B.1
that
\mathcal{R}
-machines can implement standard techniques used to parse a context-free grammar in polynomial time, and we claim in Subsection
C.1
that
\mathcal{R}
-machines can perform binary search in logarithmic time.

Both of these claims are justified by our knowledge that the same can be done by Turing machines, and by the fact that
\mathcal{R}
-machines can simulate Turing machines with constant-factor overhead. Intuitively, the contents of an index register may be thought of as the position of one head of a Turing machine with one tape and multiple heads.
We make this analogy precise in Theorem
95
.

Theorem 95
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be any bipointed structure and
\mathcal{T}
be any single-tape Turing machine.
Then there exists an
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
that simulates
\mathcal{T}
with constant-factor overhead.

Proof.

We may assume without loss of generality that the alphabet of
\mathcal{T}
is
\{0,1\}
and that
\mathcal{T}
uses a single one-directional tape.
We describe one for one all possible actions that
\mathcal{T}
can make and how they can be simulated by
M
.

•

Writing a
0
or
1
to the tape of
\mathcal{T}
may be simulated by an
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
using computation instructions that set an
\mathcal{R}
-register to
0
or
1
, respectively.

•

Changing the state of
\mathcal{T}
based on the contents of the cell currently being read by
\mathcal{T}
may be simulated by
M
using branching instructions that check if a register is currently set to
0
or
1
using the equality relation. This may be done with constant-factor overhead.

•

Changing the position of the tape head can be simulated by changing the value of the index registers.

Indeed, the entirety of the transition function of
\mathcal{T}
may be encoded into the program of
M
, and each transition of
\mathcal{T}
may be simulated by
M
in constant time.
∎

Evoking this correspondence is the reason we have used decrement instructions of the form
(7)
instead of the reset instruction of
[
19
]
, which set the contents of an index register to the smallest possible value in one time step. These instruction sets can be simulated by one another, so they are of equivalent power, but the decrement instruction brings the time complexity analysis of
\mathcal{R}
-machines closer to that of Turing machines.

A.8
Metafinite Structures

Metafinite structures may be thought of as finite structures
\mathcal{A}
that are given finite access to a potentially infinite structure
\mathcal{R}
by a finite set
\mathbf{F}
of weight functions
W\colon A^{k}\to R
.
They were first used to generalize Fagin’s theorem from
NP
to
\textup{NP}(\mathbb{R})
in
[
22
]
, and their general theory was developed in
[
23
]
.
Just as
\mathcal{R}
-machines separate the finite and discrete operations of index registers from the potentially infinite and continuous operations of
\mathcal{R}
-registers, metafinite structures separate the finite and discrete aspect of a structure from the potentially infinite and continuous aspects of a structure.
Good examples to hold in mind as we delve into the specifics are graphs with real number weights on the edges, relational databases with a numerical domain that supports arithmetic operations on that domain, or more generally any kind finite structure with numerical attributes.

In order to talk about metafinite structures, we need a notion of metafinite vocabularies. Just as a metafinite structure has a clean separation between a finite structure
\mathcal{A}
and a potentially infinite structure
\mathcal{R}
that interact via a finite set of weight functions
\mathbf{F}
, a metafinite vocabulary will consist of three separate vocabularies.

Definition 96
.

A metafinite vocabulary is a triple
\mathsf{Voc}=(\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{MVoc}),\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{MVoc}),\mathsf{W}(\mathsf{MVoc}))
where

1.

the primary vocabulary
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{MVoc})
is a finite vocabulary,

2.

the secondary vocabulary
\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{MVoc})
is a potentially infinite vocabulary,

3.

the weight vocabulary
\mathsf{W}(\mathsf{MVoc})
is a finite set of function symbols
w
, each with a nonnegative integer
k_{w}\in\mathbb{N}
called the arity of
w
.

We say that
\mathsf{Voc}
is a
metafinite vocabulary over
\mathcal{R}
whenever the secondary vocabulary of
\mathsf{Voc}
is the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
. In line with our assumption about first-order structures, we will assume that the secondary vocabulary of
\mathsf{Voc}
contains two constant symbols
0
and
1
, but we will not assume that the primary vocabulary has these constants. We will also assume that both the primary and secondary vocabularies contain a symbol for equality, although we will continue to omit this symbol when we specify vocabularies.

Definition 97
.

A metafinite structure is a triple
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
where

1.

the primary part of
\mathcal{D}
is a finite structure
\mathcal{A}
in a finite vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{A})
,

2.

the secondary part of
\mathcal{D}
is a potentially infinite structure
\mathcal{R}
in a potentially infinite vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
,

3.

the set of weight functions of
\mathcal{D}
is a finite set
\mathbf{F}
of functions
W\colon A^{k_{W}}\to R
.

We say that
\mathcal{D}
is Boolean if the image of each weight function
W\in\mathbf{F}
is contained in
\{0,1\}
so that
W\colon A^{k_{W}}\to\{0,1\}\subseteq R
.

The
size
of a metafinite structure
\mathcal{D}
, denoted by
|\mathcal{D}|
, is the cardinality of the universe
A
of its primary part
\mathcal{A}
. The
vacabulary
of
\mathcal{D}
is the triple
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{D})=(\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{A}),\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R}),\mathsf{Voc}(W))
, where
\mathsf{Voc}(W)
is a set containing exactly one function symbol of arity
k_{w}
for each
w\in W
. In practice, we will conflate the function
W\colon A^{k_{W}}\to R
with the symbol for
w
. Whenever the secondary part of
\mathcal{D}
is
\mathcal{R}
, then we say that
\mathcal{D}
is an
\mathcal{R}
-structure
. For a given metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
over
\mathcal{R}
, let
\textup{Struct}(\mathcal{R},\mathsf{Voc})
be the class of
\mathcal{R}
-structures in this vocabulary.

Example 98
.

A simple class of metafinite structures is obtained by letting
\mathcal{A}=(V,E)
be a finite graph, letting
\mathcal{R}=\mathbb{R}
be the ordered ring of real numbers, and letting
W
contain a single binary function
w\colon V^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
that assigns
0
to all pairs
(v_{1},v_{2})
that are not related by
E
. The resulting
\mathbb{R}
-structure is a finite graph with edges that have real number weights. Conversely, every graph with real weights can be interpreted as an
\mathbb{R}
-structure.

Example 99
.

Another simple class of
\mathcal{R}
-structures is obtained by letting
\mathcal{A}=(\{1,\dots,n\})
be an initial segment of the positive integers and letting
W
contain a single unary function
X\colon\{1,\dots,n\}\to R
. The resulting
\mathcal{R}
-structure induces the string

(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\in R^{n}
such that
X_{i}=X(i)
.
Conversely, every string
(X_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in R^{n}
induces an
\mathcal{R}
-structure in this same class, where
X
is the unary function
X\colon\{1,\dots,n\}\to R
such that
X(i)=X_{i}.
From this we can see that elements of
R^{*}
are in bijection with
\mathcal{R}
-structures of a certain class.

In order to treat
\mathcal{R}
-structures in any vocabulary as inputs to
\mathcal{R}
-machines, we must encode these structures as strings in
R^{*}
. Any encoding of
\mathcal{R}
-structures as strings imposes a total ordering on the universe of the primary part of these structures.
Conversely, choosing a total ordering on the primary part of
\mathcal{R}
-structures is an essential step towards devising an encoding scheme.

Definition 100
.

For every finite structure
\mathcal{A}
, let
\leq\!(\mathcal{A})
denote a choice of total ordering
\leq
on the universe of
\mathcal{A}
. We say that a finite structure
\mathcal{A}
is ordered if
\leq\!(\mathcal{A})
is a relation of
\mathcal{A}
. Note that this means
\leq
is in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{A}
. If
\leq\!(\mathcal{A})
is not a relation of
\mathcal{A}
, then we say that
\mathcal{A}
is unordered. We say that a metafinite structure
\mathcal{D}
is ordered if its primary part is ordered, and otherwise we say it is unordered.

Remark 101
.

The existence of the choice function
\leq\!(\textup{ })
can be guaranteed either by invoking the axiom of global choice or by restricting our attention to metafinite structures whose primary part has a universe that is inherently ordered, as is the case with initial segments of
\mathbb{N}
. The only difference between ordered and unordered structures is that ordered structures have the chosen order on their universe in their vocabulary.

Given our choice of total ordering
\leq
on
\mathcal{A}
, a weight function
W\colon A^{k}\to R
of an
\mathcal{R}
-structure
\mathcal{D}
may be encoded as a string
\textup{code}(w)=(r_{1},\dots,r_{m})\in R^{m}
where
m=|A^{k}|
and
r_{i}
is the value of
w
on the
i
th tuple of
A^{k}
under the lexicographic ordering on
A^{k}
induced
\leq
. Using this encoding of weight functions of
\mathcal{D}
, we devise a method of encoding the entirety of
\mathcal{D}
by pushing all of the constants, functions, and relations in the primary part of
\mathcal{D}
into the set of weight functions of
\mathcal{D}
, thereby converting
\mathcal{D}
into a metafinite algebra.

Definition 102
.

A metafinite algebra is a metafinite structure in which the primary part
\mathcal{A}
has an empty vocabulary. In other words,
\mathcal{A}
is just a set
A
without any constants, functions, or relations.

Every
\mathcal{R}
-structure
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
may be converted into a metafinite algebra in the following way. First, transform each constant and function of
\mathcal{A}
into a relation by viewing a constant as a
0
-ary function and recalling that a function, as a set of ordered pairs, is simply a functional relation. Then encode each relation
P\subseteq A^{k}
of
\mathcal{A}
by its characteristic function
\chi_{P}\colon A^{k}\to\{0,1\}
before adding
\chi_{P}
to
W
and removing the constant, function, or relation corresponding to
\chi_{P}
from the primary part of
\mathcal{D}
. The result is a metafinite algebra
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F}^{\prime})
over
\mathcal{R}
that contains the same information as
\mathcal{D}
.

Definition 103
.

The encoding of an
\mathcal{R}
-structure
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
is the string
\textup{code}(\mathcal{D})\in R^{*}
obtained in the following way. First convert
\mathcal{D}
into a metafinite algebra
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F}^{\prime})
with
\mathbf{F}^{\prime}=\{W_{1},\dots,W_{N}\}
. Then let
\textup{code}(\mathcal{D})=(\textup{code}(W_{1}),\dots,\textup{code}(W_{n}))\in R^{*}
.

We do not include the code for the equality relation in the code for a metafinite structure, even though we assume it is always a relation of the primary vocabulary. This means that every string
X\in R^{*}
is of the form
\textup{code}(\mathcal{D})
for some
\mathcal{R}
-structure
\mathcal{D}
from the class described in Example
99
. Thus, every subset of
R^{*}
is a set of metafinite structures in a fixed vocabulary, allowing us to recast decision problems in these terms without loss of generality.

Definition 104
.

A decision problem of
\mathcal{R}
-structures in vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
is a subset
S\subseteq\textup{Struct}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathsf{Voc})
. If
\mathcal{C}
is a complexity class over
\mathcal{R}
then we say that
S
belongs to
\mathcal{C}
if
\{\textup{code}(\mathcal{D}):\mathcal{D}\in S\}
is in
\mathcal{C}
.

This equivalence between decision problems over
\mathcal{R}
and decision problems of
\mathcal{R}
-structures allows us to rephrase the basic notions of complexity theory over
\mathcal{R}
in terms of
\mathcal{R}
-structures.
In particular, we may view the complexity class
\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})
as the class of all decision problems of
\mathcal{R}
-structures
S
in any vocabulary such that membership in
S
may be tested in polynomial time.

When rephrasing the definition of
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
in terms of
\mathcal{R}
-structures, we replace witness strings
Y\in R^{n}
with witness weight functions
Y\colon A^{k}\to R
, where
|A^{k}|=n
. Indeed, relative to a choice of ordering on
A
, we may view the function
Y
as the string
Y
, and vice versa, under our encoding scheme. Defining the size
|Y|
of the function
Y
as the cardinality of its domain
A^{k}
, we see that
|Y|
is the length of
Y
when
Y
is viewed as a string.

The expansion of an
\mathcal{R}
-structure
\mathcal{D}
with the weight function
Y
is denoted by
(\mathcal{D},Y)
. Note that
\textup{code}((\mathcal{D},Y))=\textup{code}(\mathcal{D})\textup{code}(Y)
, so expanding
\mathcal{D}
with
Y
corresponds to concatenation. Given this translation, we say that a decision problem of
\mathcal{R}
-structures
L
in vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
is in
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
if and only if there is a positive integer
q
and a decision problem of
\mathcal{R}
-structures
S
in vocabulary
(\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{MVoc}),\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{MVoc}),\mathsf{W}(\mathsf{MVoc})\cup\{Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m}\})
such that
S\in\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})
and

L=\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{Struct}(\mathsf{MVoc}):(\exists W\colon A^{q}\to R)\ (\mathcal{D},W)\in S\}.

We may characterize decision problems of
\mathcal{R}
-structures in
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
in the same way, with an alternating string of quantified witness weight functions.

A.9
Metafinite Logic

Fagin’s theorem shows us that existential second-order logic captures the complexity class
NP
over finite structures
[
15
]
[
35
]
. It does this by first viewing the class
NP
as a class of decision problems of finite structures, and then showing that a set
L
of finite structures is in
NP
if and only if there is a sentence
\varphi_{L}
of existential second-order logic such that
L=\{\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi_{L}\}
.

The essential idea in the proof of Fagin’s theorem is that an accepting computation sequence of a Turing machine can be described in first-order logic with an appropriate relational vocabulary. Passing to existential second-order logic allows us to existentially quantify over these relations, thereby removing the requirement that they are in our vocabulary, as well as to express the existence of a witness
Y
for the membership of a finite structure
\mathcal{A}
in a given decision problem
L
.

Gr’́adel and Meer established an analogous result in
[
22
]
by showing that existential second-order logic over
\mathbb{R}
captures
\textup{NP}(\mathbb{R})
over
\mathbb{R}
-structures. The essential idea in their proof is similar to the essential idea of Fagin’s theorem: The existence of a witness and an accepting computation sequence of an
\mathbb{R}
-machine can be expressed in a sentence of existential second-order logic over
\mathbb{R}
.

Just as
\mathbb{R}
-machines have two sorts of registers—index registers and
\mathbb{R}
-registers—the logic that Gr’́adel and Meer used is a many-sorted second-order logic with two sorts—a sort of point terms to describe the index registers, and a sort of weight terms to describe the
\mathbb{R}
-registers. Such a logic requires structures with a domain to interpret each sort of term, hence the invention of
\mathbb{R}
-structures wherein the primary part interprets the point terms and the secondary part interprets the weight terms. The requirement that the primary part of an
\mathbb{R}
-structure is finite reflects both the fact that input strings to
\mathbb{R}
-machines are finite, as well as the fact that terminating
\mathbb{R}
-machine computations are finite.

Metafinite logic was developed by Gr’́adel and Gurevich in
[
23
]
as a generalization of the logic of Gr’́adel and Meer that allows us to replace the structure
\mathbb{R}
with any other structure
\mathcal{R}
. It is a restriction of many-sorted second-order logic that is tailored to express properties of metafinite structures, as we shall see. For a given metafinite structure
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
, we have two domains,
A
and
R
, and so we have two sorts of terms—a sort of point terms, which refer to elements of the primary part
A
, and a sort of weight terms, which refer to elements of the secondary part
R
.

Definition 105

(First-Order Terms)
.

Fix a set
\mathsf{Var}=\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\dots\}
of first-order variables that range only over the primary part of a metafinite structure. The first-order terms of a metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}=(\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{MVoc}),\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{MVoc}),\mathsf{W}(\mathsf{MVoc}))
are as follows:

1.

The set of point terms is the closure of
\mathsf{Var}
and the constants of
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{MVoc})
under applications of functions from
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{MVoc})
. In other words, point terms are all the terms in the primary vocabulary.

2.

Every constant of
\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{MVoc})
is a weight term, but note that elements of
\mathsf{Var}
are not weight terms.

3.

If
p_{1},\dots,p_{k}
are point terms and
w
is a
k
-ary function symbol of
\mathsf{W}(\mathsf{MVoc})
, then
w(p_{1},\dots,p_{k})
is a weight term. In other words, applying a weight function to point terms results in a weight term.

4.

If
t_{1},\dots t_{k}
are weight terms and
f
is a
k
-ary function symbol of
\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{MVoc})
, then
f(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
is a weight term. In other words, weight terms are closed under the application of functions in the secondary vocabulary.

Suppose
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
is a metafinite structure with vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{D})=(\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{A}),\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R}),\mathsf{Voc}(W))
. For a given point term
p
, if the set of variables occurring in
p
is a subset of
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
, then
p
induces a function
p:A^{n}\to A
. Similarly, for a given weight term
t
, if the set of variables occurring in
t
is a subset of
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
, then
t
induces a function
t:A^{n}\to R
. For any tuple
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in A^{n}
,
p(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
is computed by replacing each variable
x_{i}
of
p
with
a_{i}
and recursively applying the functions of
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{A})
that occur in
p
, while
t(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
is computed by recursively applying the functions of
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{A})
,
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
, and
W
that occur in
t
.

Definition 106

(First-Order Formulas)
.

First-order atomic formulas in vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
are of the form
P(p_{1},\dots,p_{k})
or
Q(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
, where
P
and
Q
are relation symbols of arity
k
from
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{MVoc})
and
\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{MVoc})
, respectively, and where
p_{1},\dots,p_{k}
are point terms and
t_{1},\dots,t_{k}
are weight terms. The set
\textup{FO}(\mathsf{MVoc})
of first-order formulas in vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
is the closure of the set of first-order atomic formulas in vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
under Boolean operations
\{\neg,\vee,\wedge\}
and quantification
\{\forall,\exists\}
over first-order variables. The set
\textup{FO}(\mathcal{R})
of first-order formulas over
\mathcal{R}
is the union of all
\textup{FO}(\mathsf{Voc})
such that
\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{MVoc})=\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R}).
That is,

\textup{FO}(\mathcal{R})=\bigcup\{\textup{FO}(\mathsf{Voc}):\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{MVoc})=\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})\}.

Recall that a first-order variable ranges only over the universe
A
of the primary part
\mathcal{A}
of a metafinite structure
\mathcal{D}
. This accords with our usual understanding of first-order logic on the structure
\mathcal{A}
. Second-order logic on the structure
\mathcal{A}
also allows quantification over
k
-ary relations on the universe of
\mathcal{A}
, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, allowing us to create formulas stating, for example, that there is a binary relation on
A
that is a linear ordering on
A
. Since each
k
-ary relation
P
on
A
may be encoded by its characteristic function
\chi_{R}\colon A^{k}\to\{0,1\}
and added to the set of weight functions for a metafinite structure with primary part
\mathcal{A}
, we can see that allowing quantification over the set
W
subsumes second-order logic over the structure
\mathcal{A}
.

By allowing quantification over weight functions, second-order metafinite logic extends first-order metafinite logic. In order to allow quantification over weight functions of arity
k
, we introduce second-order variables of arity
k
, for each nonnegative integer
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Definition 107

(Second-Order Terms)
.

For each nonnegative integer
k
, fix a set
\mathsf{Var}_{k}=\{X_{1}^{k},X_{2}^{k},X_{3}^{k},\dots\}
second-order variables of arity
k
that range over the
k
-ary weight functions of a metafinite structure. The second-order terms of a metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
include all first-order terms of
\mathsf{Voc}
. In addition, if
t_{1},\dots,t_{k}
are second-order weight terms and
X^{k}
is a second-order variable of arity
k
, then
X^{k}(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
is a weight term.

For a given second-order weight term
t
in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{D}
, if the set of second-order variables occurring in
t
is a subset of
\{X_{1}^{k_{1}},\dots,X_{m}^{k_{m}}\}
, then any tuple
(w_{1},\dots,w_{m})
of weight functions of the appropriate arity induce a first-order term
t(w_{1},\dots,w_{m})
in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{D}
expanded with symbols for each of the weight functions, which in turn induces a function
t(W_{1},\dots,W_{N})\colon A^{n}\to A
. We sometimes write
t(W_{1},\dots,W_{m},a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
for
t(W_{1},\dots,W_{m})(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
.

Definition 108

(Second-Order Formulas)
.

Second-order atomic formulas in vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
are defined just as first-order atomic formulas are defined, except weight terms are now allowed to be second order. The set of second-order formulas in vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
is the closure of the set of second-order atomic formulas in vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
under Boolean operations
\{\neg,\vee,\wedge\}
and quantification
\{\forall,\exists\}
over both first-order and second-order variables. The set
\textup{SO}(\mathsf{Voc})
of second-order formulas in vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
is the closure of the set of second-order atomic formulas in vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
under Boolean operations
\{\neg,\vee,\wedge\}
and quantification
\{\forall,\exists\}
over first-order variables. The set
\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
of first-order formulas over
\mathcal{R}
is the union of all
\textup{SO}(\mathsf{Voc})
such that
\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{MVoc})=\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R}).
That is,

\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})=\bigcup\{\textup{SO}(\mathsf{Voc}):\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{MVoc})=\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})\}

Remark 109
.

Note that a second-order variable of arity
0
ranges over weight functions
r\colon A^{0}\to R
, which are essentially the same as elements of
R
. This means that quantifying over second-order variables subsumes quantifying over the secondary part of metafinite structures. It is essential to Lemma
129
that first-order metafinite logic only allows quantification over the primary part of metafinite structures. The set

A variable of
\varphi
is
free
if it is not bound by a quantifier. We write
\varphi(X_{1}^{k_{1}},\dots,X_{m}^{k_{m}},x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
to emphasize the the free variables of
\varphi
are a subset of
\{X_{1}^{k_{1}},\dots,X_{m}^{k_{m}},x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
, but we may still simply write
\varphi
even if
\varphi
has free variables. If
\varphi
has no free variables, then
\varphi
is a
sentence
.

Definition 110
.

Let
\varphi
be a second-order formula in the vocabulary of a metafinite structure
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
such that the free variables of
\varphi
are a subset of
\{X_{1}^{k_{1}},\dots,X_{m}^{k_{m}},x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
. Given weight functions
W_{1},\dots,W_{m}\in W
of the appropriate arity, and elements
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
, the satisfaction relation
\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi(W_{1},\dots,W_{N},a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
is defined recursively as follows.

1.

If
\varphi
is the atomic formula
P(p_{1},\dots,p_{k})
, then
\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi(W_{1},\dots,W_{m},a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
if and only if

(p_{1}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n}),\dots,p_{k}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n}))\in P^{\mathcal{A}}.

2.

If
\varphi
is the atomic formula
Q(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
then
\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi(W_{1},\dots,W_{m},a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
if and only if

(t_{1}(W_{1},\dots,W_{m},a_{1},\dots,a_{n}),\dots,t_{k}(W_{1},\dots,W_{m},a_{1},\dots,a_{n}))\in Q^{\mathcal{R}}.

3.

If
\varphi
is a Boolean combination of
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
, then the satisfaction relation is defined in the usual way. For example, if
\varphi
is
\neg\psi
, then
\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi(W_{1},\dots,W_{m},a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
if and only if

\mathcal{D}\not\vDash\psi(W_{1},\dots,W_{m},a_{1},\dots,a_{n}).

4.

If
\varphi
results from quantifying a first-order variable in
\psi
, then the satisfaction relation is defined by quantifying of
A
. For example, if
\varphi
is
\forall x_{n+1}\psi
, then
\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi(W_{1},\dots,W_{m},a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
if and only if

\mathcal{D}\vDash\psi(W_{1},\dots,W_{m},a_{1},\dots,a_{n},a)\text{ for all }a\in A.

5.

If
\varphi
results from quantifying a second-order variable of arity
k
in
\psi
, then the satisfaction relation is defined by quantifying over weight functions of arity
k
in
W
. For example, if
\varphi
is
\forall X_{m+1}^{k}\psi
, then
\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi(W_{1},\dots,W_{m},a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
if and only if

\mathcal{D}\vDash\psi(W_{1},\dots,W_{m},W_{m+1},a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\textup{ for all }W_{m+1}\colon A^{k}\to R.

Definition 111
.

Two formulas
\varphi
and
\psi
are logically equivalent whenever they are satisfied by exactly the same structures. That is
\varphi
is logically equivalent to
\psi
whenever, for all structures
\mathcal{A}
,

\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi\text{ if and only if }\mathcal{D}\vDash\psi.

Definition 112
.

A formula
\varphi
is in prenex normal form if it is of the form

Q_{1}X_{1}^{k_{1}}\dots Q_{N}X_{N}^{k_{N}}Q_{1}x_{1}\dots Q_{n}x_{n}\psi,

where
Q_{i}
are quantifiers,
X_{i}^{k_{i}}
and
x_{j}
are second and first-order variables, respectively, and
\psi
is quantifier free.

The inductive proof that every second-order formula
\varphi
is semantically equivalent to some second-order formula
\varphi^{\prime}
in prenex normal form is essentially equivalent to the proof of the analogous result for first-order logic, except that the induction uses two new conversion rules that can be found in
[
35
]
on page 115.

Theorem 113
.

For every formula
\varphi
there is a formula
\varphi^{\prime}
in prenex normal form such that
\varphi
is semantically equivalent to
\varphi^{\prime}
. ∎

Definition 114

(Second-Order Fragments)
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
k
th existential fragment of
\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
is the set
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
of all sentences in
\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
of the form

Q_{1,1}X_{1,1}\dots Q_{1,m_{1}}X_{1,m_{1}}\dots Q_{N,1}X_{N,1}\dots Q_{N,m_{N}}X_{N,m_{N}}Q_{1}x_{1}\dots Q_{n}x_{n}\psi,

where the quantifier blocks
Q_{i}=(Q_{i,1},\dots,Q_{i,m_{i}})
are all the same quantifier, starting with an existential block
Q_{1}
and alternating until reaching the prenex normal form first-order formula
Q_{1}x_{1}\dots Q_{n}x_{n}\psi.
In particular
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})=\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
is existential second-order logic over
\mathcal{R}
. The
k
th universal fragment of
\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
is the set
\Pi_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
that is defined by the same condition, except the first quantifier block is universal. In particular
\Pi\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})=\Pi_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R}).

Definition 115

(Second-Order Boolean Fragments)
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
k
th existential Boolean fragment of
\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
is the set
\exists_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
of all sentences
\varphi
in
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
such that the only constants from
\mathcal{R}
occurring in
\varphi
are 0 and 1. In particular
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})=\exists_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R}).
The
k
th universal Boolean fragment of
\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
is the set
\forall_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
of all sentences
\varphi
whose only constants from
\mathcal{R}
are
0
and
1
. In particular
\forall\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})=\forall_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R}).

Note that we are justified in calling these fragments Boolean because they may be encoded as Boolean strings and tested with Turing machines.

A.10
Describing Metafinite Structures

The
model-checking problem
asks for a given sentence
\varphi
in given logic
\mathcal{L}
and a given structure
\mathcal{A}
, does
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi
? It is via this problem that we will relate logic and computational complexity.

Definition 116
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a complexity class and
\mathcal{L}
be a logic. The data complexity of
\mathcal{L}
is
\mathcal{K}
if for every sentence
\varphi
of
\mathcal{L}
,

\{\textup{code}(\mathcal{D}):\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi\}\in\mathcal{K}.

Definition 117

(Logics Capturing Complexity Classes)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a complexity class,
\mathcal{L}
be a logic, and
\mathcal{C}
be a class of metafinite structures. We say that
\mathcal{L}
captures
\mathcal{K}
on
\mathsf{C}
if the following two things hold:

1.

For all sentences
\varphi
of
\mathcal{L},
the decision problem
\{\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{C}:\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi\}
is in
\mathcal{K}.

2.

For every property
\mathcal{P}
of structures from
\mathsf{C}
that can be tested with complexity
\mathcal{K}
, there is a sentence
\varphi_{\mathcal{P}}
of
\mathcal{L}
such that
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi_{\mathcal{P}}
if and only if
\mathcal{A}
has property
\mathcal{P}
, for each
\mathcal{A}\in\mathsf{C}
.

Appendix B
Cook-Levin Theorem Over a Structure

In this section, under weak assumptions on the structure
\mathcal{R}
, we will show that
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})=\Sigma_{1}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-complete, and we will generalize this result to the higher levels of the polynomial hierarchy over
\mathcal{R}
. Specifically, we will show that
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is complete for
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
, and
\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is complete for
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
under polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine reductions, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq 1
. Moreover, we will show that
\exists_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is complete for
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}
and
\forall_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is complete for
\forall_{k}\mathcal{R}
under polynomial-time Turing machine reductions, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq 1
.
We assume throughout this section that
\mathcal{R}
is a bipointed structure.

B.1
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is in
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})

In order to show that
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is in
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
, it is sufficient to show that given a quantifier-free formula
\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
and a tuple
(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})\in R^{n}
, we can decide whether or not
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
using an
\mathcal{R}
-machine that runs in time polynomial in the size of
\varphi
. Given the recursive construction of
\varphi
, the recursive algorithm
evaluate
naturally suggests itself as a decision procedure.

Definition 118
.

The algorithm
evaluate
takes two inputs: a quantifier-free formula
\varphi
in
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
, and a tuple
\overline{r}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})\in R^{n}
such that
n
is greater than or equal to the index of any variable occurring in
\varphi
. The algorithm then recursively decides if
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi(\overline{r})
as follows

1.

if
\varphi
is the atomic formula
t_{1}=t_{2}
, then
evaluate
returns 1 if
t_{1}^{\mathcal{R}}(\overline{r})=t_{2}^{\mathcal{R}}(\overline{r})
, and returns 0 otherwise,

2.

If
\varphi
is the atomic formula
P(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
, then
evaluate
returns 1 if
(t_{1}^{\mathcal{R}}(\overline{r}),\dots,t_{k}^{\mathcal{R}}(\overline{r}))\in P^{\mathcal{R}}
, and returns 0 otherwise.

This is the base case of the algorithm. There are three recursive cases corresponding to the Boolean connectives
\{\neg,\wedge,\vee\}
that accord with the meaning of these connectives:

3.

if
\varphi
is
\neg\psi
, then
evaluate
returns 1 if
\textup{{evaluate}}(\psi,\overline{r})=0
, and returns 0 otherwise,

4.

if
\varphi
is
\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}
, then
evaluate
returns 1 if
\textup{{evaluate}}(\psi_{1},\overline{r})=1
and
\textup{{evaluate}}(\psi_{2},\overline{r})=1
, and returns 0 otherwise,

5.

if
\varphi
is
\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2}
, then
evaluate
returns 1 if
\textup{{evaluate}}(\psi_{1},\overline{r})=1
or
\textup{{evaluate}}(\psi_{2},\overline{r})=1
, and returns 0 otherwise.

We argue that whenever
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
is of finite type, then there is an
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
implementing
evaluate
and that
M
runs in polynomial time.
In order for
M
to implement
evaluate
,
M
needs to do three things:

1.

M
needs to parse encodings of formulas in order to both (
i
) determine if a string is a valid encoding of some formula and (
ii
) convert the encoding of that formula into a syntax tree for that formula in order to determine which case of
evaluate
to apply to this formula. For example, if
\varphi
is
\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2}
, then
M
needs to be able to use some (yet to be specified) definition of
\textup{code}(\varphi)\in R^{*}
to determine that
\vee
was the last connective applied in the construction of
\varphi
so that it can apply the
\vee
case of
evaluate
to
\varphi
.

2.

M
needs to implement recursion in order to recursively apply
evaluate
in the cases where
\varphi
is not an atomic formula.

3.

M
needs to determine whether or not
\mathcal{R}
satisfies atomic formulas
t_{1}=t_{2}
and
P(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
when the free variables of this formula have been given values according to
(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
.

Programming
M
to accomplish (1) and (2) may be thought of as compiling
evaluate
into the machine language of
\mathcal{R}
-programs. It is plausible that
\mathcal{R}
-machines should be able to accomplish (3) because they are able to execute equality tests and tests on relations
P
in
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
, however, there are infinite type vocabularies for which this is impossible. Examples include the uncountably infinite vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}})
for the structure
\mathbb{R}_{\textup{lin}}=(\mathbb{R},0,1,-,+,(\mu_{r}:r\in\mathbb{R}))
and the countably infinite vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathbb{N}_{\textup{sc}})
for the structure
\mathbb{N}_{\textup{sc}}=(\mathbb{N},0,1,(\mu_{n}:n\in\text{Prime}\subseteq\mathbb{N}))
.

This follows from results of Hemmerling in

[
26
,
27
]
, which show that if
\mathcal{R}
is one of these structures, then it is impossible to encode the symbols of
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
into strings in
R^{*}
in such a way that an
\mathcal{R}
-machine can extract the function or relation of
\mathcal{R}
from its code. In other words, there is no
effective encoding
of
\mathcal{R}
. This implies that it is also impossible to encode formulas of
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
into strings in
R^{*}
in such a way that an
\mathcal{R}
-machine can evaluate, creating an obstacle to accomplishing (3).

As a consequence, it is unlikely that
\textup{SAT}(\mathbb{N}_{\text{sc}})
is in
\textup{NP}(\mathbb{N}_{\text{sc}})
simply because there is no way to effectively encode
\textup{SAT}(\mathbb{N}_{\text{sc}})
as strings in
\mathbb{N}^{*}
. We know even more in the case of
\mathbb{N}_{\text{sc}}
because a result of Gaßner implies that there is no complete problem for
\textup{NP}(\mathbb{N}_{\textup{sc}})

[
18
]
.

However, Hemmerling also establishes in
[
26
]
that whenever
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
is of finite type, then
\mathcal{R}
is effectively encodable. This is true even when
\mathcal{R}
has uncountably many constants because the only operation associated to a constant is writing that constant, and thus a constant may stand as a code for itself. We use this result to devise an encoding of formulas of
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
of finite type such that an
\mathcal{R}
-machine can parse these encodings in polynomial time.

First we enumerate the function symbols
\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{R})=\{f_{1},\dots,f_{|\mathsf{Fun}|}\}
and relation symbols
\mathsf{Rel}(\mathcal{R})=\{P_{1},\dots,P_{|\mathsf{Rel}|}\}
of
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
. The grammar of first-order logic is context sensitive because function and relation symbols have a specified arity. Despite this, we may encode first-order logic in a context-free grammar by including the arity of function and relation symbols as an explicit part of their codes, as described in Exercise 5.2.8 of
[
17
]
, where we replace the unary encoding of arity with a binary encoding.

The result is a context-free grammar
that can be parsed in polynomial time by a Turing machine
[
52
]
. Since
\mathcal{R}
-machines can simulate Turing machines in polynomial time by Theorem
95
, this grammar can also be parsed by an
\mathcal{R}
-machine in polynomial time. Furthermore, assuming that
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
is of finite type implies that
\mathcal{R}
is effectively encodable, so an
\mathcal{R}
-machine can extract the functions and relations occurring in
\varphi
in constant time after it parses the grammatical structure of the encoding of
\varphi
in polynomial time. Thus, whenever
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
is of finite type, we can encode formulas in
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
in such a way that they can be parsed in polynomial time by an
\mathcal{R}
-machine.

Definition 119
.

For any bipointed structure of finite type
\mathcal{R}
, we fix an encoding of
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
-formulas
\varphi
, denoted by
\textup{code}(\varphi)\in R^{*}
, such that
\textup{code}(\varphi)
can be parsed by an
\mathcal{R}
-machine in time polynomial in the length of
\textup{code}(\varphi)
, which we denote as
|\textup{code}(\varphi)|
or
|\varphi|
. We assume that
|\varphi|
is polynomial in the maximum of the number of symbols occurring in
\varphi
and the greatest index of any symbol occurring in
\varphi
. Furthermore, we assume that the code of each function and relation of
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
, as well as that of each variable, connective, and quantifier, is a Boolean string in
\{0,1\}^{*}
.

Remark 120
.

Our encoding is such that
\textup{code}(\varphi)\in\{0,1\}^{*}\subseteq R^{*}
if and only if the only constants occurring in
\varphi
are 0 and 1.

Using this encoding, we can program an
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
to (1) parse encodings of formulas in polynomial time. We may program
M
to (2) recursively apply
evaluate
by implementing recursion using standard techniques, such as looping back to an earlier step in the program of
M
via branching instructions. In order to (3) compute the base case of
evaluate
for an atomic formula
t_{1}=t_{2}
or
P(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
and a tuple
(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
,
M
can first compute the values
t_{i}^{\mathcal{R}}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
by writing the constant symbols occurring in
t_{i}
before recursively applying the functions occurring in
t_{i}
to these constants and values from
(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
. The machine
M
is able to do because each of these constants and functions is an operation of
\mathcal{R}
that
M
can execute via
\mathcal{R}
-instructions. Similarly,
M
is able to determine if
t_{1}^{\mathcal{R}}(\overline{r})=t_{2}^{\mathcal{R}}(\overline{r})
or if
(t^{\mathcal{R}}_{1}(\overline{r}),\dots,t^{\mathcal{R}}_{k}(\overline{r}))\in P^{\mathcal{R}}
because
M
can test equality and any relation of
\mathcal{R}
. All that remains to be seen is that
M
runs in polynomial time.

Lemma 121
.

If
\mathcal{R}
is a bipointed structure of finite type, then there is an
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
that implements the algorithm
evaluate
and runs in time polynomial in
|\varphi|
on any input
(\varphi,(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}))
, where
\varphi
is a quantifier-free
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
-formula, and
(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})\in R^{n}
.

Proof.

Suppose
M
receives input
(\varphi,(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}))
. As
M
parses
\varphi
in polynomial time,
M
can verify that the largest index of a variable occurring in
\varphi
is at most
n
, returning 0 if this is not the case. If
\varphi
is the atomic formula
t_{1}=t_{2}
, it suffices to show that
M
can compute the values
t_{i}^{\mathcal{R}}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})\in R
in time polynomial in
|t_{1}=t_{2}|
because, given these values,
M
can test if they are equal in constant time. A similar argument applies in the case that
\varphi
is the atomic formula
P(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
, so we proceed to show that
M
can compute
t_{i}^{\mathcal{R}}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
in time polynomial in
|\varphi|
, for any term
t_{i}
occurring in any formula
\varphi
.

Each constant occurring in a term
t_{i}
is included in
\textup{code}(t_{i})
, which is part of the input
(\textup{code}(\varphi),(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}))
. As they are part of the input,
M
does not need to write these constants. Similarly, the codes of the functions occurring in
t_{i}
are part of the input, and these codes can be extracted from the input in polynomial time because we have encoded
\varphi
in a context-free grammar, which may be parsed in polynomial time by a Turing machine that
M
can simulate.

By our assumption that
\mathcal{R}
is of finite type, there is a constant-time effective encoding of
\mathcal{R}
, so the codes of the functions occurring in
t_{i}
may be used to apply these functions to the constants occurring in
t_{i}
as well as the values from
(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
. Thus,
M
can compute
t_{i}^{\mathcal{R}}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
in time polynomial in
|t_{i}|\leq|\varphi|
, for any formula
\varphi
. If
\varphi
is an atomic formula, then the above implies that
M
can decide in polynomial time if
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
.

If
\varphi
is not atomic, then
M
will recursively call itself on inputs of the form
(\psi,(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}))
, where
\psi
is a subformula of
\varphi
. To see that
M
runs in polynomial time when
\varphi
is not atomic, it suffices to show that the number of recursive calls that
M
makes to itself is polynomial in
|\varphi|
. This is the case because each recursive call that
M
makes corresponds to a Boolean connective from
\{\neg,\wedge,\vee\}
occurring in
\varphi
.
∎

Theorem 122
.

If
\mathcal{R}
is of finite type, then
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is in
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
.

Proof.

Let
S
be the set of pairs
(\exists\overline{x}\varphi(\overline{x}),\overline{r})
such that
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi(\overline{r})
, where
\varphi(\overline{x})
is a quantifier-free
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
-formula, and
\overline{r}\in R^{n}
and
n=|\exists\overline{x}\varphi(\overline{x})
. By Lemma
121
,
S\in\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})
. Furthermore, the length of any reasonable encoding of
(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
will be at most linear in
|\exists x_{1}\dots\exists x_{n}\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})|
. Given the fact that
\exists x_{1}\dots\exists x_{n}\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
if and only if there is some
(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})\in R^{n}
such that
(\exists x_{1}\dots\exists x_{n}\varphi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}),(r_{1},\dots,r_{n}))\in S
, we can see that
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
.
∎

B.2
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-hard

In what follows, we prove in Theorem
123
that whenever
\mathcal{R}
is a bipointed structure of finite type that has all constants,
then
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-hard. For such structures, it is also the case that
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is in
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
, as established in Theorem
122
. Together, these results imply that for such structures,
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-complete, as expressed in Theorem
14
.

The result of Theorem
14
is similar to results in
[
5
]
and
[
38
]
but it is more general because it applies to structures other than rings. A similar result is established in
[
21
]
for the same class of structures that Theorem
14
applies to, but the proof therein uses polynomial-time constructible circuit families. The proof we give here has the advantage that it uses machines in a way that is quite close to the proof of the Boolean Cook-Levin theorem, as in
[
38
]
, and it applies to all (bipointed) structures of finite type that have all constants, as in
[
21
]
.

Theorem 123
.

If
\mathcal{R}
is of finite type and has all constants, then
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-hard.

Proof.

Fix some
L\in\text{NP}(\mathcal{R})
. By definition, there is some polynomial
q
and some polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
such that

L=\{v\in R^{*}:(\exists w\in R^{q(|v|)})\ M(v,w)=1\}.

Let
n=|v|
be the length of
v
, and let
m
be an integer such that
M
halts in fewer than
n^{m}
steps on every input
(v,w)
such that
|w|=q(|v|)
. Such an
m
exists because there is a polynomial
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
M
runs in time
p
, so on inputs of the form
(v,w)
,
M
will run in time
p(2(|v|+|w|))=p(2(n+q(n)))
, which is again polynomial in
n
.

On input
(v,w)
, the zeroth index register is initialized with value
|(v,w)|
, while all other index register are initialized with 0. Because
M
can only increment the contents of index register by one with every time step, the largest possible value of any index register during the computation of
M
on input
(v,w)
is
|(v,w)|+n^{m}
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
m
is large enough so that
n^{m}
is greater than the largest value of the index registers during a computation of
M
on any input
(v,w)
of the above form.

We build a quantifier-free formula
\varphi_{v}
in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
such that
\varphi_{v}
is satisfiable in
\mathcal{R}
if and only if
v\in L
. The formula
\varphi_{v}
will encode the sequence of configurations of
M
induced by any input of the form
(v,w)
. Recall that a configuration of
M
is a tuple
(\ell,\overline{n},\overline{r})\in\{0,\dots,\ell_{\mathcal{P}}\}\times\mathbb{N}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}}\times R^{\mathbb{N}}
. Since
M
uses at most
n^{m}

\mathcal{R}
-registers, a configuration of
M
is uniquely determined by a tuple

(\ell,\overline{n},\overline{r})\in\{0,\dots,\ell_{\mathcal{P}}\}\times\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}}\times R^{n^{m}}.

To encode the configuration
(\ell,\overline{n},\overline{r})
in a subformula of
\varphi_{v}
, we will use atomic subformulas of the form
S_{p,t}=r
,
I_{j,p,t}=r
, or
Z_{i,t}=r
where
S_{p,t},

I_{j,p,t}
, and
Z_{i,t}
are variables, and
r
is an element of
R
.

The structure
\mathcal{R}
may not contain a copy of the integers, but because we always assume that
\mathcal{R}
is bipointed we know
\mathcal{R}
has two constants
0
and
1
, so we may encode the possible contents
\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}
of the index registers as binary strings
b_{1}\dots b_{\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1}
of length
\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1
. We use
\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor
variables
\{I_{j,1,t},\dots,I_{j,\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1,t}\}
to encode the value of
j
th index register at time
t
. If the value of the
j
th index register is
n_{j}
at time
t
, and if the binary representation of
n_{j}
is
b_{1}\dots b_{\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1}
, then we encode this as

\textup{{index}}_{j}(n_{j},t)\equiv\bigwedge_{p=1}^{\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1}I_{j,p,t}=b_{p},

which is a formula in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
because
\mathcal{R}
has
0
and
1
as constants. Similarly, if
M
executes instruction
\ell
at time
t
, and if the binary representation of
\ell
is
b_{1}\dots b_{\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1}
, then we encode this as

\texttt{instruction}(\ell,t)\equiv\bigwedge_{p=1}^{\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1}S_{p,t}=b_{p}.

In contrast to the case for the contents of the index registers and the instruction, we may encode the fact that the
i
th
\mathcal{R}
-register of
M
contains
r
at time
t
using a single variable, as

Z_{i,t}=r.

In this way, variables encode the following information:

•

\{S_{p,t}:1\leq p\leq\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1\}
encode the instruction
\ell
at time
t
,

•

\{I_{j,p,t}:0\leq j\leq k_{\mathcal{P}}-1\text{ and }1\leq p\leq\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1\}
encode the contents of the
k_{\mathcal{P}}
index registers at time
t
,

•

\{Z_{i,t}:0\leq i\leq n^{m}-1\}
encode the contents of the
n^{m}

\mathcal{R}
-registers of
M
at time
t
.

The formula
\varphi_{v}
will be a conjunction of three subformulas,
\varphi_{v}\equiv\textup{{start}}_{v}\land\textup{{update}}\land\textup{{accept}}
. The subformula
\textup{{start}}_{v}
is itself the conjunction of subformulas that partially encode the starting configuration of
M
on any input
(v,w)
. Recall that we encode pairs
(v,w)
as strings of the form
(0,v_{1},0,\dots,0,v_{n},1,w_{1},0,\dots,0,w_{q(n)})
, so the initial configuration of
M
on input
(v,w)
is the tuple

\mathsf{conf}_{0}((v,w))=(0,(2n+2q(n),0,\dots,0),(0,v_{1},0,\dots,0,v_{n},1,w_{1},0,\dots,0,w_{q(n)},0,\dots,0)).

The first entry
0
of
\mathsf{conf}_{0}((v,w))
expresses that
M
executes instruction
0:\mathsf{instruction}_{0}
at time
t=0
. We express this with the subformula
\texttt{instruction}(0,0)
defined above. The second entry
(2n+2q(n),0,\dots,0)
of
\mathsf{conf}_{0}((v,w))
expresses that the zeroth index register is initialized to hold
|(v,w)|
at time
t=0
, while all other index registers are initialized to
0
. We express this with the subformula

\textup{{index}}_{0}(2n+2q(n),0)\wedge\left(\bigwedge_{j=1}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}\textup{{index}}_{j}(0,0)\right).

We do not want to encode a particular witness
w
into
\varphi_{v}
because we want
\varphi_{v}
to encode a computation of
M
on
any
potential witness. This means that we do not know specific values for
w_{1},\dots,w_{q(n)}
in the third entry of
\mathsf{conf}_{0}((v,w))
, but we do know that
(0,v_{1},0,\dots,0,v_{n},1)
is an initial segment of this entry, and that every remaining element in this string with an even index is
0
. We express this with the subformula

\textup{{input}}_{v}\equiv\left(\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}Z_{2i-1,0}=v_{i}\right)\wedge\left(\bigwedge_{i=0}^{n-1}Z_{2i,0}=0\right)\wedge Z_{2n,0}=1\wedge\left(\bigwedge_{i=n+1}^{\lfloor(n^{m}-1)/2\rfloor}Z_{2i,0}=0\right).

Note that
\textup{{input}}_{v}
does not constrain each of the variables in
\{Z_{i,0}:0\leq i\leq n^{m}-1\}
. Furthermore, all variables remain free in
\textup{{input}}_{v},
and they will remain free in
\varphi_{v}
. Also note that
\textup{{input}}_{v}
is a formula in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
because we assumed that
\mathcal{R}
has all constants, which allows us to use
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in R
as constants. We combine the above subformulas into one subformula
\textup{{start}}_{v}
partially encoding
\mathsf{conf}_{0}((v,w)),
while leaving
w
undetermined, as

\textup{{start}}_{v}\equiv\texttt{instruction}(0,0)\wedge\textup{{index}}_{0}(2n+2q(n),0)\wedge\left(\bigwedge_{j=1}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}\textup{{index}}_{j}(0,0)\right)\wedge\textup{{input}}_{v}.

We encode the way in which
M
modifies its registers based on instruction
\ell
at time
t
with a family of formulas
\textup{{update}}(\ell,t)
, where
\ell
and
t
are parameters that determine the specific formula
\textup{{update}}(\ell,t)
. This means that
\ell
and
t
are not variables of
\textup{{update}}(\ell,t)
. However
\textup{{update}}(\ell,t)
will always be a statement about the variables whose time index is either
t
or
t+1
. Each instance of
\textup{{update}}(\ell,t)
includes subformulas stating that if a register is unmodified by
M
at time
t
, then at time
t+1
it contains the same value that it did at time
t
. In what follows,
i,i^{\prime},
and
t
range over
\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}
,
j,j^{\prime}
range over
\{0,\dots,k_{\mathcal{P}}-1\}
, and
p
ranges over
\{1,\dots,\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1\}
. The definition of
\textup{{update}}(\ell,t)
is based on the possible instructions in
\mathcal{P}
and their meanings as detailed in
Table

3
and
Definition

74
.

1.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=c
, then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}(\ell,t)&\equiv&\texttt{instruction}(\ell+1,t+1)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&Z_{i,t+1}=c\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\bigwedge_{i^{\prime}\in\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}\setminus\{i\}}\left(Z_{i^{\prime},t+1}=Z_{i^{\prime},t}\right)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\bigwedge_{j=0}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}\bigwedge_{p=1}^{\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor}\left(I_{j,p,t+1}=I_{j,p,t}\right)\end{array}

2.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=f(Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{k}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}})
, then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}(\ell,t)&\equiv&\texttt{instruction}(\ell+1,t+1)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&Z_{i_{0},t+1}=f(Z_{{i_{1}},t},\dots,Z_{i_{k},t})\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\bigwedge_{i^{\prime}\in\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}\setminus\{i_{0}\}}Z_{i^{\prime},t+1}=Z_{i^{\prime},t}\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\bigwedge_{j=0}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}\bigwedge_{p=1}^{\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1}I_{j,p,t+1}=I_{j,p,t}\end{array}

3.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:\mathsf{if}\ Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}=Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{2}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{1}\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{2},
then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}(\ell,t)&\equiv&\Big(Z_{i_{1},t}=Z_{i_{2},t}\to\texttt{instruction}(\ell_{1},t+1)\Big)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\Big(\neg Z_{i_{1},t}\neq Z_{i_{2},t}\to\texttt{instruction}(\ell_{2},t+1)\Big)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\Big(\bigwedge_{i\in\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}}Z_{i,t+1}=Z_{i,t}\Big)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\Big(\bigwedge_{j=0}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}\bigwedge_{p=1}^{\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1}I_{j,p,t+1}=I_{j,p,t}\Big)\end{array}

4.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:\mathsf{if}\ P(Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{k}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}})\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{1}\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{2},
then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}(\ell,t)&\equiv&\Big(P(Z_{i_{1},t},\dots,Z_{i_{k},t})\to\texttt{instruction}(\ell_{1},t+1)\Big)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\Big(\neg P(Z_{i_{1},t},\dots,Z_{i_{k},t})\to\texttt{instruction}(\ell_{2},t+1)\Big)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\Big(\bigwedge_{i\in\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}}Z_{i,t+1}=Z_{i,t}\Big)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\Big(\bigwedge_{j=0}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}\bigwedge_{p=1}^{\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1}I_{j,p,t+1}=I_{j,p,t}\Big)\end{array}

5.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j^{\prime}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
, then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}(\ell,t)&\equiv&\texttt{instruction}(\ell+1,t+1)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\bigwedge_{(n_{j},n_{j^{\prime}})\in\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}^{2}}\Big[(\textup{{index}}_{j}(n_{j},t)\wedge\textup{{index}}_{j^{\prime}}(n_{j^{\prime}},t))\to\\
&&\\
&&\Big[\Big(Z_{n_{j},t+1}=Z_{n_{j^{\prime}},t}\Big)\wedge\\
&&\\
&&\left(\bigwedge_{i^{\prime}\in\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}\setminus\{n_{j}\}}Z_{i,t+1}=Z_{i,t}\right)\wedge\\
&&\\
&&\left(\bigwedge_{j=0}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}}\bigwedge_{p=1}^{\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1}I_{j,p,t+1}=I_{j,p,t}\right)\Big]\Big]\end{array}

6.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:\mathsf{if}\ I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j^{\prime}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{1}\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{2}
, then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}(\ell,t)&\equiv&\bigwedge_{(n_{j},n_{j^{\prime}})\in\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}^{2}}\Big[(\textup{{index}}_{j}(n_{j},t)\land\textup{{index}}_{j^{\prime}}(n_{j^{\prime}},t))\to\\
&&\\
&&\Big[\Big(\left(\bigwedge_{p=1}^{\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1}I_{j,p,t}=I_{j^{\prime},p,t}\right)\to\texttt{instruction}(\ell_{1},t+1)\Big)\wedge\\
&&\\
&&\Big(\neg\left(\bigwedge_{p=1}^{\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1}I_{j,p,t}=I_{j^{\prime},p,t}\right)\to\texttt{instruction}(\ell_{2},t+1)\Big)\Big]\Big]\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\Big(\bigwedge_{i\in\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}}Z_{i,t+1}=Z_{i,t}\Big)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\Big(\bigwedge_{j=0}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}\bigwedge_{p=1}^{\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1}I_{j,p,t+1}=I_{j,p,t}\Big)\end{array}

7.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}+1
, then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}(\ell,t)&\equiv&\texttt{instruction}(\ell+1,t+1)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\bigwedge_{n_{j}\in\{0,\dots,n^{m}-2\}}\Big(\textup{{index}}_{j}(n_{j},t)\to\textup{{index}}_{j}(n_{j}+1,t+1)\Big)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\Big(\bigwedge_{i\in\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}}Z_{i,t+1}=Z_{i,t}\Big)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\Big(\bigwedge_{j^{\prime}\in\{0\dots k_{\mathcal{P}}-1\}\setminus\{j\}}\bigwedge_{p=1}^{\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1}I_{j^{\prime},p,t+1}=I_{j^{\prime},p,t}\Big)\end{array}

8.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}j\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\hss\cr$-$\crcr}}}1
, then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}(\ell,t)&\equiv&\texttt{instruction}(\ell+1,t+1)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\bigwedge_{n_{j}\in\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}}\Big(\textup{{index}}_{j}(n_{j},t)\to\textup{{index}}_{j}(n_{j}\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\hss\cr$-$\crcr}}}1,t+1)\Big)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\Big(\bigwedge_{i\in\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}}Z_{i,t+1}=Z_{i,t}\Big)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\Big(\bigwedge_{j^{\prime}\in\{0\dots k_{\mathcal{P}}-1\}\setminus\{j\}}\bigwedge_{p=1}^{\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1}I_{j^{\prime},p,t+1}=I_{j^{\prime},p,t}\Big)\end{array}

9.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell_{\mathcal{P}}:\mathsf{stop}
and
t\leq n^{m}-1
, then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}(\ell,t)&\equiv&\texttt{instruction}(\ell_{\mathcal{P}},t+1)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\Big(\bigwedge_{i}Z_{i,t+1}=Z_{i,t}\Big)\\
&&\\
&\wedge&\Big(\bigwedge_{j=0}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}\bigwedge_{p=1}^{\lfloor\log(n^{m})\rfloor+1}I_{j,p,t+1}=I_{j,p,t}\Big)\end{array}

Letting
\textup{{update}}\equiv\bigwedge_{\ell=0}^{\ell_{\mathcal{P}}}\bigwedge_{t=0}^{n^{m}-1}\textup{{update}}(\ell,t)
and
\textup{{accept}}\equiv\textup{{index}}_{0}(1,n^{m})\wedge Z_{0,n^{m}}=1,
we define the formula
\varphi_{v}
as

\varphi_{v}(\overline{x})\equiv\textup{{start}}_{v}(\overline{x})\wedge\textup{{update}}(\overline{x})\wedge\textup{{accept}}(\overline{x}),

where
\overline{x}
denotes the tuple of all variables in
\varphi_{v}
. We now show that
v\in L
if and only if
\mathcal{R}\vDash\exists\overline{x}(\varphi_{v}(\overline{x}))
.

Suppose
v\in L
. Then there is some
w\in R^{q(n)}
such that
M(v,w)=1
. This means that the computation
\mathsf{conf}_{0}((v,w))\to\mathsf{conf}_{1}\to\dots\to\mathsf{conf}_{N}
of
M
on input
(v,w)
ends with an accepting configuration, which is a configuration of the form
\mathsf{conf}_{N}=(\ell_{\mathcal{P}},(1,n_{1},\dots,n_{k_{\mathcal{P}}}),(1,r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{n^{m}-1}))
with
N<n^{m}
. We use this sequence of configurations to define values in
R
for each variable of
\varphi_{v}
. For each
0\leq t\leq N
, we have a configuration
\mathsf{conf}_{t}
given by the computation. For each
N<t\leq n^{m}-1
, let
\mathsf{conf}_{t}=\mathsf{conf}_{N}
. Thus, we have one configuration
\mathsf{conf}_{t}
for each
t
with
0\leq t\leq n^{m}-1
.

Suppose
\mathsf{conf}_{t}=(\ell,(n_{0},\dots,n_{k_{\mathcal{P}}}),(r_{0},\dots,r_{n^{m}-1})).
We use
\mathsf{conf}_{t}
to assign values to all variables of
\varphi_{v}
indexed by
t
, including
S_{p,t}
,
I_{j,p,t}
and
Z_{i,t}
. The binary representation of
\ell
determines values for the variables
S_{p,t}
, the binary representation of
n_{j}
determines values for
I_{j,p,t}
, and
r_{i}
determines a value for
Z_{i,t}
. Let
\overline{r}
be a tuple of elements in
R
that matches the variable tuple
\overline{x}
in such a way that
x_{i}
is assigned value
r_{i}
according to the above scheme. Since
\varphi_{v}
says precisely that this tuple
\overline{r}
of values encodes an accepting computation, we can see that
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi_{v}(\overline{r})
, which implies that
\mathcal{R}\vDash\exists\overline{x}(\varphi_{v}(\overline{x})).

Conversely, suppose
\mathcal{R}\vDash\exists\overline{x}(\varphi_{v}(\overline{x})).
Then there is some tuple
\overline{r}
of values in
R
such that
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi_{v}(\overline{r}).
In particular,
\mathcal{R}\vDash\textup{{start}}_{v}(\overline{r})\wedge\textup{{update}}(\overline{r})
, so the values in
\mathcal{R}
encode the computation of
M
on the input
(v,w)
, where
w_{i}
is determined by the vales assigned to the variable
Z_{2n+2i-1,0}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,q(n)\}
. These variables determine
w
because of the form of the initial configuration
\mathsf{conf}_{0}((v,w))
of
M
on any input, as described above. Since it is also the case that
\mathcal{R}\vDash\textup{{accept}}(\overline{r})
, it must be that
M(v,w)=1
, which implies that
v\in L.

Finally, we verify that we can build
\exists\overline{x}(\varphi_{v}(\overline{x}))
from
v
,
q
, and
M
using an
\mathcal{R}
