-machine that runs in polynomial time in the length of
v
. It is sufficient to note that the number of atomic formulas in
\varphi_{v}
is polynomial in the length of
v
. Encoding
\varphi_{v}
as an element of
R^{*}
, each such atomic formula can be written down by an
\mathcal{R}
-machine in polynomial time, which means that the string in
R^{*}
that encodes
\exists\overline{x}(\varphi_{v}(\overline{x}))
may be written down in polynomial time. Thus,
L
reduces to
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
in polynomial time, from which we conclude that
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-hard.
∎

Taken together,
Theorem

122
and
Theorem

123
imply the following.

See
14

B.3
\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\exists\mathcal{R}
-Complete

See
15

Proof.

Each sentence
\varphi\in\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
contains no constants other than 0 or 1. According to our encoding scheme in
Definition

119
, this implies that
\textup{code}(\varphi)\in\{0,1\}^{*}
is a Boolean string, so we have
\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*}.
By looking at the algorithm
evaluate
, we can see that when inputs are restricted to formulas without constants other than 0 or 1, then
evaluate
may be implemented by an
\mathcal{R}
-machine without constants other than 0 or 1. Thus, by an argument analogous to
Theorem

122
, we can see that
\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})\in\exists\mathcal{R}
.

To see that
\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\exists\mathcal{R}
-hard, fix some decision problem
L\in\exists\mathcal{R}
and some arbitrary
v\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, and consider the reduction constructed in the proof of
Theorem

123
that
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-hard. There are only two ways that elements of
R
might be introduced into
\varphi_{v}
as constants:

1.

v
is hard-coded into
\varphi_{v}
with the use of constants, and

2.

the machine constants of
M
are encoded into
\varphi_{v}
using constants.

Since we assume that
v\in\{0,1\}^{*}
and that
M
has no machine constants other than 0 and 1, we see that the only constants occurring in
\varphi_{v}
are
0
and
1
. This fact, together with the argument in the proof of Theorem
123
, implies that
v\in L
if and only if
\varphi_{v}\in\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
.

The reduction
v\mapsto\varphi_{v}
is surprisingly simple in that an
\mathcal{R}
-machine implementing this reduction does not need access to any of the functions or relations of
\mathcal{R}
: it only needs to be able to read and write elements of
R
. When
L\in\exists\mathcal{R}
, we can see from the above that even this ability is not required because both
v
and
\textup{code}(\varphi_{v})
are binary strings, and reducing
v
to
\varphi_{v}
only depends on a binary encoding of
M
, which exists because
M
has no constants other than 0 or 1. Thus, this reduction can be carried out by a Turing machine, and the same complexity analysis shows that it runs in polynomial time.

∎

Corollary 124
.

\exists\mathbb{R}=\textup{BP}(\textup{NP}^{0}_{\mathbb{R}})
.

B.4
Complete Problems for
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
and
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}

In this subsection we will show that
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
-complete and
\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
-complete by induction on
k\in\mathbb{N}
. The proof we give essentially requires the use of constants from the universe of
\mathcal{R}
, but the reduction we construct does not introduce constants not already in the language being reduced, so it is also the case that
\exists_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}
-complete and
\forall_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\forall_{k}\mathcal{R}
-complete under many-one polynomial-time reductions with a Turing machine.

Although one can show that
\Pi_{1}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{coNP}(\mathcal{R})=\Pi_{1}\mathcal{R}
-complete as an easy corollary of Theorem
14
, it is instructive to give a direct proof that
\Pi_{1}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{coNP}(\mathcal{R})
-hard because this reduction provides intuition for the reductions we will construct for higher levels of the hierarchy.

Theorem 125
.

\Pi_{1}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{coNP}(\mathcal{R})
-hard.

Proof.

Suppose
L\in\textup{coNP}(\mathcal{R})
. Then there is some polynomial
q
and some polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
v\in R^{n}
,

v\in L\text{ if and only if }(\forall w\in R^{q(n)})\ M(v,w)=1.

Let each of
\textup{{start}}_{v}
,
update
, and
accept
be formulas in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
that are constructed as in the proof of Theorem
123
, and let
\varphi_{v}
be the formula

\varphi_{v}\equiv(\textup{{start}}_{v}\wedge\textup{{update}})\to\textup{{accept}}.

Given the definition of
\textup{{input}}_{v},
the variables in
\varphi_{v}
that encode a potential witness string
w
loaded into the
\mathcal{R}
-registers at time zero are the variables
Z_{2n+1,0},Z_{2n+3,0},Z_{2n+5,0},\dots,Z_{2n+2q(n)-1,0}.
We write
\forall Z
to denote the quantifier block

\forall Z_{2n+1,0}\forall Z_{2n+3,0}\forall Z_{2n+5,0}\dots\forall Z_{2n+2q(n)-1,0}.

With this definition in mind, we define the
\Pi_{k}
sentence
\Phi_{v}
in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
as

\Phi_{v}\equiv\forall Z\forall\overline{y}(\varphi_{v}(Z,\overline{y})),

where
\overline{y}
is the tuple of all variables occurring in
\varphi_{v}
except those occurring in
Z
. Since the number of atomic subformulas occurring in
\varphi_{v}
is polynomial in
n=|v|
, there is an
\mathcal{R}
-machine that constructs
\Phi_{v}
from
v
,
q
and
M
in polynomial time in
v
. Thus, it is sufficient to show that
v\in L
if and only if
\mathcal{R}\vDash\Phi_{v}
.

Suppose
v\in L.
Then
(\forall w\in R^{q(n)})\ M(v,w)=1.
Let
w
be an arbitrary string in
R^{q(n)}
that assigns values to the variables occurring in
Z,
and let
\overline{r}
be any tuple that assigns some arbitrary element
r_{i}
of
R
to each variable
y_{i}
occurring in
\overline{y}.
If
\mathcal{R}\vDash(\textup{{start}}_{v}\wedge\textup{{update}})(w,\overline{r}),
then we know that
(w,\overline{r})
encodes the computation of
M
on input
(v,w).
Since
M(v,w)=1
by our assumption that
v\in L,
we can see from the definition of
accept
that
\mathcal{R}\vDash\textup{{accept}}(w,\overline{r}).
As
w
and
\overline{r}
were arbitrary, it must be that for all
w\in R^{q(n)}
and all
\overline{r},

\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi_{v}(w,\overline{r})
. Thus,
\mathcal{R}\vDash\Phi_{v}.

Conversely, suppose
\mathcal{R}\vDash\Phi_{v}.
Then
\mathcal{R}\vDash\forall Z\forall\overline{y}(\varphi_{v}(Z,\overline{y})).
Any string
w\in R^{q(n)}
determines values for the variables occurring in
Z
, while the computation
\mathsf{conf}_{0}((v,w))\to\dots\to\mathsf{conf}_{N}
of
M
on input
(v,w)
determines values
\overline{r}
for the remaining variables
\overline{y}
of
\varphi_{v}
, as in the proof of Theorem
123
. Because
(w,\overline{r})
encodes the computation of
M
on input
(v,w)
, we can see that
\mathcal{R}\vDash(\textup{{start}}_{v}\wedge\textup{{update}})(w,\overline{r})
. Then
\mathcal{R}\vDash\textup{{accept}}(w,\overline{r})
by our assumption that
\mathcal{R}\vDash\Phi_{v}
, which implies that
M(v,w)=1
. Thus,
v\in L
.
∎

The main difference between the proof that
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-hard and the proof that
\Pi_{1}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{coNP}(\mathcal{R})
-hard is that in the former proof, it sufficed to find a single tuple of elements in
R
that encodes an accepting computation of
M
, while in the latter proof we want to show something about all tuples of
R
of a certain length. In general, such a tuple will not encode a computation of
M
, let alone an accepting computation, so we introduce the implication
(\textup{{start}}_{v}\wedge\textup{{update}})\to\textup{{accept}}
to restrict our attention to those tuples that do encode a computation.

This will be important when showing that
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
-hard in the case that
k
is even. To see this, suppose
L\in\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}.
Then there is some polynomial
q
and some polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
v\in R^{n},

v\in L\iff(\exists w_{1}\in R^{q(n)})(\forall w_{2}\in R^{q(n)})\dots(\forall w_{k}\in R^{q(n)})M(v,w_{1},\dots,w_{k})=1.

We want to construct a formula
\varphi_{v}
such that

v\in L\iff\mathcal{R}\vDash\exists Z_{1}\forall Z_{2}\dots\forall Z_{k}\forall\overline{y}\ \varphi_{v}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{k},\overline{y}),

where the variables
Z_{i}
encode a potential witness strings
w_{i}
, and the variables
\overline{y}
encode the rest of the computation of
M
on input
(v,w_{1},\dots,w_{k}).
We need to quantify the variables
\overline{y}
to be quantified universally, matching the universal quantification over
Z_{k}
, in order avoid another quantifier alternation, thereby ensuring the resulting sentence to be a
\Pi_{k}
sentence. So we will use a formula of the form
(\textup{{start}}_{v}\wedge\textup{{update}})\to\textup{{accept}}
whenever the last quantifier
Q_{k}
is universal.

In addition, when constructing
\varphi_{v},
we need to modify
\textup{{start}}_{v}
to build a formula
\textup{{start}}_{v,k}
stating that
M
starts with an input of the form
(v,w_{1},\dots,w_{k})
in its registers. Towards this end, let
m
be such that
M
halts in at most
n^{m}
steps on any input of the form
(v,w_{1},\dots,w_{k})
with each
w_{i}\in R^{q(n)}
. Furthermore, assume that
n^{m}
is greater than the largest value attained by any index register during a computation of
M
on any input of the above form, and assume that
n^{m}
is greater than the length
\ell_{\mathcal{P}}+1
of the program of
M.

Recall that our pairing function represents the tuple
(v,w_{1},\dots,w_{k})
as the string

(0,v_{1},0,\dots,0,v_{n},1,w_{1,1},0,\dots,0,w_{1,q(n)},1,\dots,1,w_{k,1},0,\dots,0,w_{k,q(n)}),

where a 1 in an even-index position (starting from index
i=0
) demarcates the end of one entry and the beginning of another. Thus, upon receiving an input of the form
(v,w_{1},\dots,w_{k}),

M
will initialize
\mathcal{R}
-registers
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}n^{m}-1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
with the values

(0,v_{1},0,\dots,0,v_{n},1,w_{1,1},0,\dots,0,w_{1,q(n)},1,\dots,1,w_{k,1},0,\dots,0,w_{k,q(n)},0,\dots,0).

Let
\textup{{input}}_{v,k}
be the formula constructed as
\textup{{input}}_{v}
from the proof of Theorem
123
, except replace the clause
Z_{2n,0}=1
with the clause
\bigwedge_{j=0}^{k-1}(Z_{2(n+jq(n)),0}=1)
expressing the fact that at time zero we now have
k
witness strings
w_{1},\dots,w_{k}
that need to be demarcated, rather than just one.

Similarly, on any input of the above form,
M
will initialize index registers
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}k_{\mathcal{P}}-1\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
with values

(2(n+kq(n)),0,\dots,0),

where the zeroth index register contains the length of the input. Let
\textup{{start}}_{v,k}
be the formula constructed as in the proof of Theorem
123
, except modify
\textup{{input}}_{v}
as described above and replace the clause
\textup{{index}}_{0}(2n+2q(n),0)
with the clause
\textup{{index}}_{0}(2(n+kq(n)),0)
expressing the fact that at time zero the zeroth index register should contain the length of an input of the above form
(v,w_{1},\dots,w_{k})
with
k
witness strings.

With this definition of
\textup{{start}}_{v,k}
in mind, let
update
and
accept
be the formulas constructed in the proof of Theorem
123
, and define the formula
\varphi_{v,k}
follows.
If
k
is even, then

\varphi_{v,k}\equiv(\textup{{start}}_{v,k}\wedge\textup{{update}})\to\textup{{accept}},

and if
k
is odd, then

\varphi_{v,k}\equiv\textup{{start}}_{v,k}\wedge\textup{{update}}\wedge\textup{{accept}}.

Given the definition of
\textup{{input}}_{v,k}
, the variables in
\varphi_{v,k}
that encode a potential witness string
w_{j}
loaded into the
\mathcal{R}
-registers at time zero are those variables
Z_{i,0}
such that
i
is odd and
2(n+(j-1)q(n))\leq i\leq 2(n+jq(n))-1
. Letting
a(j)=2(n+(j-1)q(n)),
we can see that these variables are
Z_{a(j)+1,0},Z_{a(j)+3,0},\dots,Z_{a(j)+2q(n)-1,0}.
For any quantifier
Q_{j}\in\{\exists,\forall\},
we write
Q_{j}Z_{j}
to denote the quantifier block

Q_{j}Z_{a(j)+1,0}Q_{j}Z_{a(j)+3,0}\dots Q_{j}Z_{a(j)+q(n)-1,0}.

With this abbreviation in mind, define the
\Sigma_{k}
sentence
\Phi_{v,k}
in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
as

\Phi_{v}\equiv\exists Z_{1}\forall Z_{2}\dots QZ_{k}Q\overline{y}(\varphi_{v}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{k},\overline{y})),

where the quantifier blocks
Q_{j}Z_{j}
alternate and
\overline{y}
is the tuple of all variables occurring in
\varphi
except those occurring in
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{k}
. Noting that
\Phi_{v,k}
depends on the
L\in\Sigma_{k}
from which we build
\varphi_{v,k},
we will show for all
L\in\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
that
v\in L
if and only if
\mathcal{R}\vDash\Phi_{v,k}
by induction on
k
.

Lemma 126

(Encoding Computations in First Order Formulas)
.

Let
M
be an arbitrary polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine,
q
be an arbitrary polynomial, and
v
be an arbitrary element of
R^{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}.
Then for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and for all
(w_{1},\dots,w_{k})\in(R^{q(n)})^{k},
the following are equivalent:

1.

M(v,w_{1},\dots,w_{k})=1

2.

\mathcal{R}\vDash\exists\overline{y}\ \varphi_{v,k}(w_{1},\dots,w_{k},\overline{y})

3.

\mathcal{R}\vDash\forall\overline{y}\ \widetilde{\varphi}_{v,k}(w_{1},\dots,w_{k},\overline{y})

Proof.

We prove that
(1)
is equivalent to
(2).
The proof that
(1)
is equivalent to
(3)
is similar. Suppose
M(v,w_{1},\dots,w_{k})=1.
Then the computation
\mathsf{conf}_{0}((v,w_{1},\dots,w_{k}))\to\dots\to\mathsf{conf}_{N}
of
M
on input
(v,w_{1},\dots,w_{k})
determines values
\overline{r}
for the variables
\overline{y}
as in the proof of Theorem
123
. Since
\overline{r}
encodes this computation and since this is an accepting computation, we see that
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi_{v,k}(w_{1},\dots,w_{k},\overline{r}).
Thus,
\mathcal{R}\vDash\exists\overline{y}\ \varphi_{v,k}(w_{1},\dots,w_{k},\overline{y}).

Conversely, suppose
\mathcal{R}\vDash\exists\overline{y}\ \varphi_{v,k}(w_{1},\dots,w_{k},\overline{y}).
Then there are values
\overline{r}
in
R
for the variables
\overline{y}
such that
\mathcal{R}\vDash\varphi_{v,k}(w_{1},\dots,w_{k},\overline{r}).
From the definition of
\varphi_{v,k},
we can see that this means that
\overline{r}
encodes the computation of
M
on input
(v,w_{1},\dots,w_{k}).
Since
\mathcal{R}\vDash\textup{{accept}}(w_{1},\dots,w_{k},\overline{r}),
it must be that this is an accepting computation. Thus,
M(v,w_{1},\dots,w_{k})=1.

∎

Note that the above lemma implies
Lemma

13
from the main body of this paper. At this point, the reader might be convinced that hardness for each level of the polynomial hierarchy has been established: since the condition (1) is equivalent to the condition (2) and to the condition (3) for all strings
w_{1},\dots,w_{k},
one may simply place the appropriate alternating sequence of quantifiers before each condition to see that they remain equivalent. Indeed, the above lemma shoes the
logical equivalence
of all three statements, and so we may use these statements interchangeably by a metatheorem of first-order logic called the replacement theorem
[
31
]
. This gives us the following theorem.

Theorem 127
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N},

\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
-hard and
\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
-hard.

Proof.

Suppose
L\in\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
. For any
v\in R^{n}
, let
\varphi_{v,k}
be the formula depending on
L
and
v
defined above, and let
\Phi_{v,k}
be the
\Sigma_{k}
sentence in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
defined by

\Phi_{v,k}\equiv\exists Z_{1}\forall Z_{2}\dots QZ_{k}Q\overline{y}\ \varphi_{v,0,k}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{k},\overline{y}).

Then by Lemma
126
,
v\in L
if and only if
\mathcal{R}\vDash\Phi_{v,k}.
Since the number of atomic subformulas in
\varphi_{v,0,k}
is polynomial in
|v|,
an
\mathcal{R}
-machine can construct
\Phi_{v,k}
in polynomial time from
v
and
L
. Thus
v\mapsto\Phi_{v,k}
is a polynomial-time reduction from
L
to
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R}),
so we see that
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
hard. Similarly,
\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
-hard.
∎

We also have an easy proof of membership.

Theorem 128
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is in
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
and
\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is in
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
.

Proof.

Recall from Lemma
121
that the algorithm
evaluate
runs in polynomial time. Take some
\Sigma_{k}
sentence
\Phi\equiv\exists\overline{x}_{1}\forall\overline{x}_{2}\dots Q_{k}\overline{x}_{k}\varphi(\overline{x}_{1},\dots,\overline{x}_{k})
in the vocabulary of
\mathcal{R}
. Let
n=|\Phi|
be the length of the encoding of
\Phi
and let
q(n)=n
be the constant polynomial. Each quantifier block
Q_{i}\overline{x}_{i}
is at most as long as
n
, and so any
\overline{r}_{i}\in R^{n}
determines an interpretation for each variable in
\overline{x}_{i}
. Then we can see that
\Phi\in\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
if and only if

(\exists\overline{r}_{1}\in R^{n})(\forall\overline{r}_{2}\in R^{n})\dots(Q_{k}\overline{r}_{k}\in R^{n})\ \textup{{evaluate}}(\varphi,\overline{r}_{1},\dots,\overline{r}_{n})=1.

Thus,
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})\in\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
and similarly
\Pi_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})\in\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
.
∎

From these two theorems, we have the following theorem as a corollary.

See
16

Small modifications to the proofs above give the following theorem via an analysis of what happens when we restrict to Boolean inputs and machines without constants other than 0 or 1, just as in the proof in
Section

B.3
of
Corollary

15
that
\exists\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\exists\mathcal{R}
-complete.

See
17

Appendix C
Fagin’s Theorem Over a Structure

In this section, we show that
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
for any bipointed
\mathcal{R}
. Notably, we do not need to assume that
\mathcal{R}
is of finite type nor that
\mathcal{R}
has all constants. According to
Definition

117
, showing that
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
requires showing two things: (1) if
\Phi
is a
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
sentence, then
\{\mathcal{D}:\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi\}\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
, and (2) if
L
is a decision problem of
\mathcal{R}
-structures such that
L\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
, then there is some
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
sentence
\Phi
such that
L=\{\mathcal{D}:\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi\}
. Subsection
C.1
establishes point (1) in
Theorem

130
, and Subsection
C.2
shows point (2) in Theorem
133
, thereby establishing
Theorem

19
. In Subsection
C.3
we modify the proofs of the above theorems to prove that
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\exists\mathcal{R}
over Boolean
\mathcal{R}
structures, thereby establishing
Corollary

20
, and in Subsection
C.4
we extend these results to the Polynomial and Boolean hierarchies, thereby establishing
Theorem

21
and
Corollary

22
.

C.1
Data Complexity of
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})

In this subsection we will show that for any metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
over
\mathcal{R}
and any sentence
\Phi
of
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathsf{MVoc})
, the decision problem of
\mathcal{R}
-structures
\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{Struct}(\mathsf{MVoc}):\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi\}
belongs to
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
. In contrast to the argument showing that
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is in
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
in
Theorem

122
, we do not need to assume that
\mathcal{R}
is of finite type. Towards this end, fix a metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}=(\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{MVoc}),\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{MVoc}),\mathsf{W}(\mathsf{MVoc}))
over
\mathcal{R}
, by which we mean that
\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{MVoc})=\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
.

Note that the basic operations in the primary part
\mathcal{A}
of an
\mathcal{R}
-structure
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
in this vocabulary are not basic operations of the structure
\mathcal{R}
, which means that an
\mathcal{R}
-machine cannot execute them in unit time as it does the operations of
\mathcal{R}
. However, given our knowledge of the fixed vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
, we can program an
\mathcal{R}
-machine to simulate the operations of the primary part of
\mathcal{D}
in time polynomial in
|\mathcal{D}|=|A|
when it is given
\textup{code}(\mathcal{D})
as input.

We must first determine an encoding of the elements in the primary universe of
\mathcal{D}
in order to feed them as inputs to an
\mathcal{R}
-machine because, in general, the universe of
\mathcal{A}
is not a subset of the universe of
\mathcal{R}
. Recall that we have a chosen total ordering
\leq\!(\mathcal{A})
on the primary universe of
\mathcal{D}
, which orders
A
as
\{a^{\prime}_{1},\dots,a^{\prime}_{|\mathcal{D}|}\}.
Given
a\in A
, we let
\textup{code}(a)=\textup{bin}(i)
, where
a=a^{\prime}_{i}
and
\textup{bin}(i)
is the binary representation of
i
.

Using any standard encoding of tuples, we extend this encoding of
A
to encodings of tuples
(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in A^{k}
as binary strings
\textup{code}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in\{0,1\}^{*}
. Since
\textup{code}(a)
directly references the place of
a
in the total order on
A
, we can program an
\mathcal{R}
-machine to determine the place of a tuple
(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})
in the induced lexicographic order on
A^{k}
, based on
\textup{code}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})
. Furthermore, for a fixed
k
, an
\mathcal{R}
-machine can do this in time polynomial in
|\mathcal{D}|
via
k
applications of binary search.

Suppose
P
is a
k
-ary relation of the primary part of
\mathcal{D}
. Given
\textup{code}(\mathcal{D})
and
\textup{code}(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})
as inputs, we can program an
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
to check if
(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in P
in the following way. First,
M
determines the place
i
of
(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})
in the lexicographic order on
A^{k}
. Second, using our knowledge of
\mathsf{Voc}
, we program
M
to use
|\textup{code}(\mathcal{D})|
to determine
|\mathcal{D}|
, and from this to identify the substring
\textup{code}(P)
of
\textup{code}(\mathcal{D})
. Third,
M
examines
\textup{code}(P)
to determine if the
i
th bit of
\textup{code}(P)
is one.

The length of
\textup{code}(\mathcal{D})
is polynomial in
|\mathcal{D}|
, where this polynomial is based on the arity of the symbols in
\mathsf{Voc}
. The second step of the above algorithm can be accomplished by repeatedly evaluating this polynomial on values
n\leq|\textup{code}(\mathcal{D})|
until the result is greater than or equal to
|\textup{code}(\mathcal{D})|
. Each evaluation can be done in time linear in
|\mathcal{D}|
, so all of the second step can be done in time polynomial in
|\mathcal{D}|
. In the worst case, the third step would need to scan the whole of
\textup{code}(\mathcal{D})
, which means that it can be done in time polynomial in
|\mathcal{D}|
. Thus, evaluating if
(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in P
can be done by a polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine.

Similarly, any other operation of the primary part of
\mathcal{D}
and any weight function of
\mathcal{D}
can be simulated by a polynomial time
\mathcal{R}
-machine that receives
\textup{code}(\mathcal{D})
as an input. The ability of
\mathcal{R}
-machines to simulate the operations of
\mathcal{R}
-structures in polynomial time is the basis for the following lemma.

Lemma 129
.

For any metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
over
\mathcal{R}
, if
\varphi
is a sentence of first-order metafinite logic in this vocabulary, then
\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{Struct}(\mathsf{MVoc}):\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi\}\in\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})
.

Proof.

We first prove a more general statement: For any first-order formula
\varphi
in prenex normal form

Q_{1}x_{1}\dots Q_{k}x_{k}\psi(x_{1},\dots,x_{k},x_{k+1},\dots,x_{s}),

for any metafinite structure
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
with
|A|=n
, and for any
(a_{k+1},\dots,a_{s})\in A^{s-k}
, we can determine if

\mathcal{D}\vDash Q_{1}x_{1}\dots Q_{k}x_{k}\psi(x_{1},\dots,x_{k},a_{k+1},\dots,a_{s})

in time polynomial in
n
. We proceed by induction on the number of quantifiers in the formula. If there are no quantifiers, then our formula is simply
\psi(x_{1},\dots,x_{s})
. Given some
(a_{1},\dots,a_{s})\in A^{s}
, to decide if
\mathcal{D}\vDash\psi(a_{1},\dots,a_{s})
, we only need to compute the value of
\psi
on the input
(a_{1},\dots,a_{s})
. As in the above discussion, this can be done in polynomial time in the size of
\mathcal{D}
. To determine if

\mathcal{D}\vDash Q_{1}x_{1}\dots Q_{k}x_{k}Q_{k+1}x_{k+1}\psi(x_{1},\dots,x_{k},x_{k+1},a_{k+2}\dots,a_{s}),

it suffices to check, for all
a_{k+1}\in A
, whether or not

\mathcal{D}\vDash Q_{1}x_{1}\dots Q_{k}x_{k}\psi(x_{1},\dots,x_{k},a_{k+1},\dots,a_{s}).

By our inductive hypothesis, each of these checks can be done in polynomial time. Since this means making
|A|=n
checks, in total we again need only polynomial time, where the exponent of this polynomial has been increased by one. This completes our inductive argument.

Any sentence
\varphi
is logically equivalent to a sentence
\varphi^{\prime}
in prenex normal form, which means that deciding if
\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi
is equivalent to deciding if
\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi^{\prime}
. By the above, this may be done in polynomial time.
∎

Theorem 130
.

For any metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
over
\mathcal{R}
, if
\Phi
is a sentence of
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathsf{MVoc})
, then
\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{Struct}(\mathsf{MVoc}):\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi\}\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R}).

Proof.

Any
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathsf{MVoc})
sentence
\Phi
is of the form
\exists w_{1}\dots\exists w_{h}\varphi(w_{1},\dots,w_{h})
, where
w_{1},\dots,w_{h}
are second-order variables. Suppose
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
is a metafinite
\mathsf{Voc}
-structure. Given any interpretation of each
w_{i}
as a weight functions
w_{i}\colon A^{k}\to R
, the formula
\varphi(w_{1},\dots,w_{k})
is a first-order sentence in vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}\cup\{w_{1},\dots,w_{h}\}=(\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{MVoc}),\mathsf{S}(\mathsf{MVoc}),\mathsf{W}(\mathsf{MVoc})\cup\{w_{1},\dots,w_{h}\}).
Writing
(w_{1},\dots,w_{h})
as
\overline{w}
, we can see that by
Lemma

129
applied to this vocabulary,

\{(\mathcal{D},\overline{w}):(\mathcal{D},\overline{w})\vDash\varphi(\overline{w})\}\in\textup{P}(\mathcal{R}).

Thus,
\{\mathcal{D}:\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi\}=\{\mathcal{D}:\exists\overline{w}(\mathcal{D},\overline{w})\vDash\varphi(\overline{w})\}\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R}).

∎

C.2
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
Captures
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})

In this subsection, we show that if
L
is any decision problem of
\mathcal{R}
-structures such that
L\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
, then there is some
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
sentence
\Phi
such that
L=\{\mathcal{D}:\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi\}.
In order to do this, we take some
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
such that
M
verifies
L
, and we construct
\Phi
so that it encodes the computation of
M
in the sense that
\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi
if and only if there is some
Y
such that
M(\mathcal{D},Y)=1.

In order to build
\Phi
in this way, we need to encode the starting configuration of
M
on input
(\mathcal{D},Y)
into a formula, as we do in
Lemma

132
. In contrast to the argument showing that
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
-hard in
Theorem

123
, we do not need to assume that
\mathcal{R}
has all constants.

We then show our main result in
Theorem

133
, which, together with the result of
Theorem

130
stating that the data complexity of
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
is
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
, implies
Theorem

19
.

Lemma 131
.

For any metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
, there are first-order formulas
\textup{{ordering}}(E)
,
\textup{{summation}}_{m}(E,S)
, and
\textup{{product}}_{m}(E,S,P)
such that for all
\mathsf{MVoc}
-structures
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
and for all positive integers
m
,

(\mathcal{D},E,S,P)\vDash\textup{{ordering}}(E)\wedge\textup{{summation}}(E,S)\wedge\textup{{product}}(E,S,P)

if and only if each of the following are true:

1.

E\colon A^{2}\to R
encodes a linear order
E\subseteq A^{2}
on
A
,

2.

S\colon A^{3m}\to R
encodes the graph of the summation function
S\subseteq A^{3m}
on
A^{m}
lexicographically ordered according to
E
, and

3.

P\colon A^{3m}\to R
encodes the graph of the product function
P\subseteq A^{3m}
on
A^{m}
lexicographically ordered according to
E
.

Proof.

For each of the above relations
F\in\{E,S,P\}
, we identify a relation
F\subseteq A^{k}
with its characteristic function
F\colon A^{k}\to\{0,1\}\subseteq R
. The formula
\textup{{ordering}}(E)
is simply the conjunction of the usual axioms for linearly ordered sets using
E
as the relation symbol.

Let
\overline{x}
,
\overline{y}
, and
\overline{z}
be
m
-tuples of variables. Given ordering
E
, the induced lexicographic order on
A^{m}
is definable with a first-order formula using the relation
E
. We abbreviate this formula as
E_{m}(\overline{x},\overline{y})
. Similarly, the least element in
A^{m}
is definable by a formula
\texttt{zero}(\overline{x})
, the maximum element is definable by a formula
\texttt{max}(\overline{x})
, and the immediate successor relation is definable by a formula
\texttt{successor}(\overline{x},\overline{y})
expressing that
\overline{y}
is the immediate successor of
\overline{x}
. We define
successor
in such a way that the maximum element of
A^{m}
is the immediate successor of itself, so that
successor
defines a total function on the finite set
A^{m}
.

Given these formulas, we encode the usual recursive definition of addition into a formula
\textup{{summation}}(E,S)
, which is then the conjunction of a clause for the base case and a clause for the recursive case of this definition. The clause corresponding to the base case is

\forall\overline{x}\forall\overline{z}(\texttt{zero}(\overline{z})\to S(\overline{x},\overline{z},\overline{x})).

The clause corresponding to the recursive case is

\forall\overline{x}\forall\overline{y}\forall\overline{z}\forall\overline{y^{\prime}}\forall\overline{z^{\prime}}\Big[\Big(S(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z})\wedge\texttt{successor}(\overline{y},\overline{y^{\prime}})\wedge\texttt{successor}(\overline{z},\overline{z^{\prime}})\Big)\to S(\overline{x},\overline{y^{\prime}},\overline{z^{\prime}})\Big].

Similarly, we can encode the usual recursive definition of multiplication in terms of addition into a formula
\textup{{product}}(E,S,P)
, which is the conjunction a clause corresponding to the base case and a clause corresponding to the recursive case of this definition.
∎

Lemma 132
.

For any metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
over
\mathcal{R}
and for any positive integer
m
such that
m\geq\sum_{j}k_{j}
, the sum of arities
k_{j}
of all symbols in
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{MVoc})\cup\mathsf{W}(\mathsf{MVoc})
, there is first-order formula
\textup{{input}}(E,S,P,Z)
in the expanded vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}\cup\{E,S,P,Z\}
such that for all
\mathsf{MVoc}
-structures
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
,

(\mathcal{D},E,S,P,Z)\vDash\textup{{input}}(E,S,P,Z)

if and only if each of the following are true:

1.

E\subseteq A^{2}
is a linear order on
A
,

2.

S\subseteq A^{3m}
is the graph of the summation function on
A^{m}
lexicographically ordered according to
E

3.

P\subseteq A^{3m}
is the graph of the product function on
A^{m}
lexicographically ordered according to
E
, and

4.

Z\colon A^{m}\to R
encodes the string
\textup{code}_{E}(\mathcal{D})0\dots 0
, which we express by writing
Z=\textup{code}_{E}(\mathcal{D})0\dots 0
.

Proof.

Lemma

131
deals with the first three conditions, so we will focus on the fourth condition by constructing a formula
\bigwedge_{j=1}^{h+1}\textup{{input}}_{j}(E,S,P,Z)
expressing the fourth condition. We will then take the conjunction of this formula with the previously constructed formulas to arrive at

\textup{{input}}(E,S,P,Z)\equiv\textup{{ordering}}(E)\wedge\textup{{summation}}_{m}(E,S)\wedge\textup{{product}}_{m}(E,S,P)\wedge\bigwedge_{j=1}^{h+1}\textup{{input}}_{j}(E,S,P,Z).

Assume that
\mathsf{MVoc}
is a vocabulary of metafinite algebras, meaning that
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{MVoc})
is empty and
\mathsf{W}(\mathsf{MVoc})=\{w_{1},\dots,w_{j},\dots,w_{h}\}
, where
j\in\{1,\dots,h\}
. The heart of the idea is illustrated with this case, and the proof in the case where
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{MVoc})
is nonempty may be obtained with a simple modification of the construction presented below. For any metafinite structure
\mathcal{D}=(A,\{w_{1},\dots,w_{j},\dots,w_{h}\})
of vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
and cardinality
|A|=n
, the code of
\mathcal{D}
relative to the ordering
E
on
A
is

\textup{code}_{E}(\mathcal{D})=\textup{code}_{E}(w_{1})\dots\textup{code}_{E}(w_{j})\dots\textup{code}_{E}(w_{h})\in R^{*}.

Recall from Definition
103
that for each weight function
w_{j}\colon A^{k_{j}}\to R
, the code of
w_{j}
is the string
\textup{code}_{E}(w_{j})=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n^{k_{j}}})\in R^{n^{k_{j}}}
where
r_{i}
is the value of
w_{j}
applied to the
i
th tuple
(p_{1},\dots,p_{k_{j}})
of
A^{k_{j}}
according to the lexicographic order on
A^{k_{j}}
induced by
E
.

In what follows, we will analyze the string
\textup{code}_{E}(\mathcal{D})
in terms of the order relation
\leq
on the natural numbers that index entries in this string, as well as in terms of the arithmetic operations of addition
+
and multiplication
\times
on the natural numbers. Using the relations
E
,
S
, and
P
that encode these operations, we will be able to use our analysis of
\textup{code}(\mathcal{D})
to construct a first order formula expressing that
Z=\textup{code}_{E}(\mathcal{D})0\dots 0
.

We think of
\overline{p}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{m})\in A^{m}
as the
n
-ary representation of
\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\times n^{m-i}\in\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}
, thereby identifying
A^{m}
with
\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}
. In the same way, we identify each
A^{k_{j}}
with
\{0,\dots,n^{k_{j}}-1\}.
According to this
n
-ary representation,
\textup{code}_{E}(w_{j})
is the substring of
\textup{code}_{E}(\mathcal{D})
indexed by tuples
(p_{1},\dots,p_{m})
such that

\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}n^{k_{i}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\times n^{m-i}\leq\left(\sum_{i=1}^{j}n^{k_{i}}\right)-1.

Keeping in mind that the length of
\textup{code}_{E}(w_{j})
is
n^{k_{j}}
, we can see that for every
\overline{p}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{m})
in the above range, there is a unique
\overline{q}=(q_{1},\dots,q_{k_{j}})\in A^{k_{j}}
such that
\overline{p}=\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}n^{k_{i}}+\overline{q},
by which we mean that

\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\times n^{m-i}=\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}n^{k_{i}}+\sum_{i=1}^{k_{j}}q_{i}\times n^{k_{j}-1}.

Viewing the function
Z\colon A^{m}\to R
as a string in
R^{n^{m}}
, we can see that
Z
agrees with
\textup{code}_{E}(\mathcal{D})
on the substring
\textup{code}_{E}(w_{j})
if and only if
Z(\overline{p})=w_{j}(\overline{q})
for all
\overline{p}
in the range from
\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}n^{k_{i}}
to
\left(\sum_{i=1}^{j}n^{k_{i}}\right)-1
and for the unique
\overline{p}
such that
\overline{p}=\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}n^{k_{i}}+\overline{q}
.

We now proceed to express the above conditions in a first-order formula. By Lemma
131
, each of
\leq
,
+
and
\times
are first-order definable using the weight function symbols
E
,
S
and
P
encoding the corresponding operations in
A^{m}
ordered lexicographically by
E
. This implies that there is a first-order formula
\texttt{range}_{j}(E,S,P,p_{1},\dots,p_{m})
in vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}\cup\{E,S,P\}
expressing that
\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\leq\overline{p}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{j}n^{k_{i}}
. Similarly, there is a first-order formula
\texttt{offset}_{j}(E,S,P,p_{1},\dots,p_{m},q_{1},\dots,q_{k_{j}})
in this same vocabulary expressing that
\overline{q}=\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}n^{k_{i}}+\overline{q}.
Given these formulas, we can express that
Z
agrees with
\textup{code}_{E}(\mathcal{D})
on the substring
\textup{code}_{E}(w_{j})
with the formula

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{input}}_{j}(E,S,P,Z)&\equiv&\forall p_{1}\dots\forall p_{m}\Big[\texttt{range}_{j}(E,S,P,p_{1},\dots,p_{m})\to\\
&&\\
&&\exists q_{1}\dots\exists q_{k_{j}}\Big[\texttt{offset}_{j}(E,S,P,p_{1},\dots,p_{m},q_{1},\dots,q_{k_{j}})\wedge\\
&&\\
&&\hskip 49.79231ptZ(p_{1},\dots,p_{m})=w_{j}(q_{1},\dots,q_{k_{j}})\Big]\Big].\\
\end{array}

Similarly, there is a first-order formula
\texttt{range}_{h+1}(E,S,P,p_{1},\dots,p_{m})
expressing that
\left(\sum_{i=1}^{h}n^{k_{i}}\right)-1\leq\overline{p}
, so we can express that
Z
ends with zeros beyond
\textup{code}_{E}(\mathcal{D})
with the formula

\textup{{input}}_{h+1}(E,S,P,Z)\equiv\forall p_{1}\dots\forall p_{m}\Big[\texttt{range}_{h+1}(E,S,P,p_{1},\dots,p_{m})\to Z(p_{1},\dots,p_{m})=0\Big].

We define the formula
\textup{{input}}(E,S,P,Z)

input
as the conjunction

\textup{{input}}(E,S,P,Z)\equiv\textup{{ordering}}(E)\wedge\textup{{summation}}_{m}(E,S)\wedge\textup{{product}}_{m}(E,S,P)\wedge\bigwedge_{j=1}^{h+1}\textup{{input}}_{j}(E,S,P,Z).

The first three formulas guarantee that we can formally express the above arithmetic with the weight functions
E
,
S
, and
P
, and the fourth formula guarantees that
Z=\textup{code}_{E}(\mathcal{D})0\dots 0.

∎

Theorem 133
.

For every metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
over
\mathcal{R}
, if
L
is a decision problem of
\mathcal{R}
-structures such that
L\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
, then there is a sentence
\Phi
of
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathsf{MVoc})
such that

L=\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{Struct}(\mathsf{MVoc}):\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi(L)\}.

Proof.

Since
L\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
, there is some positive integer
k
and some polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
such that

L=\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{Struct}(\mathsf{MVoc}):(\exists Y\colon A^{k}\to R)\ M(\mathcal{D},Y)=1\}.

Let
n=|\mathcal{D}|=|A|
be the size of the
\mathcal{R}
-structure
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
of vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
, and let
m
be a natural number such that
M
stops on
(\mathcal{D},Y)
after fewer than
n^{m}
steps for all
Y\colon A^{k}\to R
. Furthermore, assume that
n^{m}
is larger than the value attained by any index register of
M
during a computation on any input
(\mathcal{D},Y)
of the above form. Lastly, assume that
m
is larger than the sum of the arities of all symbols in
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{MVoc})\cup\mathsf{W}(\mathsf{MVoc})
so that
Lemma

132
applies.

We assume that we have access to some linear order
E\subseteq A^{2}
on
\mathcal{A}
that we will existentially quantify over at the end of our construction. The linear order
E
induces a lexicographic order on
A^{m}
, and we think of
\overline{p}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{m})\in A^{m}
as the
n
-ary representation of
\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\times n^{m-i}\in\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}
, thereby identifying
A^{m}
with
\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}
.
Notably, by Lemma
131
, there are first-order formulas that define the minimal element in this order, as well as the successor operation, addition, and multiplication. We represent each of these operations by simply writing
\bar{t}=0
,
\overline{t}=\overline{u}+1
,
\bar{t}=\bar{u}+\bar{v}
, and
\bar{t}=\bar{u}\times\bar{v}
, but formally they should be interpreted as the corresponding statement in terms of the relations
E
,
S
, and
P
. Using the elements of
A^{m}
, we will encode the computation of
M
on input
(\mathcal{D},Y)
in the following way:

•

We introduce the relation
C\subseteq A^{2m}
that we will constrain with formulas expressing that
C(\overline{u},\overline{t})
is true in
(\mathcal{D},Y)
if and only if
M
on input
(\mathcal{D},Y)
executes instruction
\overline{u}
at time
\overline{t}
. We choose the letter
C
to stand for “command”.

•

We introduce the relations
I_{0},\dots,I_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}
, where
k_{\mathcal{P}}-1
is the maximum index register accessed by the program
\mathcal{P}
of
M
. For each
i
, we will constrain
I_{i}\subseteq A^{2m}
with formulas expressing that
I_{i}(\overline{u},\overline{t})
is true in
(\mathcal{D},Y)
if and only if the
i
th index register of
M
on input
(\mathcal{D},Y)
contains
\overline{u}
at time
\overline{t}
.

•

We introduce the weight function
Z\colon A^{2m}\to R
that we will constrain with formulas expressing that
Z(\overline{p},\overline{t})=r
is true in
(\mathcal{D},Y)
if and only if the
\overline{p}
th
\mathcal{R}
-register of
M
on input
(\mathcal{D},Y)
contains
r
at time
\overline{t}
.

Now our goal is to build first-order formulas that correctly constrain the relations
C,I_{0},\dots,I_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}
and the weight function
Z
. We begin by constructing a first-order formula
\textup{{start}}(E,S,P,Z)
that encodes the starting configuration of
M
on input
(D,Y)
. On this input
M
starts by executing instruction
0
, which we express with the formula
C(\overline{0},\overline{0}).

We need to constrain
I_{0}
to represent the fact that
M
starts with
|\textup{code}(D,Y)|
in its zeroth index register. Assuming without loss of generality that we have converted
(\mathcal{D},Y)
into a metafinite algebra
(A,\{w_{1},\dots,w_{h}\})
as described in
Definition

103
, the length of
\textup{code}((\mathcal{D},Y))
is
\sum_{i=1}^{h}n^{k_{i}}
. The
n
-ary representation of
n^{k_{i}}
is an element of
(p_{1},\dots,p_{m})\in A^{m}
such that all
p_{j}=1
when
m-j=k_{i}
, and
p_{j}=0
for all other
j
.
Since we can express
0
and
1
as the minimum element of
A
and its successor, we can express the
n
-ary representation of
n^{k_{i}}
in a first-order formula, which implies that we can express
\sum_{i=1}^{h}n^{k_{i}}
in a first-order formula. Note that we can express the above summation because we can express addition, Lemma
131
, and because
h
and each
k_{i}
are fixed by the vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
.

Thus, we constrain
I_{0}
with the formula
\exists\overline{u}\left(\overline{u}=\sum_{i=1}^{h}n^{k_{i}}\wedge I_{0}(\overline{0},\overline{u})\right)
, which expresses that
M
starts with
|\textup{code}((\mathcal{D},Y))|=\sum_{i=1}^{h}n^{k_{i}}
in its zeroth index register when it receives input
(\mathcal{D},Y)
. Furthermore, we constrain
I_{1},\dots,I_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}
with the formula
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}I_{i}(\overline{0},\overline{0})
expressing that
M
starts with
0
in all other index registers.

By Lemma
132
applied to vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}\cup\{Y\}
, there is a first-order formula
\texttt{input}(E,S,P,Z)
that is true in
(\mathcal{D},Y)
if and only if the function
\overline{p}\mapsto Z(\overline{0},\overline{p})
encodes the string
\textup{code}((\mathcal{D},Y))0\dots 0
, which constrains
Z
to correctly represent the fact that at time
\overline{0}
,
M
starts with the code of
(\mathcal{D},Y)
loaded into its
\mathcal{R}
-registers, followed by zeros. We define the first-order formula
\textup{{start}}(E,S,P,Z)
as the conjunction of all of the above formulas encoding the starting configuration of
M
on input
(\mathcal{D},Y).
That is,

\textup{{start}}(E,S,P,Z)\equiv C(\overline{0},\overline{0})\wedge\exists\overline{u}\left(\overline{u}=\sum_{i=1}^{h}n^{k_{i}}\wedge I_{0}(\overline{0},\overline{u})\right)\wedge\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}I_{i}(\overline{0},\overline{0})\wedge\textup{{input}}(E,S,P,Z).

Next, we encode the way
M
modifies its registers based on instruction
\ell
at time
t
with the family of formulas
\textup{{update}}_{\ell}
, where
\ell
is a parameter determining the specific form of this formula, and
\overline{u}
,
\overline{p}_{j}
, and
\overline{t}
are
m
-tuples of first-order variables ranging over
A^{m}
. We let
\ell
range over
\{0,\dots,\ell_{\mathcal{P}}\}
, where
\ell_{\mathcal{P}}
is the label of the last instruction in
\mathcal{P}
, and we base the definition of
\textup{{update}}_{\ell}
on the possible instructions in
\mathcal{P}
as well as their meanings, as detailed in
Table

3
and
Definition

74
.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\ell_{\mathcal{P}}
and all indices occurring in
\mathcal{P}
are smaller than
n^{m}
. Note that any fixed value
i
of
\{0,\dots,n^{m}-1\}
is first-order definable as the
i
th successor of the minimum element of
A^{m}
. In the following formulas, an equality such as
\overline{x}=i
should be interpreted as the formula stating that
\overline{x}
is the
i
th element of
A^{m}.

1.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=c
, then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}_{\ell}&\equiv&\forall\overline{u}\forall\overline{p}\forall\overline{t}\Big[\Big(\overline{u}=\ell\wedge\overline{p}=i\wedge C(\overline{u},\overline{t})\Big)\to Z(\overline{p},\overline{t}+1)=c\Big].\\
\end{array}

2.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{0}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=f(Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{k}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}})
, then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}_{\ell}&\equiv&\forall\overline{u}\forall\overline{p}_{0}\dots\forall\overline{p}_{k}\forall\overline{t}\Big[\Big(\overline{u}=\ell\wedge\bigwedge_{j=0}^{k}\overline{p}_{j}=i_{j}\wedge C(\overline{u},\overline{t})\Big)\to\\
&&\\
&&Z(\overline{p}_{0},\overline{t}+1)=f(Z(\overline{p}_{1},\overline{t}),\dots,Z(\overline{p}_{k},\overline{t}))\Big].\\
\end{array}

3.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:\mathsf{if}\ Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}=Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{2}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{1}\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{2},
then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}_{\ell}&\equiv&\forall\overline{u}_{0}\forall\overline{u}_{1}\forall\overline{u}_{2}\forall\overline{p}_{0}\dots\forall\overline{p}_{k}\forall\overline{t}\Big[\Big(\bigwedge_{j=0}^{2}\overline{u}=\ell\wedge\bigwedge_{j=0}^{k}\overline{p}_{j}=i_{j}\wedge C(\overline{u}_{0},\overline{t})\Big)\to\\
&&\\
&&\Big[\Big(Z(\overline{p}_{1},\overline{t})=Z(\overline{p}_{2},\overline{t})\rightarrow C(\overline{u}_{1},\overline{t}+1)\Big)\wedge\\
&&\\
&&\Big(\neg(Z(\overline{p}_{1},\overline{t})=Z(\overline{p}_{2},\overline{t}))\rightarrow C(\overline{u}_{2},\overline{t}+1)\Big)\Big]\Big].\end{array}

4.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:\mathsf{if}\ P(Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}},\dots,Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{k}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}})\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{1}\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{2},
then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}_{\ell}&\equiv&\forall\overline{u}_{0}\forall\overline{u}_{1}\forall\overline{u}_{2}\forall\overline{p}_{0}\dots\forall\overline{p}_{k}\forall\overline{t}\Big[\Big(\bigwedge_{j=0}^{2}\overline{u}=\ell\wedge\bigwedge_{j=0}^{k}\overline{p}_{j}=i_{j}\wedge C(\overline{u}_{0},\overline{t})\Big)\to\\
&&\\
&&\Big[\Big(P(Z(\overline{p}_{1},\overline{t})\dots,Z(\overline{p}_{k},\overline{t}))\rightarrow C(\overline{u}_{1},\overline{t}+1)\Big)\wedge\\
&&\\
&&\Big(\neg P(Z(\overline{p}_{1},\overline{t})\dots,Z(\overline{p}_{k},\overline{t}))\rightarrow C(\overline{u}_{2},\overline{t}+1)\Big)\Big]\Big].\end{array}

5.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{2}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
, then for all
t

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}_{\ell}&\equiv&\forall\overline{u}\forall\overline{t}\Big[\overline{u}=\ell\wedge C(\overline{u},\overline{t})\to\\
&&\\
&&\forall\overline{p}_{1}\forall\overline{p}_{2}\Big[\Big(I_{i_{1}}(\overline{p}_{1},\overline{t})\wedge I_{i_{2}}(\overline{p}_{2},\overline{t})\Big)\to Z(\overline{p}_{1},\overline{t}+1)=Z(\overline{p}_{2},\overline{t})\Big]\Big]\\
\end{array}

6.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:\mathsf{if}\ I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{1}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i_{2}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{1}\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{2}
, then for all
t

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}_{\ell}&\equiv&\forall\overline{u}_{0}\forall\overline{u}_{1}\forall\overline{u}_{2}\forall\overline{t}\Big[\Big(\overline{u}_{0}=\ell\wedge\overline{u}_{1}=\ell_{1}\wedge\overline{u}_{2}=\ell_{2}\wedge C(\overline{u}_{0},\overline{t})\Big)\to\\
&&\\
&&\forall\overline{p}_{1}\forall\overline{p}_{2}\Big[\Big(I_{i_{1}}(\overline{p}_{1},\overline{t})\wedge I_{i_{2}}(\overline{p}_{2},\overline{t})\wedge\overline{p}_{1}=\overline{p}_{2}\to C(\overline{u}_{1},\overline{t}+1)\Big)\wedge\\
&&\\
&&\hskip 28.45274pt\Big(I_{i_{1}}(\overline{p}_{1},\overline{t})\wedge I_{i_{2}}(\overline{p}_{2},\overline{t})\wedge\neg(\overline{p}_{1}=\overline{p}_{2})\to C(\overline{u}_{1},\overline{t}+1)\Big)\Big]\Big].\par\end{array}

7.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}+1
, then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}_{\ell}&\equiv&\forall\overline{u}\forall\overline{t}\Big[\Big(\overline{u}=\ell\wedge C(\overline{u},\overline{t})\to\\
&&\\
&&\forall\overline{p}\Big[I_{i}(\overline{p},\overline{t})\to I_{i}(\overline{p}+1,\overline{t}+1)\Big]\Big].\end{array}

8.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell:I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\hss\cr$-$\crcr}}}1
, then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}_{\ell}&\equiv&\forall\overline{u}\forall\overline{t}\Big[\Big(\overline{u}=\ell\wedge C(\overline{u},\overline{t})\to\\
&&\\
&&\forall\overline{p}\Big[I_{i}(\overline{p},\overline{t})\to I_{i}(\overline{p}\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\hss\cr$-$\crcr}}}1,\overline{t}+1)\Big]\Big].\end{array}

9.

If the
\ell
th instruction of
\mathcal{P}
is
\ell_{\mathcal{P}}:\mathsf{stop}
, then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{{update}}_{\ell}&\equiv&\forall\overline{u}\forall\overline{p}_{0}\dots\forall\overline{p}_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}\forall\overline{t}\Big[\Big(\overline{u}=\ell\wedge C(\overline{u},\overline{t})\wedge\bigwedge_{i=0}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}I_{i}(\overline{p}_{i},\overline{t})\Big)\to\\
&&\\
&&\Big(C(\overline{u},\overline{t}+1)\wedge Z(\overline{p},\overline{t})=Z(\overline{p},\overline{t})\wedge\bigwedge_{i=0}^{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}I_{i}(\overline{p}_{i},\overline{t}+1)\Big)\Big].\end{array}

In each case defining
\textup{{update}}_{\ell}
, we have additional clauses stating that every register that is not modified by the instruction remains unchanged at time
\overline{t}+1
, and that if instruction
\ell
is not a branching instruction, then
\ell+1
is the next instruction executed. In particular, the last case of the definition
\textup{{update}}_{\ell}
asserts that if
M
reaches the instruction
\ell_{\mathcal{P}}:\mathsf{stop}
at time
\overline{t}
, then the contents of the registers of
M
will not change at any time in between time
\overline{t}
and time
n^{m}-1
. We define the formula
update
as the conjunction

\textup{{update}}\equiv\textup{{update}}_{0}\wedge\dots\wedge\textup{{update}}_{\ell_{\mathcal{P}}}.

Lastly, we must encode the statement that
M
accepts the input
(\mathcal{D},Y)
. By definition,
M
accepts its input when it stops with
1
in its zeroth index register
I\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
, indicating that the length of the output is 1, and 1 in its zeroth
\mathcal{R}
register
Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}0\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}
, giving the output. We express this with the formula

\textup{{accept}}\equiv I_{0}(1,n^{m}-1)\wedge Z(1,n^{m}-1)=1.

The formula
\varphi_{M}\equiv\textup{{start}}\wedge\textup{{update}}\wedge\textup{{accept}}
is a first-order sentence in vocabulary

\mathsf{MVoc}\cup\{Y,C,I_{0},\dots,I_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1},E,S,P,Z\},

which means that

\Phi_{M}\equiv\exists Y\exists C\exists I_{0}\dots\exists I_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}\exists Z\exists E\exists S\exists P\ \varphi_{M}(W,C,I_{0},\dots,I_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1},E,S,P,Z)

is an existential second-order sentence in vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
. We now show that
\mathcal{D}\in L
if and only if
\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi_{M}
.

Suppose
\mathcal{D}\in L
. Then there is some
Y\colon A^{k}\to R
such that
M(\mathcal{D},Y)=1
. We may use the computation of
\mathsf{conf}_{0}(\mathcal{D},Y)\to\mathsf{conf}_{1}\to\dots\to\mathsf{conf}_{n^{m}-1}
of
M
on input
(\mathcal{D},Y)
to determine relations
C
and
I_{0},\dots,I_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}
. For example, we let
C\subseteq A^{2m}
be the relation that contains
(\overline{u},\overline{t})\in A^{2m}
such that
\overline{u}
is the
n
-ary representation of the instruction label
\ell
that
M
executes at time
\overline{t}
on input
(\mathcal{D},Y)
. We let
I_{0},\dots,I_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1}
be determined by the computation of
M
on input
(\mathcal{D},Y)
in a similar way.

When
(\mathcal{D},Y)
is fed to
M
as an input, we implicitly assume there is some chosen linear ordering
E\subseteq A^{2}
on the primary universe of
\mathcal{D}
. This is so that we can define
\textup{code}_{E}((\mathcal{D},Y))
as a string in
R^{*}
, as discussed in
Definition

103
. Let
E
be this chosen linear ordering, and let
S\subseteq A^{3m}
and
P\subseteq A^{3m}
be the graphs of addition and multiplication in
A^{m}
lexicographically ordered according to
E
. Finally, the computation of
M
on input
(\mathcal{D},Y)
determines a weight function
Z\colon A^{2m}\to R
by defining
Z(\overline{p},\overline{t})
to be the value
r\in R
that
M
holds in its
\overline{p}
th
\mathcal{R}
-register at time
\overline{t}
.

Since the subformulas of
\varphi_{M}
constraining these relations and weight functions say precisely that they encode an accepting computation of
M
on input
(\mathcal{D},Y)
, we can see that

(\mathcal{D},Y,C,I_{0},\dots,I_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1},E,S,P,Z)\vDash\varphi_{M}(W,C,I_{0},\dots,I_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1},E,S,P,Z),

and this implies that
\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi_{M}
.

Conversely, suppose
\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi_{M}
. Then there are relations and weight functions such that

(\mathcal{D},Y,C,I_{0},\dots,I_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1},E,S,P,Z)\vDash\varphi_{M}(W,C,I_{0},\dots,I_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1},E,S,P,Z).

In particular,

(\mathcal{D},Y,C,I_{0},\dots,I_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1},E,S,P,Z)\vDash(\textup{{start}}\wedge\textup{{update}})(W,C,I_{0},\dots,I_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1},E,S,P,Z),

so these relations and weight functions encode the computation of
M
on input
(\mathcal{D},Y)
. Since it is also the case that

(\mathcal{D},Y,C,I_{0},\dots,I_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1},E,S,P,Z)\vDash\textup{{accept}}(W,C,I_{0},\dots,I_{k_{\mathcal{P}}-1},E,S,P,Z),

it must be that
M
accepts input
(\mathcal{D},Y)
. Thus,
\mathcal{D}\in L
.
∎

Theorem

133
together with
Theorem

130
gives us the following corollary.

See
19

Example 134
.

Theorem

122
tells us that if
\mathcal{R}
is of finite type, then
\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
. We will construct a sentence
\Phi
of
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
such that
\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi
if and only if
\mathcal{D}
encodes a quantifier-free
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
-formula
\psi
such that
\mathcal{R}\vDash\exists\overline{x}\psi(\overline{x})
. Note that since
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})=(\mathsf{Con},\mathsf{Fun},\mathsf{Rel})
is of finite type, there are finitely many function and relation symbols in this vocabulary. The metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
that we use has a primary vocabulary that consists of the relation symbols

\{\text{Var},\text{Con},\text{Term},\text{Subform},\text{Form}\}\cup\{F_{f}:f\in\mathsf{Fun}\}\cup\{Q_{P}:P\in\mathsf{Rel}\}\cup\{\text{Equal}\}\cup\{\text{Not},\text{And},\text{Or}\}.

These relation symbols will specify the syntactic structure of
\psi
. The metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
also has a weight function symbol
C
that will specify the constants occurring in
\psi
. The
\mathcal{R}
-structure
\mathcal{D}_{\psi}=(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R},\mathbf{F})
encoding
\psi
will have primary universe
A=\{1,\dots,n\}
, which has one element for each term and subformula occurring in
\psi
. Essentially,
\mathcal{D}_{\psi}
will encode the syntax tree of
\psi
in the following way:

1.

\text{Var}(i)
indicates that
i
represents a variable occurring in
\psi
,

2.

\text{Con}(i)
indicates that
i
represents a constant occurring in
\psi
,

3.

\text{Term}(i)
indicates that
i
represents a term occurring in
\psi
,

4.

\text{Subform}(i)
indicates that
i
represents a subformula of
\psi
,

5.

\text{Form}(i)
indicates that
i
represents the formula
\psi
,

6.

F_{f}(i,j)
indicates that term
j
results from applying
f
to term
i
,

7.

Q_{P}(i_{1},\dots,i_{k},j)
indicates that subformula
j
results from applying relation symbol
P
to terms
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}
,

8.

\text{Equal}(i_{1},i_{2},j)
indicates that subformula
j
results from applying
=
to terms
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
,

9.

\text{Not}(i,j)
indicates that subformula
j
results from applying
\neg
to subformula
i
,

10.

\text{And}(i_{1},i_{2},j)
indicates that subformula
j
results from applying
\wedge
to subformulas
i_{1}
and
i_{2},

11.

\text{Or}(i_{1},i_{2},j)
indicates that subformula
j
results from applying
\vee
to subformulas
i_{1}
and
i_{2}.

For example, if
\psi
is the formula
(f(g(x))=c)\wedge P(y,y)
, then we may represent
\psi
with the following enumerated syntax tree:

8:(f(g(x))=c)\wedge P(y,y)
7:P(y,y)
2:y
6:f(g(x))=c
3:c
5:f(g(x))
4:g(x)
1:x

We then encode this syntax tree into an
\mathcal{R}
-structure
\mathcal{D}_{\psi}
by specifying the following information:

1.

A=\{1,\dots,8\}

2.

\text{Var}=\{1,2\}

3.

\text{Con}=\{3\}

4.

\text{Term}=\{1,2,3,4,5\}

5.

\text{Subform}=\{6,7,8\}

6.

\text{Form}=\{8\}

7.

F_{g}=\{(1,4)\}

8.

F_{f}=\{(4,5)\}

9.

Q_{P}=\{(2,2,7)\}

10.

\text{Equal}=\{(5,3,6),(6,7,8)\}
,

11.

C\colon A\to R
is the weight function such that
C(i)=\begin{cases}c&\text{ if }i=3\\
0&\text{ if }i\neq 3.\end{cases}

The
\mathsf{MVoc}
-sentence
\Phi
we construct will consist of two parts: first, a subformula
valid
asserting that an
\mathcal{R}
-structure
\mathcal{D}
encodes a valid
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
-formula, and second, a subformula
\textup{{satisfied}}(X)
asserting that
X\colon A\to R
specifies
\overline{r}
such that
\mathcal{R}\vDash\psi(\overline{r})
. Let
M
be the maximum arity of a symbol in
\mathsf{Fun}\cup\mathsf{Rel}
of
\mathsf{Voc}(\mathcal{R})
. Then
valid
is the formula whose prefix is
\forall i_{1}\dots\forall i_{M}\forall i\forall j\exists t
and whose matrix is the conjunction of the following formulas:

1.

(\text{Var}(i)\vee\text{Con}(i))\to\text{Term}(i)

2.

\text{Term}(i)\leftrightarrow\neg\text{Subform}(i)

3.

\text{Form}(i)\to\text{Subform}(i)

4.

\text{Form}(t)

5.

(\text{Form}(i_{1})\wedge\text{Form}(i_{2}))\to i_{1}=i_{2}

6.

\bigwedge_{f\in\mathsf{Fun}}F_{f}(i_{1},\dots,i_{k_{f}},j)\to(\bigwedge_{\ell=1}^{k_{f}}\text{Term}(i_{\ell})\wedge\text{Term}(j))

7.

\bigwedge_{P\in\mathsf{Rel}}Q_{P}(i_{1},\dots,i_{k_{P}},j)\to(\bigwedge_{\ell=1}^{k_{P}}\text{Term}(i_{\ell})\wedge\text{Subform}(j))

8.

\text{Equal}(i_{1},i_{2},j)\to(\text{Term}(i_{1})\wedge\text{Term}(i_{2})\wedge\text{Subform}(j))

9.

\text{Not}(i,j)\to(\text{Subform}(i)\wedge\text{Subform}(j))

10.

\text{And}(i_{1},i_{2},j)\to(\text{Subform}(i_{1})\wedge\text{Subform}(i_{2})\wedge\text{Subform}(j))

11.

\text{Or}(i_{1},i_{2},j)\to(\text{Subform}(i_{1})\wedge\text{Subform}(i_{2})\wedge\text{Subform}(j))

The subformula
\textup{{satisfied}}(X)
has a free second-order variable
X
ranging over weight functions
X\colon A\to R
that specifies values
\overline{r}
to assign to the variables
\overline{x}
of
\psi
, as well as the value
t^{\mathcal{R}}(\overline{r})\in R
of each term
t
that occurs in
\psi
and the truth value
\psi^{\prime}(\overline{r})\in\{0,1\}
of each subformula
\psi^{\prime}
occurring in
\psi
. The subformula
\textup{{satisfied}}(X)
asserts that
X
correctly computes the value of terms and the truth value of subformulas occurring in
\psi
by using the prefix
\forall i_{1}\dots\forall i_{M}\forall i\forall j
and the matrix resulting from the conjunction of the following formulas:

1.

\text{Con}(i)\to X(i)=C(i)

2.

\bigwedge_{f\in\mathsf{Fun}}F_{f}(i_{1},\dots,i_{k_{f}},j)\to X(j)=f(X(i_{1}),\dots,X(i_{k_{f}}))

3.

\text{Subform}(i)\to(X(i)=0\vee X(i)=1)

4.

\bigwedge_{P\in\mathsf{Rel}}Q_{P}(i_{1},\dots,i_{k_{P}},j)\to(X(j)=1\leftrightarrow P(X(i_{1}),\dots,X(i_{k_{P}})))

5.

\text{Equal}(i_{1},i_{2},j)\to(X(j)=1\leftrightarrow X(i_{1})=X(i_{2}))

6.

\text{Not}(i,j)\to(X(j)=1\leftrightarrow X(i)=0)

7.

\text{And}(i_{1},i_{2},j)\to(X(j)=1\leftrightarrow X(i_{1})=1\wedge X(i_{2})=1)

8.

\text{Or}(i_{1},i_{2},j)\to(X(j)=1\leftrightarrow X(i_{1})=1\vee X(i_{2})=1)

The sentence of
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
expressing that
\mathcal{D}
encodes some
\psi(\overline{x})\in\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})
is then

\Phi\equiv\exists X\ \textup{{valid}}\wedge\textup{{satisfied}}(X).

C.3
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
Captures
\exists\mathcal{R}

In this subsection, we prove
Corollary

20
, which says that
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\exists\mathcal{R}
over Boolean
\mathcal{R}
-structures. According to
Definition

117
, showing this requires showing two things for any metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
over
\mathcal{R}
: (1) If
\Phi
is an
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathsf{Voc})
sentence, then
\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{BStruct}(\mathsf{Voc}):\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi\}\in\exists\mathcal{R}
, and (2) If
L
is a decision problem of Boolean
\mathcal{R}
-structures in vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
such that
L\in\exists\mathcal{R}
, then there is some
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathsf{Voc})
sentence
\Phi
such that
L=\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{BStruct}(\mathsf{Voc}):\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi\}.
The proofs of these statements are simple modifications of the corresponding statements for
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
and
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
.

Lemma 135
.

For any metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
over
\mathcal{R}
, if
\varphi
is a sentence of first-order metafinite logic in this vocabulary such that the only constants from
\mathcal{R}
that occur in
\varphi
are 0 and 1, then
\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{BStruct}(\mathsf{Voc}):\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi\}\in P(\mathcal{R})^{0}.

Proof.

Assume without loss of generality that
\varphi
is in prenex normal form, and recall that
\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{0}
is the class of decision problems that have a decision algorithm implemented on a polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
machine with no machine constants other than 0 and 1.
From
Lemma

129
, we know that we can decide if
\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi
in polynomial time with an
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
. In order for
M
to execute the algorithm described in
Lemma

129
, only the constants appearing in
\varphi
need to be machine constants of
M
. Since only
0
and
1
occur in
\varphi
, we can see that
M
is constant free.
∎

Theorem 136
.

For any metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
, if
\Phi
is a sentence of
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathsf{MVoc})
, then

\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{BStruct}(\mathsf{Voc}):\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi\}\in\exists\mathcal{R}.

Proof.

Since we restrict
L=\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{BStruct}(\mathsf{MVoc}):\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi\}
to Boolean
\mathcal{R}
-structures, if we can show that
L\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0},
we then will have shown that
L\in\textup{BP}(\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0})=\exists\mathcal{R}
. Any
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathsf{MVoc})
sentence
\Phi
is of the form
\exists X_{1}\dots\exists X_{h}\varphi(X_{1},\dots,X_{h})
, where
X_{1},\dots,X_{h}
are second-order variables and
\varphi(W_{1},\dots,W_{h})
is a first-order formula in vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}\cup\{X_{1},\dots,X_{h}\}
, expanded by regarding the variable
X_{i}
as a weight function symbol. Given any interpretation of each
X_{i}
as a Boolean weight function
W_{i}\colon A^{k}\to\{0,1\}\subseteq R
,
Lemma

135
tells us that

\{(\mathcal{D},W_{1},\dots,W_{h}):(\mathcal{D},W_{1},\dots,W_{h})\vDash\varphi(W_{1},\dots,W_{h})\}\in P(\mathcal{R})^{0}.

Thus,
L=\{\mathcal{D}:\exists W_{1}\dots\exists W_{h}(\mathcal{D},W_{1},\dots,W_{h})\vDash\varphi(W_{1},\dots,W_{h})\}\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}.
This argument is essentially the same as the argument occurring in the proof of
Theorem

130
, except using
Lemma

135
instead of
Lemma

129
.
∎

Theorem 137
.

For every metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
over
\mathcal{R}
, if
L
is a decision problem of Boolean
\mathcal{R}
-structures such that
L\in\exists\mathcal{R}
, then there is a sentence
\Phi
of
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathsf{Voc})
such that

L=\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{BStruct}(\mathsf{Voc}):\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi\}.

Proof.

Since
L\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
, there is some positive integer
k
and some polynomial-time
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
, with no machine constants other than 0 and 1, such that

L=\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{BStruct}(\mathsf{Voc}):(\exists Y\colon A^{k}\to R)\ M(\mathcal{D},Y)=1\}.

With the same method as in the proof of
Theorem

133
, we can construct a
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathsf{MVoc})
sentence
\Phi
such that there is some
Y\colon A^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
for which
M(D,Y)=1
if and only if
D\vDash\exists Y\Phi_{M}(Y)
. It remains to show that our method of construction results in
\Phi_{M}
having no constants from
\mathcal{R}
other than 0 and 1 when the same is true of
M
. The only place where constants from
\mathcal{R}
are potentially introduced into
\Phi_{M}
is in case 1 of the definition of
\textup{{update}}_{\ell}
, for instructions in the program
\mathcal{P}
of
M
of the form
\ell:Z\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\lfloor$\cr$\lceil$\crcr}}i\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\rfloor$\cr$\rceil$\crcr}}:=c
. Since
M
uses only the constants 0 and 1, we know that any
c
occurring in an
\textup{{update}}_{\ell}
of this form is either 0 or 1. Thus,
\exists Y\Phi_{M}(Y)
is an
\exists\textup{SO}(\mathsf{Voc})
formula.
∎

Theorems
136
and
137
together give us the following corollary.

See
20

C.4
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
Captures
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
and
\exists_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
Captures
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}

In this subsection, we prove
Theorem

21
and
Corollary

22
. A common way to prove that the
k
th existential fragment of second-order logic
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SO}
captures the
k
th existential level of the polynomial hierarchy
\Sigma_{k}
is to use the oracle characterization of
\Sigma_{k}
and proceed by induction. This proof method generalizes to establish
Theorem

21
that
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
, but we cannot modify it to get a proof of
Corollary

22
that
\exists_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}
.

To get an intuition for what goes wrong, suppose that we have already proven that
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
and we now want to show that
\Sigma_{k+1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\Sigma_{k+1}\mathcal{R}=\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}.
For any
L\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}
, there is a polynomial-time verification
\mathcal{R}
-machine
M
with access to some oracle
S\in\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
such that
M
verifies
L
. By our inductive hypothesis, there is a sentence
\Psi
of
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
such that
\mathcal{D}\in S
if and only if
\mathcal{D}\vDash\Psi
. With a suitable modification to
\Phi_{M}
as constructed in the proof of
Theorem

133
, we can express any oracle calls that
M
makes to
S
with the sentence
\Psi
placed inside of
\Phi_{M}
, and bringing the second-order quantifiers to the front of
\Phi_{M}
results in one more quantifier alternation. Thus,
\Sigma_{k+1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\Sigma_{k+1}\mathcal{R}
, completing our inductive argument.

However, if we try to adapt this proof to the Boolean analogue, the sentence
\Psi
causes a problem. Even though restricting
M
to have no constants other than 0 and 1 means that the same is true of
\Phi_{M}
, in general
\Psi
may have constants other than 0 and 1 because it describes a machine
N
that may have constants other than 0 or 1, which follows from the definition of
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
. If we could replace
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
with
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}
, then our problem would be solved, but it is unlikely that
\exists\mathcal{R}^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}=\exists\mathcal{R}^{\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}}
in general. Since this simpler proof technique does not work, we will directly adapt the proof that
\Sigma_{1}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
to show that
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
captures
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
.

Towards this end, fix a metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
over
\mathcal{R}
, and let
M
be a polynomial time
\mathcal{R}
-machine. Construct the formulas
update
and
accept
as in the proof of
Theorem

133
, but modify the formula
start
constructed therein to become a family of formulas
\textup{{start}}_{j,k}
, which results from building the subformula
input
for the vocabulary

\mathsf{Voc}\cup\{X_{1},\dots,X_{j},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k}\}

via the same method used in
Lemma

132
. (Previously we had built
input
specifically for the vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}\cup\{Y\}
.)
Define the family of formulas
\varphi_{M,k}
and
\widetilde{\varphi}_{M,k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
as follows.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\varphi_{M,k}&\equiv&\ \textup{{start}}_{M,k}\wedge\textup{{update}}\wedge\textup{{accept}}\\
\widetilde{\varphi}_{M,k}&\equiv&(\textup{{start}}_{M,k}\wedge\textup{{update}})\to\textup{{accept}}.\end{array}

The heart of the idea is captured in the following lemma.

Lemma 138

(Encoding Computations in Second-Order Logic)
.

For all
\mathcal{R}
-structures
\mathcal{D}
in vocabulary
\mathsf{Voc}
and for all interpretations of
Y_{i}
as weight functions
Y_{i}\colon A^{q}\to R
, the construction of these formulas implies that the following are equivalent:

1.

M(\mathcal{D},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k})=1

2.

\mathcal{D}\vDash\varphi_{M,k}(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k})

3.

\mathcal{D}\vDash\widetilde{\varphi}_{M,k}(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k}).

Proof.

The proof follows essentially the same argument as that given in the proof of Fagin’s theorem over
\mathcal{R}
, shown in
Theorem

19
.
∎

Note that the above lemma implies
Lemma

18
from the main body of this paper. Given this, it is intuitively clear that we can stick the appropriate alternating sequence of quantifiers in front of these statements to get the desired result. Indeed, this is justified by the replacement theorem
[
31
]
, which says we may use logically equivalent statements interchangeably. We just need the following theorem before we arrive at our main result.

Theorem 139
.

For any metafinite vocabulary
\mathsf{MVoc}
over
\mathcal{R}
, and for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
if
\Phi
is a sentence of
\Sigma_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathsf{MVoc})
, then
\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{Struct}(\mathsf{MVoc}):\mathcal{D}\vDash\Phi\}\in\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
, and if
\widetilde{\Phi}
is a sentence of
\Pi_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
, then
\{\mathcal{D}\in\textup{Struct}(\mathsf{MVoc}):\mathcal{D}\vDash\widetilde{\Phi}\}\in\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
.

Proof.

If
\Phi\in\Sigma_{k}\textup{SO}(\mathcal{R})
, then
\Phi
is of the form
(\exists\overline{Y}_{1})(\forall\overline{Y}_{2})\dots(Q\overline{Y}_{k})\varphi
, and it is intuitively clear that
\{\mathcal{D}:\exists\overline{Y}_{1}\forall\overline{Y}_{2}\dots Q\overline{Y}_{k}(\mathcal{D},\overline{Y}_{1},\dots,\overline{Y}_{k})\vDash\varphi\}\in\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
because we can test if structures satisfy first-order formulas by
Lemma

129
.
∎

As a corollary of
Lemma

138
and
Theorem

139
, we have the desired theorem.

See
21

The same sort of analysis as in
Theorem

136
and
Theorem

137
can be generalized to give us the following corollary.

See
22

Appendix D
Oracles and Hierarchies

D.1
Oracles for
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}

We begin with some technical lemmas that simplify the main result.

Lemma 140
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}\subseteq\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}.

Proof.

Suppose
L\in\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
. Then
\overline{L}\in\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
. Let
M
be the machine with a
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
oracle that on input
v
returns 0 if
v\in\overline{L}
and 1 if
v\not\in\overline{L}.
Since
M
runs in polynomial time and decides
L
, we can see that
L\in\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}.

∎

Lemma 141
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k+1}\mathcal{R}}\subseteq\Sigma\Pi\left(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}\right).

Proof.

Suppose
L\in P(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k+1}\mathcal{R}}
, let
M
be a polynomial time
\mathcal{R}
-machine with a
Q
oracle, for some
Q\in\Sigma_{k+1}\mathcal{R}
, such that
M
decides
L
. Take some arbitrary element
v
of
R^{*}
. On input
v
,
M
will make finitely many queries
u_{1},\dots,u_{p}
by executing oracle query instructions, and each oracle query instruction is of the form

\mathsf{if}\ u_{i}\in Q\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{(i,1)}\ \mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{goto}\ \ell_{(i,0)},

where
\ell_{(i,1)}
and
\ell_{(i,0)}
are numbers labeling instructions in the program of
M
. We can encode the answer that the oracle returns after query
u_{i}
in a single bit
b_{i}
such that

b_{i}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }u_{i}\in Q\\
0&\text{ if }u_{i}\not\in Q.\end{cases}

If we were given the number
p
of queries that
M
will make on input
v
, as well as the answers
b_{1},\dots,b_{p}
to those queries, then we could simulate
M
on input
v
with an
\mathcal{R}
-machine that does not have a
Q
oracle in the following way: Simulate
M
until it makes its first oracle query. If
b_{1}=1
, then continue to simulate
M
starting from instruction
\ell_{(1,1)}
. If
b_{1}=0
, then continue to simulate
M
starting from instructions
\ell_{(1,0)}
. Continue to simulate
M
in this manner, using bit
b_{i}
to decide which way to branch upon query
i
, until
M
halts. This allows us to rephrase membership in
L
in terms of this answer sequence as follows:

{\begin{array}[]{rcl}v\in L&\iff&\exists(p,(b_{1},\dots,b_{p}))\\
&&[\text{On input $v$, if $M$ branches according to answers $b_{1},\dots,b_{p},$}\\
&&\text{then $M$ will make queries $u_{1},\dots,u_{p}$ before accepting $v$}]\wedge\\
&&[\bigwedge_{i=1}^{p}(b_{i}=1\implies u_{i}\in Q]\wedge\\
&&[\bigwedge_{i=1}^{p}(b_{i}=0\implies u_{i}\not\in Q].\end{array}}

Of course, to know that our simulation of
M
on
v
returns the correct result, we must know that the answers
b_{1},\dots,b_{p}
are correct, which is what the last two clauses of the above condition guarantee. It is unlikely that any
\mathcal{R}
-machine is able to determine whether or not
u_{i}\in Q
in polynomial time without using an oracle. However, because
Q\in\Sigma_{k+1}\mathcal{R}=\Sigma\left(\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}\right),
there is a simpler oracle
S\in\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
that we can use to make this determination with the help of witness strings. In particular,
S
can tell us about
u_{i}
via the following equivalences:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}u_{i}\in Q&\textup{ if and only if }&(\exists w_{i}\in R^{n_{i}})\,(u_{i},w_{i})\in S,\\
&&\\
u_{i}\not\in Q&\textup{ if and only if }&(\forall w^{\prime}_{i}\in R^{n_{i}})\,(u_{i},w^{\prime}_{i})\not\in S.\end{array}

The implication
b_{i}=1\implies u_{i}\in Q
partially guaranteeing the correctness of answer
b_{i}
is then equivalent to
(\exists w_{i}\in R^{n_{i}})\,[b_{i}=1\implies(u_{i},w_{i})\in S].
Note that pulling the existential quantifier to the front of the implication, as we have done, is logically equivalent to leaving it in front of the statement
(u_{i},w_{i})\in S
. Thus, we can rephrase the condition determining membership in
L
as follows:

{\begin{array}[]{rcl}v\in L&\iff&\exists(p,(b_{1},\dots,b_{p}),(w_{1},\dots,w_{p}))\forall(w^{\prime}_{1},\dots,w^{\prime}_{p})\\
&&[\text{On input $v$, if $M$ branches according to answers $b_{1},\dots,b_{p},$}\\
&&\text{then $M$ will make queries $u_{1},\dots,u_{p}$ before accepting $v$}]\wedge\\
&&[\bigwedge_{i=1}^{p}(b_{i}=1\implies(u_{i},w_{i})\not\in\overline{S}]\wedge\\
&&[\bigwedge_{i=1}^{p}(b_{i}=0\implies(u_{i},w^{\prime}_{i})\in\overline{S}].\end{array}}

Since
M
runs in polynomial time, the number
p
of queries that
M
makes on input
v
will be polynomial in
|v|
. Similarly, the length of the code of each query
u_{i}
will be polynomial in
|v|
, as will the length of any potential witness
w_{i}
for
u_{i}
. Taken together, this means that the length of the encoding of
(p,(b_{1},\dots,b_{p}),(w_{1},\dots,w_{p}))
will be polynomial in
|v|
, as will the length of the encoding of
(w^{\prime}_{1},\dots,w^{\prime}_{p})
. Thus, to show that
L\in\Sigma\Pi\left(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}(\mathcal{R})}\right),
it suffices to show that the quantifier-free portion of the above condition can be checked in polynomial time by an
\mathcal{R}
-machine
N
with an
S
oracle.

The machine
N
rejects any input that is not of the form

(v,(p,(b_{1},\dots,b_{p}),(w_{1},\dots,w_{p})),(w^{\prime}_{1},\dots,w^{\prime}_{p})).

Given an input of the above form,
N
will simulate
M
on input
v
until
M
makes its first query
u_{1}
. If
b_{1}=1
, then
N
uses its oracle to check if
(u_{1},w_{1})\in S
. If this is not the case, then
N
rejects its input. If this is the case, then
N
branches according to
b_{1}
by proceeding to simulate
M
staring from instruction
\ell_{(1,1)}
. If
b_{1}=0
, then
N
proceeds similarly, except it uses its oracle to check if
(u_{1},w^{\prime}_{1})\in S
before rejecting or continuing to simulate
M
from instruction
\ell_{(1,0)}
.

The machine
N
continues to simulate
M
in this manner, checking at the
i
th query if answer
b_{i}
is correct, relative to the witness strings
w_{i}
and
w^{\prime}_{i}
, before rejecting or branching according to
b_{i}
. If
M
accepts
v
, then
N
accepts
(v,(p,(b_{1},\dots,b_{p}),(w_{1},\dots,w_{p})),(w^{\prime}_{1},\dots,w^{\prime}_{p}))
, and otherwise
N
rejects this input. Everything that
N
does in addition to simulating
M
can be done in polynomial time in
|v|
. Since
M
runs in polynomial time in
|v|
, simulating
M
by branching according to answers
b_{1},\dots,b_{p}
can also be done in polynomial time. Thus,
N
runs in polynomial time.
∎

See
23

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
k
. Recall that because we define
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
and
\textup{coNP}(\mathcal{R})
in terms of witnesses rather than nondeterministic machines, we have

\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}=\Sigma\left(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}\right)\text{ and }\textup{coNP}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}=\Pi\left(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}\right).

By definition we have the equality
\Sigma_{0}\mathcal{R}=\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})
. Thus,
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{0}\mathcal{R}}=\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})}=\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
and similarly
\textup{coNP}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{0}\mathcal{R}}=\textup{coNP}(\mathcal{R}),
thereby establishing our base case.

Assume that
