\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}=\Sigma\left(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k-1}\mathcal{R}}\right)
and
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}=\Pi\left(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k-1}\mathcal{R}}\right)
. Suppose
L\in\Sigma_{k+1}\mathcal{R}
. Since
\Sigma_{k+1}\mathcal{R}=\Sigma\left(\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}\right)
, we know there is some polynomial
q
and some
S\in\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
such that for all
v\in R^{*}
,

v\in L\text{ if and only if }\left(\exists w\in R^{q(|v|)}\right)(v,w)\in S.

Since
S\in\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
, the inclusion
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}\subseteq\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}
established in Lemma
140
implies that
S\in\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}
. Thus, using the same polynomial
q
to bound the length of potential witnesses, we have
L\in\Sigma\left(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}\right)
, thereby establishing the inclusion
\Sigma_{k+1}\mathcal{R}\subseteq\Sigma\left(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}\right).

Conversely, suppose
L\in\Sigma\left(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}\right)
. Then there is some polynomial
q
and some
S\in\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}
such that for all
v\in R^{*}
,

v\in L\text{ if and only if }\left(\exists w\in R^{q(|v|)}\right)(v,w)\in S.

Since
S\in\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}
, the inclusion
\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}}\subseteq\Sigma\Pi\left(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k-1}\mathcal{R}}\right)
established in Lemma
141
implies that
S\in\Sigma\Pi\left(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k-1}\mathcal{R}}\right).
By our inductive hypothesis, we know
\Pi\left(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k-1}\mathcal{R}}\right)=\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}
, so
S\in\Sigma\left(\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}\right)
, and this implies that
L\in\Sigma\Sigma\left(\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}\right)
. It is clear that
\Sigma\Sigma\left(\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}\right)=\Sigma\left(\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}\right)
, so we can see that
L\in\Sigma\left(\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}\right)=\Sigma_{k+1}\mathcal{R}
, thereby establishing the inclusion
\Sigma\left(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{\Sigma_{k}\textup{SAT}(\mathcal{R})}\right)\subseteq\Sigma_{k+1}\mathcal{R}
.
∎

D.2
Oracles for
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}

Recall that we define
\exists\mathcal{R}=\textup{BP}^{0}(\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R}))
. That is,
\exists\mathcal{R}
is the class of decision problems
L\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*}
for which there is a polynomial-time verification algorithm, implemented on a constant-free
\mathcal{R}
-machine that only accepts Boolean inputs, using witness strings
w\in R^{*}
. The meaning of
\exists\mathcal{R}^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}^{0}}
is then the class of decision problems
L\subseteq\{0,1\}
where the above mentioned
\mathcal{R}
-machine has an oracle for some
Q\in\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}^{0}
.

We can alter the above lemmas and theorem to derive
Corollary

24
below. Specifically, we modify
Lemma

140
to show that
\Pi_{k}\mathcal{R}^{0}\subseteq(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{0})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}^{0}}
, and we alter
Lemma

141
to show that
(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{0})^{\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}^{0}}\subseteq\Sigma\Pi((\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{0})^{\Sigma_{k-1}\mathcal{R}^{0}}).
Using these altered lemmas, we may then use essential the same argument as in the proof of
Theorem

23
to derive the following corollary.

See
24

This corollary tells us that it is unlikely that we can replace the non-Boolean oracle class
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}^{0}
with the Boolean oracle class
\exists_{k}\mathcal{R}
, as one might expect from a straightforward analogy with the classical polynomial hierarchy. For example, while
\textup{NP}^{\textup{NP}}=\Sigma_{2}
and
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})}=\Sigma_{2}\mathcal{R}
,
Corollary

24
tells us that
\exists\mathcal{R}^{\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}}=\exists_{2}\mathcal{R}
, so it is unlikely that
\exists\mathcal{R}^{\exists\mathcal{R}}=\exists_{2}\mathcal{R}
. It is instructive to reflect on why our proof requires the constant-free, non-Boolean oracle class
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}^{0}
.

Already for
k=0
, in order to show that
\exists\mathcal{R}^{\Sigma_{0}\mathcal{R}^{0}}=\exists\mathcal{R}
, we need to use a constant-free machine to simulate a constant-free machine with an oracle
Q\in\Sigma_{0}\mathcal{R}^{0}=\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})^{0}
. This is possible only because
Q
is itself decidable with a constant-free machine. Otherwise, we could hide a constant in the oracle
Q
, making this simulation impossible. We can see this from the observation that
\textup{BP}(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R}))\subseteq\exists\mathcal{R}^{\textup{P}(\mathcal{R})}
, and in general there are problems in
\textup{BP}(\textup{P}(\mathcal{R}))
that cannot be decided with a constant-free machine. For more detail on hiding constants, see the warning below
Definition

80
and
[
11
]
. Thus, we need to take the constant-free part of
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}
as an oracle class.

We then want to show for
k=1
that
\exists\mathcal{R}^{\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}}=\exists_{2}\mathcal{R}
. To show the inclusion
\exists\mathcal{R}^{\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}}\subseteq\exists_{2}\mathcal{R}
, it is again important that we use the constant-free part of
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})
so that we are not able to hide constants in oracles. To show the inclusion
\exists_{2}\mathcal{R}\subseteq\exists\mathcal{R}^{\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}}
, it is important that we use a non-Boolean oracle class because witness strings can be non-Boolean.

In more detail, suppose
L\in\exists_{2}\mathcal{R}
. Then
L\subseteq\{0,1\}^{*}
and there is a constant-free, polynomial-time machine
M
such that for all
v\in\{0,1\}^{*}
,

v\in L\text{ if and only if }(\exists w_{1}\in R^{*})(\forall w_{2}\in R^{*})M(v,w_{1},w_{2})=1.

Looking only at the universally quantified witness strings
w_{2}\in R^{*}
, we can see that
Q=\{u\in R^{*}:(\forall w_{2}\in R^{*})M(u,w_{2})=1\}\in\textup{coNP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}
. Furthermore,
v\in L
if and only if there is some
w_{1}\in R^{*}
such that
(u,w_{1})\in Q
. Because a constant-free machine with an oracle for
\overline{Q}=R^{*}\setminus Q\in\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}
can decide
Q
in polynomial time, we see that
L\in\exists\mathcal{R}^{\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}}
. In general, the oracle
\overline{Q}
will be non-Boolean because the witness strings
w_{1},w_{2}\in R^{*}
will be non-Boolean. Thus, we cannot generally take the Boolean part of
\textup{NP}(\mathcal{R})^{0}
, nor of
\Sigma_{k}\mathcal{R}^{0}
, as our oracle class.
